<summary>This is the title page for Thomas Goodwin's influential Puritan treatise, "A Child of Light Walking in Darkness," which explores the theological and practical reasons why a believer might experience spiritual desertion or a lack of divine comfort.</summary>

## By Thomas Goodwin, B.D. <note>B.D. stands for Bachelor of Divinity, a postgraduate academic degree in theology.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for Thomas Goodwin's influential Puritan work, which explores the spiritual and psychological causes behind why a believer might experience a "dark night of the soul" or a loss of the sense of God's presence.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Goodwin, Child of Light, Walking in Darkness, spiritual desertion, distress of conscience, Isaiah 50, Job 34, Puritan theology, 1643</keywords>

<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the work, written originally in Latin. It introduces the patron, Robert Greville, Lord Brooke, and the author, Thomas Goodwin.</meta>

### TO THE MOST HONORABLE LORD,
# ROBERT,
## LORD BROOKE,
### BARON BROOKE, OF
### BEAUCHAMP COURT,

->*A NOBLE LEADER* <note>original: "HEROI"</note><-

->OF EXCEPTIONAL INSIGHT, OF THE HIGHEST<-
->INTEGRITY <note>original: "candoris"</note>, PIETY, AND<-
->LEARNING <note>original: "literarum"</note>, A CULTIVATOR AND PATRON,<-
->THIS SMALL WORK <note>original: "opellam hanc"</note>,<-
->FOR THE CONSOLATION OF A<-
->LABORING CONSCIENCE,<-
->IN<-
->PERPETUAL RESPECT<-
->*AS A TESTIMONY:*<-

->I GIVE, DEDICATE, AND CONSECRATE.<-

## THOMAS GOODWIN.

<summary>This is a formal Latin dedication from the Puritan theologian Thomas Goodwin to Robert Greville, 2nd Baron Brooke, a noted parliamentarian and patron of learning. The text presents the book as a spiritual offering intended to provide comfort to those struggling with their conscience.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Goodwin, Robert Greville, Lord Brooke, dedication, patronage, conscience, piety, Beauchamp Court</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page consists of a formal Latin dedication from the Puritan theologian Thomas Goodwin to his patron, Robert Greville, 2nd Baron Brooke (1607–1643). Lord Brooke was a significant figure in the Parliamentary cause and a supporter of non-conformist religious thought. The dedication establishes the book's purpose: to offer spiritual comfort to those struggling with their faith.</meta>

### TO THE MOST HONORABLE

# ROBERT,

## LORD BROOKE,

### BARON BROOKE OF
### BEAUCHAMP COURT,

### A NOBLE HERO <note>original: "HEROI"</note>,

### OF EXCEPTIONAL CHARACTER, OF THE HIGHEST
### AND SINCERE PIETY, AND THE MOST
### DEVOTED IN FAITHFUL SERVICE
### TOWARD HIS COUNTRY,
### A COMFORTER OF THE
### STRUGGLING CONSCIENCE <note>original: "LABORANTIS CONSCIENTIÆ CONSOLATORI"</note>,
### IN
### TESTIMONY OF
### PERPETUAL RESPECT,

## I GIVE, DEDICATE, AND CONSECRATE. <note>original: "DO: DICO: CONSECRO."</note>

## THOMAS GOODWIN.

<summary>This page is a formal dedication by Thomas Goodwin to Robert Greville, Lord Brooke, a prominent Parliamentary leader and patron. Goodwin praises Brooke's piety and character, presenting this work as a source of comfort for those with a "struggling conscience."</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Goodwin, Robert Greville, Lord Brooke, dedication, patronage, piety, conscience, Beauchamp Court</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page begins the author's preface to the reader. It follows a formal Latin dedication to Lord Brooke. In this section, Thomas Goodwin explains his motivations for publishing these sermons, which were originally delivered eight years prior.</meta>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage, floral motifs, and a central decorative urn.</image-desc>

## To the Reader.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'T' within a square border, surrounded by vine and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

What motivated me to publish these sermons—besides your own benefit—was the need to set the record straight <note>original: "to right myself"</note>. They were first preached eight years ago, and some notes of them were (to say the least) circulated among many people in a way that was harmful to my reputation <note>original: "to my prejudice"</note>. They are presented here just as they were preached, with very little change or addition in terms of their organization, style, or content. The only exception is that, to make this treatise more complete for publication, I added the entire discourse regarding Satan’s role and influence in these spiritual desertions <term>desertion: a period of spiritual darkness where a believer feels abandoned by God or loses the sense of His comfort</term>, which begins in Chapter 6. 

In dealing with this subject, I trust I have not at all deserved that severe rebuke <note>original: "increpatio"</note> of the Apostle <note>Goodwin refers to the Apostle Paul’s warning in Colossians 2:18 against those who "intrude into things they have not seen."</note> against prying speculations about angels, or intruding into matters for which I have found no basis or authority in the Word of God. I am certain that I have tried to follow the Schoolmen <term>Schoole: referring to Scholastic theologians of the Middle Ages and Renaissance who used complex logic to debate divinity</term> through their intricate mazes <note>original: "Labyrinths"</note> on this topic only as far as I found a...

<summary>Thomas Goodwin explains that he is publishing these eight-year-old sermons to correct unauthorized and inaccurate notes that had been circulating. He also notes that he has added a new section on Satan’s role in spiritual darkness, while being careful to stay within biblical limits.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Goodwin, preface, sermons, spiritual desertion, Satan, Scholasticism, unauthorized publication, biblical authority</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface to the reader. Thomas Goodwin explains that his analysis of spiritual conflict is grounded in Scripture and logic, and he offers guidance for readers who may find the technical theological sections difficult to navigate.</meta>

*...the guidance of Scripture and sound reason, which clearly lead and justify my path. Without these, I consider the ways of that old and winding serpent <note>Satan</note> in his interactions with us to be as Solomon says: "Like the path of a snake on a rock" <note>Proverbs 30:19</note>, hidden and impossible to trace or discover. Lest any of the less experienced <note>original: "weaker"</note> readers—especially those in spiritual distress, who often find theoretical and doctrinal discussions (even about practical matters) to be tedious and unpleasant—be discouraged at the beginning of this work, my advice <note>original: "advertisement"</note> is this: if they find that part of the path difficult or tiring (which I hope they will not), they should skip ahead and pick up the text again at Chapter 11. From that point to the end, they will find material that is better suited to their understanding and circumstances, and which speaks more practically to their distress. May the blessing of heaven go with it.*

->**THOMAS GOODWIN.**<-

<summary>In this conclusion to his preface, Goodwin advises readers in spiritual distress to skip the technical theological chapters if they find them too difficult, directing them instead to the practical advice starting in Chapter 11.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual distress, Satan, Proverbs 30:19, biblical guidance, practical theology, Thomas Goodwin</keywords>

*...even if he knows little about us in particular. p. 84*

1. *He may know all that another man can know of us. p. 85. Such as all physical acts done by bodily substances. <note>original: "ibid."; Latin abbreviation for "ibidem," meaning "in the same place"</note>*

**Chapter 10. The conclusion: Seven advantages in common, which Satan has over us in all these dealings.** 106
1. *That he can suggest frequently and familiarly.* <note>original: "ibid."; in the same place</note>
2. *That he can present his suggestions and false reasonings together at once.* <note>original: "ibid."</note>
3. *That he holds and keeps the thoughts and intentions of the mind fixed on them.* 108
4. *That he sets upon everything with an imperious affirmation.* 109
5. *That he backs them also with terrors, which is an argument to sense.* <note>An "argument to sense" is a proof that feels undeniably real because it is experienced through physical or emotional sensations.</note> 110
6. *That he suggests without being noticed.* <note>original: "undiscerned"</note> <note>original: "ibid."</note>

# THE TABLE.

<meta>This page continues the text from page 1, completing the word "either." It begins the detailed exposition of Isaiah 50:10, describing the experience of "spiritual desertion" where a true believer loses the sense of God's favor.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

...either; the one in the tenth verse, and the other in the eleventh.

<margin>
Concerning the tenth verse.
2 Corinthians 4:6.
</margin>
First, consider a true believer who has had even the smallest beam of the *light of the glory of God* (which shines *in the face of Christ*) let into his soul. His heart was so captivated by that sight that it became eternally divorced from all worldly things, and he resolved to risk all his future hopes of comfort and happiness on the enjoyment of that *Light* of God’s countenance <note>original: "countenance"; meaning God's favorable presence or "face"</note> alone. So that he may enjoy this, he fears offending the *Lord* more than he fears Hell; and he endeavors as truly to obey the *voice of His servants* as he ever desires to attain that happiness. 

Think to yourselves: what is the worst thing, next to the eternal loss of God, that could actually happen to this man? What could be worse than having that cranny <note>original: "cranny"; a small opening or crack through which light enters</note> through which he first glimpsed that beam to be, as it were, completely shut up? The *Light of God’s countenance* is withdrawn; indeed, all *Light* and evidence of his own graces are withheld and overclouded. The face of heaven is so overcast with *Darkness* that neither *sunlight* nor *starlight* appears to him, so that he *has no light*. Furthermore, he finds his soul beset and besieged on all sides by the powers of Hell and darkness, with the terrors of the Almighty shot into his soul. 

And, being thus completely left *walking in this darkness*, he is filled with strong fears and suspicions <note>original: "jealousies"; here meaning painful anxieties or doubts about God's love</note> that God is not *his God*; he even questions whether God ever *will be* his God. Indeed, perceiving the wrath he feels, he believes *God has become his enemy*. All of this is presented to us here as the very condition of one *who fears the Lord and obeys Him*, and it is summarized in

<margin>
Conscience upon 
Psalm 77:7.
</margin>

...these words: *“Who walks in darkness and has no light.”* You see him at his worst.

In this forlorn <note>original: "forlorne"; meaning desolate or abandoned</note> condition, what can be found to relieve and support this man? There is only one thing held out to him: *the name of the Lord*, for him to trust and lean upon. This includes both the Name of God in Exodus 34:6—*“The Lord God, gracious and merciful, etc.”*—and the Name of Christ, which is called in Jeremiah 23:6, *“The Lord our Righteousness”* <note>original: "Iehovah our righteouſneſſe"; a translation of the Hebrew name *Yahweh Tsidqenu*</note>. By the naked hand of faith, he may lay hold of either or both of these and use them as a staff (just as David compares it in Psalm 23:4). While he walks in darkness and through the valley of the shadow of death, he may safely trust and lean upon them, so that in the end he may come forth <margin>Psalm 36:9.</margin> to see light in God’s light forevermore. You see here the support for his soul in this condition.

<margin>Concerning verse 11.</margin>
On the contrary, let us behold (as everyone is called to do here) the best and most secure of unregenerate <note>original: "unregenerate"; those who have not experienced spiritual rebirth or conversion</note> men. They are surrounded by every means and support for confidence and comfort, whether it is their own "legal righteousness" <note>original: "Legall righteouſneſſe"; performing religious duties or moral acts to earn God's favor through the Law</note>—which these Jews boasted of—or the addition of all worldly and outward comforts. The Prophet here compares both of these to *fire* and *sparks*, as they provide a temporary light and comfort. 

For example, <gloss>1</gloss> let their lives and natural dispositions abound with ever so many sparks of legal righteousness which they have kindled themselves. He compares these to the various acts of natural and acquired righteousness—forced out and drawn from <note>original: "educed"; brought out or developed</note> the power of improved natural principles—which create a great blaze in a person’s own opinion and self-esteem.

<summary>This page explains that a believer in spiritual darkness must rely solely on the "Name of the Lord" and the righteousness of Christ, contrasting this with the "unregenerate" who rely on their own self-kindled "sparks" of moral effort.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual darkness, Name of the Lord, Jehovah our Righteousness, legal righteousness, faith, unregenerate, self-esteem</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the contrast between the true light of God and the "sparks" of self-righteousness, specifically addressing those who rely on their own works and worldly comforts rather than the Holy Spirit.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

Yet, these actions do not proceed from the Holy Spirit baptizing them as "with fire" and renewing them; nor do they come from the internal principles of rebirth <note>original: "regeneration"</note>, which Christ compares to fire in the last verse of Mark 9 <note>original: "Mar. 9. ult."; likely referring to Mark 9:49, "everyone will be salted with fire."</note>. Therefore, in God's account, they are all merely like a **sacrifice offered up with strange fire**—a practice that was strictly forbidden—and are described here as being of their own kindling. 

Such were the **sparks in the light of which** those **Jews** walked, who went about <margin>Romans 10:3</margin> to **establish their own righteousness** and trusted confidently in it, rather than in the **name of the Lord**. 

Furthermore:
2. Let these men be surrounded and encompassed by the greatest splendor of worldly glory, and let them abound in all the good things this world can afford them. Solomon likewise compares the comforts of the world to a **fire of thorns** <margin>Ecclesiastes 7:6</margin> and its pleasures to the **crackling of thorns**, just as they are compared here to **sparks**. No matter how many good fires they keep to warm and cheer themselves—even if they pile on so much fuel every day that they are completely **encompassed by sparks**—and though they walk for many years in the light and confidence of both their own works and their worldly success, despising that other poor believer who fears being found relying on his own righteousness and refuses to be comforted by any of these things; yet, let them know (says Christ, who is presented as the speaker here) that when they have walked presumptuously and securely, and even walked themselves weary <margin>Isaiah 40:31</margin>—weary of all their own ways and pleasures, as they certainly will be one day—and then think to lie down and rest on their deathbeds:

They shall indeed lie down, says Christ, but their bed shall be of my making and providing: **this you shall have from my hand**,

### Chapter I.

*The main proposition and subject of this discourse, deduced from the text: That a child of God may walk in darkness. It is proved that this refers to distress of conscience and desertion <note>original: "desertion"; in Puritan theology, this refers to a period where a believer feels abandoned by God or lacks the "sensible" feeling of His presence.</note> regarding the lack of assurance of justification <note>original: "justification"; the theological state of being declared righteous by God through faith.</note>.*

<margin>
Dear Savior, let me find your face;
on You my hope I place.
So shall I fight the fight of faith
And run my race with joy.
</margin>
<margin>
The first and main observation: That a child of God may walk in darkness.
</margin>
That this is the meaning of the words will appear more fully in explaining the various propositions derived from them. The first and most important of these is this: *That one who truly fears God and is obedient to him may be in a condition of darkness and have no light; and he may walk for many days and years in that condition.*

<margin>
§. 1.
I.
What it means to "walk in darkness."
</margin>
In order to further explain the text and ground this important point firmly upon it—and more particularly to reveal the condition of a child of God who is thus in darkness—we will first inquire what is meant by "walking in darkness" in this specific passage.

<margin>
What is not meant here:
1. Not walking in sin.
</margin>
<margin>
1 John 1:6.
</margin>
First, "walking in darkness" is used in 1 John 1:6 to mean *living in sin and ungodliness*: committing known sins or omitting known duties, and continuing in the "works of darkness." However, it is not meant that way here. For Christ would not have encouraged people who are living in that way to trust in God. God is light, and there can be no fellowship between Him and that kind of darkness, as the Apostle tells us. Furthermore, the Holy Spirit reproves

<summary>This page introduces the primary theme of the book: that a sincere believer who fears and obeys God can nevertheless experience long periods of spiritual darkness and a lack of assurance. It distinguishes this "darkness" from the darkness of living in sin.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual darkness, child of God, desertion, assurance of faith, justification, 1 John 1:6, conscience</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of what it means to "walk in darkness," specifically clarifying that for a believer, this darkness is not the darkness of sin or ignorance, but rather a state of spiritual sorrow or distress.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

<margin>Micah 3:11</margin>
describes those who lean on the Lord and yet still sin <note>original: "transgresse"</note>. Furthermore, the text speaks of people who, in their present condition, fear God and are obedient to him. If they were "walking in darkness" in the sense of living in sin, they could not be described as being obedient.

<margin>
2.
Not in ignorance.

Proverbs 19:2
</margin>
Secondly, it is not meant as *walking in ignorance*, as the phrase is used in John 12:35 <note>The original citation is John 12:36, but the context of walking in darkness is verse 35.</note>. For someone who *has no light* in that sense can never truly fear God or obey him. Solomon says that *a heart that lacks knowledge is not good* (Proverbs 19:2); walking in that kind of darkness is accompanied by *walking in the futility of the mind* (Ephesians 4:18).

<margin>
3.
But in sorrow and discomfort.

Ecclesiastes 11:7
</margin>
Thirdly, the author means it as *discomfort and sorrow*. We often find *darkness* used this way in Scripture, such as in Ecclesiastes 5:17. Conversely, *light* is used to represent comfort because it is *such a pleasant thing to behold* (Ecclesiastes 11:7). That this is the meaning here is evident by the contrast in the next verse: *walk in your own light, yet you shall lie down in sorrow*.

<margin>4.</margin>
Fourthly, we must ask: what kind of *sorrow* is this, and what is its cause? Does it come from outward afflictions or inward distress of the mind and conscience? Or (to use Solomon's distinction), is it because of a man's ordinary weaknesses <note>original: "infirmities"</note> or a *wounded spirit*? That is the question at hand.

<margin>
1.
And that not of outward afflictions only.
</margin>
First, this state should not be limited to outward afflictions alone, which are called *human weaknesses* because they are *common to all people*. These arise from the things of this world or from the people of the world. While *walking in darkness* is used in this sense in Isaiah 59:9, and I will not exclude that meaning here—for in those trials, too, a person’s best support is to *trust in God*—it is the safest way to interpret the Scriptures in the broadest sense which...

<summary>This page clarifies that "walking in darkness" for a believer refers to a state of sorrow and spiritual discomfort rather than a state of sin or ignorance. It begins to distinguish between outward worldly afflictions and inward spiritual distress.</summary>

...which the words and the context <note>original: "coherence"</note> will support. But yet that cannot be the only or principal meaning of it. For, besides what is further to be said to the contrary, the prophet adds as well, *and has no light*, that is, no comfort. Now, as philosophers say, *there is no such thing as pure darkness* <note>original: "non dantur pura tenebræ"</note>—there is no pure darkness without some mixture of light. So we may say, there is no mere or utter darkness caused by outward afflictions; no outward affliction can so universally surround the mind as to shut up all its openings <note>original: "crannies"</note>, so that a man should have no light at all. And besides, God’s people, when they walk in the greatest outward darkness, may have—indeed, they often do have—the most light in their spirits. But here a state is spoken of: *such a darkness as has no light in it*.

<margin>But chiefly inward, from the lack of the sense of God's favor.</margin>
Therefore, secondly, it is principally to be understood as the lack of inward comfort in their spirits, resulting from something that is between God and them. It refers to those darknesses and terrors which accompany the lack of a sense of God’s favor. And so *darkness* is used elsewhere to mean inward affliction of spirit and mind, and a lack of light regarding the assurance that God is a man’s God and that his sins are pardoned. For example, in Psalm 88:6, Heman uses this word to express his distress. The reasons why it should be understood this way here are as follows:

<margin>Proved by three reasons. The first.</margin>
First, because the remedy prescribed here is faith: to lean <note>original: "stay"</note> himself upon God, and specifically upon *his God*. He adds *his God* emphatically because that is the very point the person is troubled about, the matter concerning which he is in darkness, and of which the prophet would have such a person be persuaded. And that is...

<margin>Micah 3:11</margin>...proves such as lean on the Lord and yet transgress. Besides, the text speaks of those who, in their current state, fear God and are obedient to Him—which, if they walked in darkness <note>in the sense of living in sin</note>, they could not be said to do.

<margin>2. Not in ignorance. Proverbs 19:2.</margin>Neither, secondly, is it to be understood as *walking in ignorance*, as it is used in John 12:35 <note>original: "John 12. 36"</note>. For one who has no light in that sense can never truly fear God nor obey Him; "the heart that lacks knowledge is not good," says Solomon; and so to walk in darkness is accompanied by walking in the vanity of the mind (Ephesians 4:18).

<margin>3. But in sorrow and discomfort. Ecclesiastes 11:7.</margin>But thirdly, he means it *of discomfort and sorrow*. That it is used this way here is evident by the contrast in the next verse: "*walk in your own light, yet you shall lie down in sorrow*" <note>Isaiah 50:11</note>.

<margin>4.</margin>But fourthly, of what kind of sorrow, and for what cause? Is it from outward afflictions or inward distress of mind and conscience? Or (to use Solomon’s distinction) is it by reason of a person’s ordinary infirmities or a wounded spirit? That is still the question.

<margin>1. And that not of outward afflictions only.</margin>And first, it is not to be restricted to outward afflictions only, which are called "human infirmities" <note>original: "mans infirmities"</note> because they are common to everyone and arise from worldly things or from the men of the world. Although walking in darkness is used that way in Isaiah 59:9, and I will not exclude that meaning here—for in those situations, too, a person’s best support is to *trust in God*—it is safest to interpret the Scriptures in the broadest sense which

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 6, narrowing the definition of "walking in darkness" from general sorrow to specific inward spiritual distress.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>walking in Darkness.</header>

...which the words and context will allow. But still, that cannot be the only or primary meaning of it. For, besides what else may be said to the contrary, the author adds, *and has no light*, meaning no comfort. Now, as philosophers say, *there is no such thing as pure darkness* <note>original: "non dantur pura tenebræ"</note>, as there is no darkness without some mixture of light. So we may say there is no absolute or total darkness caused solely by outward afflictions; no external suffering can so completely surround the mind as to shut up every crack and crevice, leaving a person with no light at all. Furthermore, God's people, when they walk in the greatest outward darkness, can have—and often do have—the most light in their spirits. But the condition spoken of here is such a state, such a darkness, that it has no light in it at all.

In this way, "darkness" is used elsewhere in Scripture to mean *inward affliction* of the spirit and mind, and a lack of light regarding the assurance that God is a person's God and that their sins are pardoned. For example, in Psalm 88:6, Heman <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the traditional author of Psalm 88, which is known as one of the most sorrowful psalms in the Bible.</note> uses this word to express his distress. ### The details of the distress contained in these two phrases: *Walking in Darkness* and *Having no Light*.

As light and faith are separated <note>original: "severed"</note> here, as you see, so sight is also distinguished from faith in 2 Corinthians 5:7. Faith is the *evidence of things absent and not seen* (Hebrews 11:1). Therefore, when he says here that the person has *no light*, the meaning is that he lacks <note>original: "wants"</note> all present sensory <note>original: "sensible"; meaning: perceivable by the senses or felt experience</note> testimonies of God’s favor toward him. It is as if they are not there, or never had been; he is completely deprived of that light which normally reveals these things as present.

<margin>A threefold light added to faith to cause assurance.</margin>
To understand this, we must recognize that to help our *faith* (which, as I said before, is distinct from *sight*), God graciously provides a threefold *light* to his people. This is intended to add *assurance* and *joy* to their *faith*. This light is to faith like a steel backing on a bow to strengthen it; it is designed to be taken off or put on at God’s good pleasure.

<margin>1. The immediate light of God's countenance,</margin>
First, there is the *immediate light of his countenance* <term>the expression or presence of God’s face, signifying His favor</term>. This is a clear, evident beam and revelation of God’s favor, testifying directly that we are his. This is called the *sealing of the Spirit*, received *after believing* (Ephesians 1:13), which *David* desired and rejoiced in more than in all worldly things.

<margin>Psalm 4:6.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how God may temporarily hide his presence from believers. It explains that even when the "light" of God's favor is gone, the "influence" of his grace remains to support the soul, comparing this to how the sun still affects the earth during an eclipse.</meta>

# A Child of Light

His children out of his presence when he is angry, and it is but for a moment; that is, in comparison to eternity. This is true even if it might happen <note>original: "happily"; here meaning "perchance" or "by hap"</note> to be this way with a man during his whole life. He therefore takes the liberty to do it because he has such an eternity of time to reveal his kindness in—time enough for kisses and embraces, and to pour forth his love.

<margin>2. And with the real influence of grace.</margin>
And for the second point: the real, gracious *influences* and effects of his favor may be continued, upholding, strengthening, and carrying on the soul to still *obey and fear him*, even while he still conceals his favor.

<margin>God communicates himself either as he is blessed, or as he is holy; as he is blessed, he gives joy and glory; as he is holy, he gives grace. Both are given voluntarily, and therefore both are not necessarily given at the same time. <note>original Latin: "Deus se communicat, vel qua beatus; vel qua sanctus; qua beatus, gaudium et gloria; qua sanctus, gratiam: Vtrumque voluntarie, ideoq; non utrumq; simul necessario."</note></margin>
For when Christ complained, *My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?* (when as great an eclipse regarding the light of God's countenance was upon his spirit as was upon the earth), yet he never more obeyed God, nor was he ever more strongly supported than at that time, for then he was *obeying even to death*. We see that when the sun is eclipsed, although the earth lacks its *light*, it does not lack its *influence* <note>In the 17th century, "influence" referred to an invisible power or fluid flowing from the stars or sun that affected the earth.</note>. The metals which are generated in the bottom of the earth are matured <note>original: "concocted"; an alchemical term for the process of ripening or purifying substances through heat.</note> by the sun; so, although the light of the sun does not reach them, the influence and virtue of it does, and it alters and changes them. In the same way, God’s favor visits men's hearts in the power, heat, and vigorous influence of his grace, even when the light and comfort of it does not, but is blocked <note>original: "intercluded"</note>.

<margin>2. Light is of present graces;</margin>
The second light which God ordinarily grants his people to help and supplement <note>original: "eek out"</note> their faith is *the sight and comfort of their own graces*, to which so many promises belong—such as their love for his people, fear of his name, and desire to obey him.

<summary>This page explains that God may withdraw the "light" of his comfort while still providing the "influence" of his grace to sustain a believer's obedience. It uses the analogy of a solar eclipse to show how God’s power can work in the heart even when His presence cannot be felt.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual darkness, grace vs. comfort, solar eclipse, influence of grace, Christ's suffering, internal evidence of faith</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the different levels of spiritual "light" a believer might lose. After explaining that God’s influence remains even when His presence is hidden, the author now compares visible grace to starlight and explains how even that light can be extinguished in the soul's perception.</meta>

<center>Walking in Darkness</center>

Often when the sun has set, starlight still appears. That is, even if the immediate presence and evidence of God's favor <note>The "Sun" represents the direct feeling of God's love.</note> does not shine on the soul, his graces still appear within it as tokens of that love. In this way, the soul knows that a sun still exists to give light to these stars, even though it cannot see the sun itself. Just as in the night we know there is a sun in another horizon <note>original: "Horizon"</note> because the stars we see receive their light from it, we are certain that it will rise for us again.

<margin>
How blessed is my present state,
Immensely rich am I:
Christ is my life, death is my gain,
I soon shall reign on high.
</margin>

<margin>
A soul may also lack this starlight.
</margin>

Now, a soul that possesses true grace and continues to obey God may also lack the light needed to see these graces, and might look at its own heart as if it were completely empty of all good. Just like those caught in the storm in Acts 27:20, a person facing temptation may reach a point where they have neither *sunlight* nor *starlight*—*no light* at all, as the text says. Likewise, in Isaiah <note>original: "Esay"</note> 63:17, the Church complains that God had *hardened their hearts from his fear*. They were afraid, feeling their hearts were so hard that the fear of God was missing; and yet, that fear was actually present, because they were mourning the lack of it.

<margin>
3. Light may be understood as the memory of former graces and evidence of faith.
</margin>

Thirdly, even if someone lacks the present light of God’s countenance <term>countenance: God's smiling face or favor</term> and the sight of current grace, they may still have a comforting memory of what they once possessed. As long as that remains, they are not left in total darkness. Therefore, understand further that the state of one who fears and obeys God can be such that they have no comforting light or memory of whatever grace or evidence they *formerly had*. In 2 Peter 1:9, it speaks of someone who has true grace but lacks the exercise of it. I believe this passage refers to a regenerate <term>regenerate: a person who has been spiritually reborn</term> man, because it says he *was purged from his old sins*, and is now said...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how a believer may lose the perception of their own spiritual state. It explains that even if a person possesses grace, they may lose the "light" needed to recognize it, comparing the soul's internal graces to stars that only shine when illuminated by the sun of God’s Spirit.</meta>

# A Child of Light

<margin>which he may lack.</margin>
A person can be said to lack grace because they do not *use* it. For *to not have something is the same as to not use it* <note>original: "id est non habere, & non uti"</note>; a person is said to lack what they do not *use* when they ought to use it (especially in things whose worth lies entirely in their use and application, for it is as if they do not have it at all). In this state, a person may fall into such *blindness* that they cannot see *into the distance* <note>A reference to 2 Peter 1:9, describing someone who has forgotten their salvation.</note>, and so they forget their former assurance that they *were cleansed from their old sins*. They may even begin to question everything. This happened to *David* in *Psalm* 30:6–7. Even though his heart had been *full of joy* and assurance of God’s favor just a moment before, as soon as God *hid his face*, everything was gone. *I was troubled*, he says. He was so blind that he could not see what had been true just a short time before, much like a person lost in a mist.

<margin>The reason for both.</margin>
<margin><insert>graces shine</insert></margin>
The reason for these last two points is as clear as the experience of them. For the graces within us *shine* only with a *borrowed light*, just as the stars do with light borrowed from the sun. Therefore, unless God chooses to shine secretly and give light to your graces—to illuminate <note>original: "irradiate"</note> them—your graces will not appear to comfort you, nor will they serve as a witness of God's favor to assure you. For *our spirit*—that is, our internal graces—never bears witness alone. If God’s Spirit does not join in testimony with it, it remains silent: *The Spirit of God witnesses with our spirits* (Romans 8:16). Therefore, when God has withdrawn his testimony, the testimony of our own hearts and our own graces carries no weight.

<margin>Question.</margin>
But you might ask: can a person practice grace and not know it? Can they fear God and yet not perceive that they do?

<summary>The author explains that spiritual assurance depends on God's Spirit illuminating a person's internal graces; without this "borrowed light," a believer may become "blind" to their own faith and past experiences of God's mercy.</summary>

# Walking in Darkness

<margin>Answer: How grace may be practiced, even when it is not noticed.</margin>
Yes: and some graces may then be practiced in the heart just as much as at any other time. A person may fear God as truly and as much as ever, and yet this fear has no light in it to reveal itself to him. It may be in the heart *in being and in operation* <note>original: "in esse & operari"</note> even when it is not *perceived* <note>original: "in cognosci"</note>. It may have a presence and a function there even when you are not aware of it.

<margin>The reason.</margin>
<insert>
Will Death this body soon destroy?
Oh yes! I wait the coming hour.
What then? The Lord will raise it up
Again, by His Almighty Power.
</insert>
The reason is that just as the influence of God’s favor may truly be in the heart when the sense, sight, and light of it are withdrawn (as was said before), the power of grace may similarly be in the heart when its light and comfort are missing. And although it is true that every person has the power to reflect upon their own actions and can discern what thoughts and affections are within them—being able to tell, as far as the subject matter goes, what they are thinking, that they are placing their trust, or that they are grieving, etc.—they may still question whether those thoughts are acts of true and sincere faith. They may doubt whether those feelings of sorrow for sin are sanctified, holy, genuine, and spiritual affections. 

The reason for this difference is that although the natural spirit within a person "knows the things of a man" (as the Apostle says in 1 Corinthians 2:11)—that is, he understands his own thoughts and actions physically, as they are the natural acts of a human being—the spirit of a man is "deceitful" <margin>Jeremiah 17:9</margin> and cannot know the true moral goodness of those acts without the supernatural light of the Spirit of God. Just as the Spirit is the giver and mover of that grace within us, He is also "given by God so that we might know the things that are freely given to us by God" (1 Corinthians 2:12). <meta>This page concludes the argument that grace and joy can exist independently of each other, then begins to define "walking in darkness" as a state of extreme spiritual doubt where a believer questions their own salvation and God's mercy.</meta>

...righteous, and joy for the upright," says the Psalmist. Grace and the practice of it are the seed which they continually scatter, but light and joy are the harvest that is to be reaped. The seed often lies hidden for a long time, though it will eventually sprout in the end. Thus, light or joy may be separated from grace; the comfort of grace may be absent even while its power remains active.

<margin>
§ 2.
2.
What his condition is, as expressed by "walking in darkness."
</margin>

Secondly, let us further consider the second phrase, and what it suggests about his condition when (as it is said) he "walks in darkness."

<margin>
1.
To be in doubt about what will become of him.
</margin>

First, "to walk in darkness" implies being in doubt about where to go: as it says in *John 12:35, "He who walks in darkness does not know where he is going."* In this way, the soul of one who fears God may be filled with doubts about whether God will ever be merciful to him or not. He may not know what God intends to do with him—whether he shall go to heaven or hell. *Psalm 77:7–9* asks, *"Will the Lord be merciful?"* These words are spoken in doubt; for in verse 10, he says this was his *infirmity* <note>original: "infirmity"; here meaning a spiritual weakness or a lapse in faith</note> to even call this into question.

So it was with Heman in *Psalm 88:5, 6, 11, 12*. He thought of himself as one who was already in hell—*"free among the dead."* That is, he felt like one admitted into the company of those there (verse 5); "free of that company" <note>This uses the metaphor of being a "freeman" or a full member of a group or guild—in this case, the "company" of the damned.</note>, as you might say, and counted among the number of those whom God no longer remembered. He was in such darkness (verse 6) that he doubted whether God would ever raise him out of that condition: *"Will you show wonders to the dead? Shall your wonders be declared in the grave?"* (verses 10–12). By this, he meant: has God ever shown mercy to someone in the same state as those in hell?—which was how he viewed his own current state. Indeed, he was so far gone as to be without hope: as in *Lamentations 3:18, "My hope has perished from the Lord."*

Secondly, those in *darkness* are likely to *stumble* at everything. In *Isaiah 59:10*, one effect of darkness mentioned is to *stumble at noonday*. Take a soul that is left in darkness, and it will stumble at everything it hears out of the Word, whether in conversation or in sermons. It is more discouraged by everything it reads and all the promises it encounters. "Oh," they think, "how can there be such glorious promises that do not belong to us!" Such a person misapplies and misinterprets all of God's dealings and the Scriptures against themselves, and refuses comfort, as in *Psalm 77:2*. Indeed, as in the third verse, when he *remembers God, he is troubled*.

Psalm 88.
</margin>
Thirdly, *darkness* is extremely terrifying and full of horror. Just as children in the dark think they see fearful sights, it is called the *horror of darkness* in *Genesis 15:12*. Similarly, a soul here may be filled with fears and terrors regarding God’s wrath and the fear of God being an enemy to him. *Heman* <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the traditional author of Psalm 88, often cited as a primary example of a godly person suffering extreme spiritual despair.</note> was almost driven to madness <note>original: "distracted and out of his wits"</note> with terrors. The Church thought the same in *Lamentations 3*, and even concluded for certain that God was her enemy: *Surely he has turned against me (verse 3)*.

<margin>The three direct causes of this distress.</margin>
First, regarding the efficient causes of this so woeful, desperate, and dark condition for God's child: there are three which play a part in it.

<margin>

First, God’s Spirit.

<margin>
§. 1.
1. The Spirit,
</margin>
First, regarding God’s Spirit: Although he has a hand in some part of this disquiet, we must be careful not to attribute to him any of those doubts, desperate fears, or conclusions by which the child of God questions his own spiritual state. For the Spirit is not the *direct agent* <note>original: "direct efficient"</note> or *actual cause* <note>original: "positive cause"</note> of them.

<margin>Not the cause of doubtful and despairing thoughts;</margin>
To this end, we may consider that well-known passage: *Romans 8:15 <note>The original text cites Romans 8:16, but the quoted verse is Romans 8:15: "For ye have not received the spirit of bondage again to fear; but ye have received the Spirit of adoption."</note>: You have not received the spirit of bondage to fear again, but the spirit of adoption.* A right understanding of this will also prevent an objection. Some have argued from this passage that after a child of God has once received the Spirit as a seal of their adoption, they could never again fall into a sense of bondage—that is, into fears of eternal damnation or of being handed over to hell. They claim that this can only happen to a person once, at their first conversion.

But if we observe the words carefully, the Apostle does not affirm that fears of bondage can never happen to God's child again. Instead, his purpose is to show that the Spirit we have received—once he has become the *Spirit of adoption*—that Spirit is never after...

<meta>The text continues on the next page with "afterward the cause of such fears."</meta>

Indeed, at first conversion, and before he witnessed our adoption, he revealed our condition to us as a state of bondage. He did this in love to drive us out of it; and at that time, he was indeed a *spirit of bondage*. The Apostle Paul refers to this when he says "to fear again," because the Spirit was once such a spirit to them. The *Holy Spirit* <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note> might then testify to them that their state was damnable. That was a truth at the time, because they had lived in a state of bondage for which damnation was immediately due; had they died in it, it certainly would have fallen upon them. 

But once he has made a person a son and has become the *spirit of adoption* to him, if he should ever put him into such apprehensions and fears again, he would be testifying to an untruth. Therefore, for the comfort of them and all believers, he tells them that he never contradicts or reverses his testimony of adoption. Instead, his office is to be ready as a witness to seal and confirm it. <margin>But our own hearts and Satan. 1 John 3:20.</margin> 

Yet, even though the *Judge* <note>God.</note> does not condemn us anymore, the *jailer* <note>original: "Iaylor"; meaning Satan.</note> may still trouble and frighten us, and our own *hearts* may condemn us. God may give *Satan* leave to cast us into prison, to put shackles <note>original: "clap bolts"</note> <margin>April 1 Proverbs 5:22.</margin> upon us again, and to become a lying *spirit of bondage* to us—just as he became a lying spirit in the mouths of Ahab's prophets <note>A reference to 1 Kings 22, where a "lying spirit" was permitted by God to deceive the prophets of King Ahab.</note>. God may also give our hearts up to be fettered with the "cords of our own sins" (Proverbs 5:22) and to be ensnared by their own inventions, fears, and jealousies.

The whole situation is to be attributed to these three distinct sources <note>original: "severall hands"</note>, and the works of God's Spirit and his involvement in this must be carefully separated from Satan's work, just as light was separated from darkness at the beginning of creation.

So far, then, the Spirit of God may concur in this darkness that befalls his child:

<margin>1. Privately, by withdrawing his testimony.</margin>
1. *Privately*: He may suspend his testimony and the fulfillment of his role in *witnessing to our adoption*. He may withdraw his comforting presence and hide himself *for a moment*, concealing his love as human fathers sometimes do. For example, *David* did this even when his heart was still toward *Absalom* <note>Referring to 2 Samuel 14, where King David allowed his son Absalom to return to Jerusalem but refused to see him face-to-face for two years.</note>. A father may not allow a son to see his face; he may shut a son out of the house, even when he has not truly cast him off.

*He may retain their sins* (to use Christ's expression). This means he may call back the patent <note>term: A "patent" was a formal legal document or decree granting a specific right or privilege, such as a pardon.</note> of pardon that he had issued under his hand and seal *on earth*—that is, in their own consciences. He may take it out of their hands and custody and call it back to the "pardon office" in *Heaven* and keep it there. Furthermore, when Satan comes and provides false testimony and evidence, and our own hearts consequently condemn us, the Holy Spirit may stand by silently, as it were. He might say nothing to the contrary, choosing not to contradict Satan with a clear testimony or a secret rebuke as he does at other times, such as in *Zechariah 3:1–2*.

Forbid it, Lord! Your Spirit [sent?] <note>The OCR "ſe" is unclear; "sent" or "be" fits the poetic meter.</note> to quicken every thought to soar above this trifling world till I to You am brought.

1. To reveal and represent God as angry with his child for specific sins committed in the past, and to make the child feel that anger. This is done not merely by concealing His love, but by making impressions of His wrath upon the conscience directly <note>original: "immediately," meaning without an intervening cause</note>, and not only through outward hardships <note>original: "crosses"</note>. 

Thus, in Isaiah 57:17–18, God not only hid himself and was angry (expressing His wrath by hiding), but He says, *"I struck him and was angry."* And in verse 16, He "contended" and was angry—that is, He fought against him like an enemy, as in Isaiah 63:10. This was done by placing His wrath upon the person's spirit, for the text continues by saying that the spirit was ready to fail, along with the soul which He had made. Thus, the spirit was the target <note>term: "White" refers to the center mark or bullseye of an archery target.</note> God shot at and wounded—and wounded so deeply that it was ready to fail and perish. 

Solomon calls this, by way of distinction, a "wounded spirit," asking, *"who can bear it?"* (Proverbs 18:14). He distinguishes it from all other afflictions of the "outward man," which strike the spirit only indirectly <note>original: "mediately"</note> through the "clothes" of the body. For he says, *"The spirit of a man will sustain his infirmity"*—meaning all those outward afflictions where the spirit suffers only through sympathy and compassion for the body. But when the spirit itself is laid bare and naked, and wounded directly by God’s wrath (which alone can reach it and wound it), *"Who can bear this?"* 

Similarly toward Heman, God did not only hide His face from him (Psalm 88:14), but Heman says, *"Your fierce wrath went over me, and your terrors cut me off"* (verse 16). God did not just wound him, but even "cut him off." Such impressions of direct wrath, acting as expressions and effects of God's anger, the Holy Spirit <note>original: "holy Ghost"</note> may indeed make upon the spirit of His child; for it is a truth...

<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of how God "actively" represents His anger to a believer's soul. It moves from the internal feeling of God's displeasure to the specific way the Holy Spirit uses scripture and spiritual discipline to correct a child of God.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

...that God is angry and wrathful with them when they sin. He may make this anger known not only through "dumb signs" in outward hardships <note>original: "crosses"</note> and consequences, but by a direct <note>original: "immediate," meaning without any intervening medium</note> witnessing—speaking so clearly and expressly to their consciences that they feel it as "scalding drops" of His hot displeasure falling upon them. 

Just as human fathers show their anger by whipping the bodies of their children (for the reason given by the Apostle, because they are the *fathers of our flesh*, Hebrews 12:9), God may, for a similar reason, show His anger and discipline His children by lashing their spirits. For He is the *Father of our spirits*, as the same passage says. Likewise, our spirits—even their very "bones and marrow"—lie open and naked to Him <margin>Hebrews 4:12, 13</margin> *with whom we have to deal*. His Word and Spirit are "quick" <note>term: "quick" here means living or active</note> and powerful, and *sharper than any two-edged sword*, able *to divide and cut even to the bones and marrow*, as the same author says. 

Yet, even when He expresses His wrath on their consciences in this way, He does not testify that this is an *eternal* wrath He has conceived against them. Instead, it is only a temporary displeasure—*it is but for a moment*, as Isaiah <note>original: "Esay"</note> says. It is the indignation of a father; it is not a wrath stirred by vengeful justice, but by *Fatherly affection* <margin>Hebrews 12:6</margin>. And although the Spirit tells them that God is displeased, He never tells them they are accursed. To believe so is a false conclusion <note>original: "collection"</note> drawn from the experience.

<margin>2. By brandishing threats of eternal wrath over him.</margin>
Secondly, the Holy Spirit may proceed even further in this matter. He may go so far as to bring forth, show him, and brandish <note>original: "shake over him"</note> the rod of His eternal wrath, especially...

And this is done, both <margin>1</margin> by presenting to them and impressing upon them all those threats which hypothetically and conditionally threaten even believers with eternal damnation. Such is the threat we find in Romans 8:13: *If you live after the flesh, you (even you believers) shall die.* For there is a truth in all such threats, when they are conditionally proposed, which reaches even God’s dearest children. They are applied under a condition and in relation to continuing in sin, in order to stop and prevent the person when he is going on stubbornly in the way of his heart. The Holy Spirit may bring home such threats to him regarding the course he is entering into, and accordingly stir up the fear of that damnation thus threatened, if he should go on in those sins he has begun to commit. But to apply threats of eternal damnation simply to his person—such as "you shall die eternally"—this the Holy Spirit does not speak to the heart of a believer while he remains a believer.

For instance, how He swore against many of the Israelites, because of their provocations of Him, *that they should never enter into His rest*; and how He rejected Esau for the despising of his birthright. All of this is done with the goal to startle and awaken the believer, and with the hint that for such and such sins, God might deal with him in a similar way. For these and similar examples does...

## Chapter 4
### *How Satan and our own hearts increase this darkness by drawing false conclusions from the Spirit's work, illustrated by similar situations in the enlightenment of "temporaries" <note>original: "Temporaries" — a theological term for those who appear to have faith for a time but eventually fall away, as in the Parable of the Sower</note>. The Spirit's work in both cases compared.*

<margin>From this work of the Spirit, Satan and our hearts draw false conclusions.</margin>
And now, the Spirit of God having gone this far in causing such darkness and terrors of conscience in those who fear Him, Satan and their own hearts (to which God may and often does further abandon them) may take the opportunity from these actions of the Holy Spirit—which are all holy, righteous, and true—to draw false and fearful conclusions against themselves and their spiritual standing <term>original: "estates"</term>. They may raise staggering <term>original: "amazing"</term> doubts and fears about their total lack of grace, believing they lie under the curse and threats of eternal wrath at the present moment. Furthermore, they may fear eternal rejection in the future, believing that God will never be merciful. In this way, they are laid lower and cast into a deeper darkness and bondage than the Holy Spirit intended to cause...

This is caused by, or intended to be: misinterpreting and perverting all of these, His righteous proceedings. For instance, they interpret the withdrawal of His light and presence, and His hiding of Himself, as being "cast off." This was the case with **Heman** <margin>*Psalm* 88:14</margin>. Similarly, they misconstrue that temporary wrath—which chastises and wounds their spirits for the present—as being nothing other than the impressions and the first installment <term>original: "earnest"</term> of God's eternal vengeance. They argue that because they are currently *under wrath*, they must be "children of wrath." 

Furthermore, they misapply the warnings of eternal damnation given by the Spirit. These warnings are intended to be conditional—given in relation to certain sinful paths one might take in the future—but they take them as absolute judgments against their own persons and as descriptions of their present spiritual state <term>original: "estate"</term>. Because examples of people who were rejected by God are presented to them to show what justice God *could* execute against them, they mistakenly think they are reading their own destiny laid out in those examples. They conclude that God will deal with them in the same way. 

The Apostle Paul says something similar regarding the Law in *Romans* 7:11: *Sin, taking opportunity through the commandment* (which he misunderstood when he was a **Pharisee**), *deceived him, and through it killed him*. Yet, the commandment itself is *holy, and just, and good* (verse 12). In the same way, Satan and our own hearts take advantage of these dealings of the Spirit (which are righteous and true, just as He is the Spirit of truth who leads into truth) to deceive believers. They cause believers to perceive themselves as being *among the slain whom God remembers no more*, as **Heman** says in *Psalm* 88:5.

<margin>The same thing is seen in the case of "temporaries" <note>original: "Temporaries" — a theological term for those who appear to have faith for a time but eventually fall away</note>.</margin>
And just as it is in these cases, so it is in other works and distributions of God’s Spirit: it is common for Satan and our hearts to practice similar delusions and false conclu- <note>The text continues on the next page with "conclusions."</note>

<summary>This page explains how believers in spiritual darkness often misinterpret God's temporary discipline or withdrawal as signs of eternal damnation. It compares this deception to how the Apostle Paul once misunderstood the Law, noting that while the Spirit's work is holy, Satan and the human heart can twist it into despair.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the human heart, influenced by Satan, misinterprets the Holy Spirit's actions. It transitions from how believers misread "desertion" (spiritual darkness) to how those who are not yet fully converted misread "illumination" (spiritual light).</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

<margin>Things that accompany salvation <note>original: "ἐχόμενα τῆς σωτηρίας"</note>.</margin>
...conclusions upon them. To give an example in those more common and inferior works of the Spirit on the hearts of men who are not yet saved through spiritual rebirth <term>original: "savingly regenerated"</term>: The **Spirit** enlightens them, along with feelings of joy and a taste of sweetness in the promises of the **Gospel** and of the salvation revealed in it. On the condition of true repentance and conversion, the **Spirit of God** makes the offer and presentation of these things known to their hearts. 

The Spirit worked this way upon the "stony ground" <note>A reference to Jesus's Parable of the Sower (Matthew 13), where some receive the word with joy but have no roots.</note> and in the Jews through **John's** ministry (*John 5:35*). Through Satan’s abuse of this good work and the self-flattery of their own hearts, they often mistake this light, this "taste," and this revelation of a conditional offer—which is intended to lead toward salvation by drawing their hearts to seek it—for that **grace which accompanies salvation**, or which has salvation joined to it. 

The Apostle Paul, in *Hebrews 6:9*, uses that very expression to distinguish between true grace and those **enlightenings** mentioned in verse 4. These people mistake these works that lead up to grace just as the Jews mistook **John** (who was only sent ahead to **prepare the way for Christ**) for the true **Christ** who was to come into the world. By misunderstanding the intention <term>original: "entendment"</term> of God's most blessed Spirit in such dealings, they reach a hasty conclusion that the **Spirit** did not intend in those premises.

God, out of a temporary anger, chastises them for a moment, just as He shines upon the others with a temporary favor. So, as they rejoice for a season in that light (*John 5:35*), God’s dearest children *may be* for a season *in much heaviness* (as the Apostle speaks in *1 Peter 1:6*) *and walk in darkness*.

Therefore, the blame for these feelings is to be divided between Satan and our own hearts. To speak more specifically of each:

### CHAPTER V.

->*How our own hearts are the causes of this darkness:*<-
->*The principles within them which are the causes of it.*<-

<margin>
April 10
While others boast of nature's frames
And ecstasies of joy

First, it is not at all a wonder that our own hearts should be the causes and producers of such distress and darkness when the Holy Spirit deals with us in this way; because:

<margin>
§. 1.
1. By reason of our weakness as we are creatures.
</margin>

1. As we are creatures, there is such a weakness and frailty in us, as *David* speaks of; because of this, if God does but hide himself and withdraw his presence (which supports us in comfort just as it does in our very existence), we are immediately ready to fall into these fears by ourselves. <margin>Psalm 104:29.</margin> The *Psalmist* says of all creatures, *“You hide your face, and they are troubled.”* This trouble is due to their weakness and dependence upon God. And the dependence of the "new creature" <term>original: "new creature"; the spiritual nature of a person born again in Christ</term> upon God’s face and presence is no less—in fact, it is far greater. It cannot stand alone or support itself; it fails if God hides himself, as *Isaiah* <note>original: "Esay"; the Early Modern spelling of Isaiah</note> says in chapter 57. This is especially true during this life, in the "infancy" of our spiritual nature, while it is still a child. As God says of *Ephraim* in *Hosea* 11:1, then it cannot stand or go alone unless God carries it in his arms and teaches it to walk, as he says there in verses 1 and 3. And then also, just as children left alone in the dark are afraid of "bugbears" <term>original: "bugbeares"; imaginary monsters or objects of fear used to frighten children</term> and things they do not understand, and are prone to stumble and fall because of their weakness: so it is with the new creature in its childhood here in this life. *“It was my infirmity,”* says *David*.

If I withdraw my trust from You
I soon grow arrogant
Forget Your fear; grow proud in self
And so prove I know nothing

<margin>Psalm 30:6.</margin>David; and again, *“You did hide your face and I was troubled.”*

There is not only such a weakness in us because we are creatures, but:

<margin>§ 2. 2. Of an innate darkness because we are sinful creatures.</margin>2. There is also an innate *darkness* in our spirits because we are *sinful creatures*. Since the Fall <note>original: "the fall"; the biblical event of Adam and Eve's first sin which corrupted human nature</note>, our hearts by themselves are nothing but *darkness*. Therefore, it is no wonder if, when God merely draws the curtains and shuts out the light from us, our hearts produce and conceive such horrific fears and doubts. Thus, in 2 Corinthians 4:6, the Apostle compares this native darkness of our hearts to that *Chaos* and mass of *darkness* which covered *the face of the deep* at the first creation. He says that *“God, who commanded light to shine out of darkness”* (referring to the first creation in Genesis 1:1–2) *“has shined into our hearts”* [even those of the Apostles] *“to give the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Jesus Christ.”* 

So, our hearts [even those of us who are believers] retain light only as long as God continues to shine either the *light of comfort* or the light of *grace* upon us. And if at any time he withholds *that light of comfort from his face*—even while he continues to provide an influence of grace—our hearts immediately return to their former darkness to that extent. Then *that vast womb of darkness* conceives and forms all those fears and doubts within itself. Consider also that our hearts are a *great deep as well*; they are so deep in darkness and deceitfulness that no plummet <term>original: "plummet"; a lead weight on a string used by sailors to measure the depth of water</term> can fathom them: *“The heart is deceitful above all things... who can know it?”* (Jeremiah 17:9). *Darkness does not only cover the face of this deep*, but it is dark all the way to the bottom—total darkness throughout.

<summary>This page explains that human hearts possess an "innate darkness" due to the Fall, meaning they have no spiritual light of their own. Consequently, if God withdraws His "light of comfort," the heart naturally reverts to a state of fear, doubt, and deceit.</summary>
<keywords>innate darkness, sinful nature, the Fall, 2 Corinthians 4:6, Jeremiah 17:9, spiritual desertion, light of grace, deceitfulness of the heart</keywords>

...through the distempers and prejudices of self-love, to construct—to speak in the phrasing of the Apostle <note>referring to St. Paul in Romans 1</note>—false syllogisms <term>syllogism: a form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</term> and to draw wrong conclusions, more than about any other spiritual truth whatever.

It leads them to conclude decisively against their present spiritual state by similarly misapplying the word, but especially by misinterpreting God’s dealings toward them. 

Because they are sometimes led by their physical senses and human reason while they walk in darkness, they are prone to interpret God’s mind toward them based on his works and providential dealings—which they see and feel—rather than by his word, which they are called to believe. 

We can see this in **Gideon** in **Judges 6**. Because God was not working miracles as he had done for his people in the past, but had instead delivered them into the hands of their enemies, Gideon reasoned against the message of the Angel (who was Christ himself). The Angel told him, *“The Lord is with you”* (verse 12), but Gideon objected: *“O my Lord, if the Lord is with us, why then has all this happened to us? And where are all the miracles our fathers told us about? But now the Lord has forsaken us,”* and so on. This we can also see in **Asaph** <note>a Levite and choir leader credited with writing several Psalms</note>, or whatever other holy penman <note>original catchword: "pen-"</note>...

How would he have sunk in Heman’s deeps *(Psalm 88)* or in David’s *(Psalm 69:2)*: *I sink in the deep mire where there is no standing; I have come into deep waters, where the floods overflow me.* 

He is speaking of such waters as entered into his very soul *(verse 1)*: even the floods of God’s immediate wrath breaking in upon his conscience, overflowing the inward man <term>inward man: a person's soul, spirit, or conscience</term> and not just the outward body. How much more definitively would he have concluded against himself if this had been his condition? As indeed they did;

In general: *Reason* is of itself a busy principle <term>principle: an inner force or inclination</term> *that will be prying into and making false interpretations <note>original: "glosses"</note> upon all of God’s matters as well as our own,* and trying its skill in arguing about all His dealings with us. Thus *Jeremiah* felt he must reason with God about His dispensations toward wicked men (Chapter 12, verses 1–2), and *Job* about God’s dealings with himself (Chapter 13, verse 3). And *Reason* being likewise the supreme principle in us by nature—and our highest distinction as human beings—it is no wonder if, when we are left to ourselves *to walk in darkness, we walk as men*, as the Apostle speaks in 1 Corinthians 3:3. Here, it represents trusted but fallible human logic.</note> and (as David says of him) *our guide with whom we have taken so much sweet counsel* in all our worldly and political affairs. In those matters only should we make use of its advice. But we too often *take it into the Sanctuary* with us, *and walk in company with it into the house of God* (to allude to what David says there in Psalm 55:13–14). That is, we allow it to meddle in matters that pertain to the Sanctuary, and to debate and conclude about our spiritual and eternal states (as well as our temporal ones). And what is worse, we are dogmatic in our reliance on its judgment <note>original: "opinionative of its judgement"</note> there. *I thought* (says *Asaph* in that previously mentioned Psalm) *to know this* (verse 16); that is, he thought he could have compre—

But more particularly: Carnal reason <term>carnal reason: natural human logic, unguided by the Holy Spirit</term> is a more desperate enemy to faith than any other principle in man. For until faith is created <term>wrought: produced or shaped</term>, reason is the supreme principle; but then faith deposes and subjects it. Afterward, faith often contradicts reason—indeed, it excludes it from its counsel as being unskillful in these matters. And so deep and desperate is this enmity against faith, that whatever is the most special work and business of faith (which is to change our status <note>original: "estates"</note> before God, to put us into a state of justification <term>justification: being declared righteous or "right" with God through faith</term>, and to assure us of it), in that very thing reason shows a more peculiar enmity against faith by opposing it in that work more than in any other. <meta>This page continues the discussion on how "carnal reason"—human logic independent of God—acts as a persistent enemy to a believer's faith. It specifically describes how reason shifts its tactics from overconfidence before conversion to deep despair and doubt after conversion.</meta>

# A Child of Light
<page-num>36</page-num>

<margin>Reasoning <note>original: "λογισμὸς" (logismos)</note></margin>
...uses its strength to persuade a person of the goodness of their spiritual condition even without faith. It does this to prevent the entrance of faith and to keep us from seeking it at all, suggesting that faith is not necessary to change our condition or to justify us; in this way, reason would keep faith out entirely. 

Therefore, in the *first working of faith*, the Holy Spirit brings faith in by force of arms, like a conqueror *casting down all those strongholds and reasonings* <note>original: "2 Cor. 10.4"; refers to 2 Corinthians 10:4: "For the weapons of our warfare are not carnal but mighty in God for pulling down strongholds."</note> which carnal reason <term>carnal reason: natural human logic, unguided by the Holy Spirit</term> had been building and fortifying for a long time. In this way, He erects a throne for faith upon the ruins of them all. 

In the same manner, *after faith* is established, all that *carnal reason* which remains unsubdued continues to oppose faith. Out of a desire for revenge for its defeat, and with an even greater degree of hostility, it simply shifts the direction of the war. It gathers new forces and turns all its heavy artillery in the completely opposite direction. Now, it tries to persuade a person—through every objection it can raise—of the badness of their spiritual condition *now*, just as it previously tried to convince them of its goodness. 

By doing this, it seeks to insult the great work of faith in justifying us. Also, because next to *justifying us*, the duty and purpose of faith is to establish *peace with God* in our hearts and a conviction of being in His favor (as in Romans 5:1), carnal reason uses the full extent of its power and *sharpness* <note>original: "Acumen"</note> to persuade us at every opportunity. Using the most plausible and seemingly convincing arguments it can invent, it suggests that God is not at peace with us, nor yet reconciled to us. It does this merely to contradict faith on the primary point that faith seeks to convince us of.

So it is that, just as in people who are not yet born again, carnal...

<margin>reason</margin>

To conclude this point: if carnal reason ever has the upper hand and advantage over faith in any situation that happens to God's child, it is now—when they are in the *valley of the shadow of death*, as *David* speaks, and when they *walk in darkness and have no light*. This is a condition that provides the most complete grounds <note>original: "Topicks"; in classical rhetoric, "topics" were the places or themes one used to find arguments</note> for *carnal reason* to frame objections. 

In this state, regarding God's dealings with the person, there seems to be a gathering of all bad signs threatening ruin and destruction. Faith is under such a great eclipse that it is left to fight it out alone in the darkness without any supporter. On the contrary, *carnal reason* and our dark hearts (which are led by our physical senses) are possessed by a sense—the deepest and most intense sense—of that which the heart is most afraid of: God’s heavy wrath and displeasure. This is felt and argued not indirectly or from a distance (as a consequence of outward troubles), but immediately from God’s own hand. 

"You always suspected," says *carnal reason*, "that you were a child of wrath and that you and God were enemies. But now you find it placed beyond all question, and that from God's own mouth <margin>Jeremiah 31:20</margin>, who speaks painful things against you. You also have it in His own handwriting <margin>Job 13:26</margin>, for look, He *writes bitter things against you*—that is, in your conscience, as *Job* speaks—and *treats you as*

It is no wonder, then, if carnal reason is most busy at such a time, taking this advantage to frame and suggest the strongest objections to the soul while it is in this disordered state.

<margin>§. 4. A fourth principle: there are corrupt affections of jealousy, suspicion, and incredulity <term>incredulity: an inability or unwillingness to believe something</term>, which join with carnal reason in this.</margin>
Add to all this a fourth point: just as there is such strength in corrupt reason which is opposite to faith, there are also many other principles of corrupt affections in the heart. These join and take part with carnal reason in all its opposition against faith; they set it to work and support it just as much in persuading God’s children that their spiritual states <term>estates: a person's spiritual standing or condition before God</term> are worthless as they do in reassuring unregenerate <note>original: "unregenerate"; those who have not been spiritually reborn</note> men that their states are good. The hand of self-love—which bribes and biases carnal reason, especially when judging our own spiritual condition—is found to be just as active in the one as in the other; and this gives further light to the point at hand. For just as before faith is established, **self-flattery** (which is one branch of self-love) bribes and sets carnal reason to work to plead the goodness of their condition to unregenerate men, and causes them to accept all such false reasons which tend to persuade them to think well...

These grow along with the work of faith. These feelings sharpen the wit of carnal reason <term>carnal reason: human logic or wisdom that operates independently of God's Spirit</term> to argue and wrestle against the work of faith and grace that has begun. All the objections that carnal reason finds against faith are pleasing and believable to these corrupt principles, for they are nourished and strengthened by them.

<margin>The origin of these feelings and how they work in the heart.</margin>

<margin>
We are satisfied through the truth,
From which no lie did ever spring;
But all will surely be found liars,
Who dare to say we have no sin.

The reason why such *jealousies* and *suspicions*—which are the opposite of the self-flattery that previously influenced our opinions of our spiritual state—should arise and start up in the heart once faith is at work, and why they are more likely to prevail now after faith, is:

1. Because in the work of humiliation <note>humiliation: the spiritual process of being brought low by the realization of one's sin and need for God</note> (which prepares a person for faith), all those strongholds of carnal reason that upheld self-flattery and a false good opinion of one's state are demolished. Those towering thoughts of presumption are laid low, and a person is forever cured of having a high opinion of themselves by their own merits <note>original: "put out of conceit with himself, as of himself"</note>.

2. At that same time, the person was so thoroughly and deeply convinced of the heinousness of sin (which they previously ignored) and of the sheer number of their sins, that they are now inclined—instead of presuming safety as they did before—to be suspicious of God. They fear they might have provoked Him so much that He will never pardon them. Consequently, they are likely to misinterpret all of God's dealings with them to support that fear.

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

Because this same conviction has so clearly revealed and identified the infinite error and deceitfulness of his heart—which previously flattered him and judged his condition to be good when it was actually most accursed—he becomes exceedingly suspicious <note>original: "jealous"</note> and afraid of continuing to err in that same way. Therefore, he is prone to listen to every doubt and scruple that is suggested to him.

4. Especially since he is now made aware of the infinite danger to his eternal salvation that exists in nourishing a false opinion of the goodness of his state, should it prove to be otherwise. This is because such a false conceit <term>conceit: a favorable but false opinion or imagination of oneself</term> keeps a person from reaching saving faith. On the other hand, to cherish the opposite error—judging his state as bad when it is actually good—tends only to his present discomfort. Thus, he thinks it safer to err on the side of doubt than on the side of presumption.

5. Also, being now sensible of the transcendent importance of his eternal salvation (which he previously neglected), this awakens suspicion. (Suspicion is always doubting and inquisitive in all matters of great consequence and importance.) This awareness keeps suspicion awake, whereas before it lay sleeping. All these feelings, once startled and stirred up, do more than just provoke carnal reason <term>carnal reason: human logic or wisdom that operates independently of God's Spirit</term> to restlessly pry into everything that might suggest God's disfavor or the unsoundness of our hearts; they also welcome and applaud every objection that is found, and lead the person to jump too hastily to false conclusions from them.
