As we are dark and weak creatures, we are guilty creatures as well. This guilt—like the waves of the sea or the swelling of the Jordan River—begins to rise, swell, and overflow in our consciences during these terrible storms from God. This is seen in the example of *David* in *Psalm* 38. When God's wrath was heavy upon him (verses 1–2), he also complained, "*My iniquities have gone over my head*" (verse 4). There is much deceit <note>original: "guile"</note> and falsehood of heart which—during these times of distress <term>distempers: periods of spiritual or emotional disorder</term> when our consciences boil within us and are stirred and heated to the bottom—comes to the surface and floats on top like scum. 

Thus, when *David* was under God's discipline <term>under the rod: a metaphor for divine punishment or correction</term> for his sin of murder, both the guilt of his sin and the deceit of his spirit rose up. He then called for "*Truth in the inward parts*" (*Psalm* 51:6). For just as *his sin* (verse 2), so also *his falsehood of heart was always before him*. With this in mind, he spoke these words in *Psalm* 32: "*Oh, blessed is the man in whose spirit there is no deceit, and to whom the Lord does not count sin*." He said this when God had charged him with the guilt of his sin and revealed to him the deceit of his spirit (verses 4–5). 

This deceit often appears so clearly that our consciences can hardly see anything else within us. It lies on the surface and hides our virtues <note>original: "graces"</note> from our view. Just as chaff comes to the top when wheat is tossed in a winnowing fan, so do our corruptions float in our consciences during these spiritual commotions and siftings <term>winnowings of spirit: a metaphor for trials that test the soul by separating the "wheat" of true faith from the "chaff" of sin</term>, while the virtues that are in us lie covered beneath them, out of

And indeed, there are in the best of us enough humors <term>humors: in early modern medicine, these were bodily fluids thought to influence temperament; here used metaphorically for internal inclinations or moods</term> which, if they are stirred and gathered together in our consciences, may by themselves cast us into these burning fits of trouble and distress. Thus, while **God’s Spirit** withholds from us the light of our own graces <term>graces: the spiritual virtues or evidence of God's work in the soul</term>, and our own consciences represent to us the guilt and corruptions present in our best actions <note>original: "performances"</note>, our hearts may conclude that we are hypocrites. Indeed, it can happen that even *our own consciences* (which are the only faculty now left in us that should support and encourage *faith*, and testify to us—as is its duty—the goodness of our spiritual state <note>original: "estates"</note>) may join with those former corruptions against us, bringing in false evidence and pronouncing a false judgment. Even conscience itself, which is intended to serve like the urine of the body to show the condition <note>original: "estate"</note> of the whole person <note>In early modern medicine, uroscopy—examining urine—was a primary method for diagnosing internal diseases.</note> (and is therefore called *good* or *evil* depending on the person's state), is prone in such distress <term>distempers: periods of spiritual or emotional illness</term> to change and turn color. To a man’s own view, it may look as foul as the state of a *complete hypocrite*.

<margin>The reason.</margin>
And the reason for this is as obvious as the experience of it. It is because *conscience* remains partly defiled even in a man who is regenerate <term>regenerate: spiritually reborn or converted</term>. And though we are *cleansed* <note>original: "sprinkled"</note> *from an evil conscience* in part, it is not yet done completely. So, although *our persons* are fully discharged from the guilt of our sins before **God** *through the sprinkling of Christ’s blood*, the application <note>original: "sprinkling"</note> of that blood upon our consciences...

Our ability to understand this is imperfect. The reason is that this very "sprinkling of conscience" <note>A reference to Hebrews 10:22; the internal assurance of being cleansed from sin through Christ.</note>—by which it testifies to the sprinkling of Christ's blood and our justification through it—is only a part of the sanctification of the conscience. As a faculty, its office and duty is to testify and bear witness to our spiritual state <note>original: "estates"</note>. Therefore, just as the sanctification of all other faculties is imperfect, the conscience is also imperfect in this regard. 

Because of this, when God’s Spirit stops witnessing along with our conscience regarding the goodness of our state, and ceases to embolden and encourage the conscience by His presence and the sprinkling of Christ's blood upon it against remaining defilement, our consciences are then just as likely to fall into fears, doubts, and self-condemnation as our other faculties are to fall into any other sin when He withdraws the assistance of His grace. Therefore, just as the *law of sin* in our other members <note>A reference to Romans 7:23, referring to the parts of the human nature that resist God.</note> may rise up in arms and prevail so far as to lead us captive to sin, the guilt of sin in our partially defiled consciences may, for the same reason, prevail against us. It can gain the upper hand, lead us captive to fears and doubtings, and cast us into bondage.

*The third efficient cause: Satan. His special malice in this temptation, his commission, and his access to and advantage over us in this temptation because of the darkness within us.*

# A Child of Light

But in this matter, believers do not struggle only with "flesh and blood" <note>A biblical phrase referring to human nature and its inherent weaknesses.</note> and its darkness. They also conflict with those *spiritual wickednesses*—the Princes of darkness (Ephesians 6:12)—concerning their interest in those heavenly privileges. The phrase used there, *in the heavenly places* <note>original: "ἐν τοῖς ἐπουρανίοις"</note>, may be well interpreted as contending even with Satan and his angels. The Apostle compares Satan to a *roaring lion that seeks whom he may devour* (1 Peter 5:8). Just as when *God creates natural darkness and it is night, then the young lions creep forth and roar after their prey*, as the Psalmist says (Psalm 104:20–21), so do these roaring lions act. When God has withdrawn the light of His presence <note>original: "countenance"</note> and night comes on, and those damp fogs of jealousy and guilt begin to rise out of a person’s own heart, then these spirits come forth. They say, as David's enemies said during his distress, *“Come, let us take him now, for God has forsaken him.”* Let us now devour him and swallow him up with sorrow and despair.

And as God says of those enemies of His Church in Zechariah 1:15, *“I was only a little displeased, and they helped forward the affliction,”* so it is here. When God is angry with His child, even if only *a little*, and hides His face *but for a moment*, Satan watches for that *hour of darkness* (as Christ calls it in Luke 22:53). He joins his power of darkness to our natural human darkness to cause—if possible—a "blackness of darkness," even utter despair in us.

He tempted *Peter* to deny his Master; as Jesus said, *"Satan desires to sift you like wheat,"* <note>original: "winnow you"; a metaphor for a violent shaking or testing of one's faith.</note> but he had a further goal: a design against Peter's *faith*. Christ foresaw this and therefore primarily focused His prayer against it, saying, *"But I have prayed that your faith does not fail."* Satan hoped that through that gross sin, he might have drawn him into despair.

We may likewise observe how he placed this temptation at the forefront of those three assaults he made upon Christ. Christ is made a complete example for us, both in His obedience and in His temptations; for He was tempted *in all things*—that is, with all sorts of temptations—and also *like us* in the way it was done, *only without sin* (Hebrews 4:15).

Now, he tempted Christ not only to vain hopes (when he showed Him the glory of the whole world) and to presumption (by telling Him to throw Himself down headlong from an unauthorized height <note>original: "unwarrantable ground"</note>), but first and primarily to jealousies and distrusts between Him and His Father, and between His human nature and the Divine. For when Christ had just received that testimony from all three persons of the Trinity—the Father proclaiming Him to be His *Son* from heaven, and the Spirit descending on Him at His baptism (it being the special grace and purpose <note>original: "institution"</note> of that religious rite to seal the assurance of adoption and rebirth <note>original: "regeneration"</note>)—then comes Satan and tempts Him to question that voice, [suggesting that it might be but a delusi-

# A Child of Light

...jealousy; and since Christ’s human nature had not yet performed any outward miracle (as seen in John 2:11), Satan wanted Him to take the opportunity of His extreme hunger to turn stones into bread. This was to test whether He truly was the Son of God and hypostatically united <note>The "hypostatic union" refers to the theological doctrine that the divine and human natures of Christ are united in one single person.</note> to the second person of the Trinity. If God would not do this for Him, Satan hoped Christ would question His status as God's Son and believe it all to be a delusion. This was the meaning of the temptation: *“If you are the Son of God, command these stones to be made bread,”* etc. In doing so, he was hinting that since God left Him without daily bread—which even “evil parents” give to their children rather than a stone instead of bread—it might justify an [if], or a doubt, about whether He really was the Son of God or not.

<margin>
Reason 1.
</margin>
The reasons for this are: 1. Of all the spiritual graces within us, Satan is the greatest enemy to faith. Therefore, in 1 Thessalonians 3:5, the Apostle Paul was more concerned about Satan's influence on faith than anything else, fearing he had been tampering with and perverting their belief. He wrote, *“I sent to find out about your faith, fearing that the tempter had somehow tempted you.”* Faith in God is Satan's greatest enemy; it “extinguishes all his flaming arrows” (Ephesians 6:16). By standing firm in faith, we resist him so that *“he flees from us”* (1 Peter 5:9).

Romans 15:13.

Regarding faith, he is especially envious of the *joy* our faith brings. Just as providing comfort is the primary and most pleasing work of the Holy Spirit, so causing discomfort and distress is the primary work of this evil spirit.

<margin>
Reason 2.

Secondly, because he is most opposed to the Holy Spirit, he delights in blaspheming the Spirit's work in our hearts.

<margin>3.</margin>
3. He is called the "enemy" <note>original: ἐχθρός (echthros)</note>, that *envious one*, and the main object and target of his envy is this: *That God should be our God*, after God has rejected him. Therefore, when he sees he cannot truly separate God and us, he will endeavor to create and raise up jealousies—suspicions that God is not our God—within our own minds. He endeavored to raise jealousies between God and our first parents: *“God knows you shall be as gods, etc.”* (Genesis 3:5). It was as if God had forbidden them that fruit out of an envy toward them, to keep them from a better condition. He attempted the same thing between Christ’s human nature and the divine, even though they were hypostatically united <note>The "hypostatic union" is the theological doctrine that the divine and human natures of Christ are joined in one person.</note>.

And likewise, 4. [*“That God has given us eternal life, and that life is in His Son”*]—this being that great truth of the Gospel. Indeed, a Christian who *does not believe it makes God a liar* (1 John 5:10–11). Therefore, Satan, being that great *liar*, opposes this great truth and our faith in it above all others. His envy at the advancement of our human nature in Christ (according to that truth) is thought by some to have been the cause of his fall and ruin; this is how some understand the passage in <margin>John 8:44.</margin> *John 8*, where it says he *did not remain in the truth*. Regardless, he now delights in making God seem like a liar to us in our perceptions by questioning His promises. He especially tries to force this persuasion on us by misinterpreting God’s own dealings with us, *perverting His righteous ways*.

God may and does give up His child into Satan's hands, and permit him thus to tempt him.

Once permission is given, we hear **Job** (although he was never brought to question his spiritual standing <note>original: "estate"</note>, yet) crying out from terrors and from the sins of his youth; for **Satan** then, just as he struck Job's body with boils, so he buffeted his spirit. And though **Satan has** the will of himself, a desire for it, and enough **natural** <note>original: "physicall"</note> power and ability to inflict this at all times, he must further have **legal** <note>original: "moral"</note> power, or leave and a commission from God. God sometimes gives to Satan power over the sons and daughters of **Abraham** (Luke 13), even as well as others; and just as their bodies are to be vexed by him, so are their spirits. And as he is permitted to provoke them toward sin, so he is permitted much more to terrify them for sin, there being more of punishment than of sin in that experience. 

Thus, God left **David** to Satan, to provoke him into sin, as well as **Judas**. Therefore, that provocation to count <note>original: "number"</note> the people, as it is attributed to Satan and his malice in 1 Chronicles 21:1, is also attributed to God and His anger in giving leave first to Satan in 2 Samuel 24:1. And as an evil spirit from the Lord troubled **Saul’s** mind (1 Samuel 16:14), so a messenger of Satan was sent to buffet **Paul’s** spirit (2 Corinthians 12). In this, God does not help Satan with any additional power beyond what He furnished him with as an angel at his creation, nor with any assistance or information regarding our secret sins to enable him to better assault us (this I do not find in Scripture), but only gives **permissive power**.

We might say that, just as he could do nothing unless he had power from above (that is, from God), he could also not trouble us unless he had help from beneath—specifically, from those principles of guilt and darkness within us that were mentioned before. *Satan comes* (says *Christ*), *but has nothing in me* <note>original: John 14:30</note>. How could he have such near and inward access to our own spirits, if not for the darkness within us? It is because of this darkness that he is able to come inside us; for just as darkness mingles with darkness, so he mingles with our spirits. Therefore, just as the light of grace beginning within us prepares us for God to draw near to us, so this remaining, unexpelled darkness exposes us to Satan’s approach—drawing so near as to mingle with our spirits and, in a sense, become one spirit with us.

2. Since he has the advantage of access to get inside us, this internal darkness is also like fuel or tinder for his fiery temptations, which quickly ignite and flame up. Because darkness is his dominion, the more darkness there is in us, the larger the foothold he has to work with. 

Therefore, all the effects he produces in unregenerate <note>term for those who have not experienced spiritual rebirth or conversion</note> people—who are composed of nothing but darkness—he may also produce in regenerate <note>term for believers who have been spiritually reborn</note> people to a certain degree and for a limited time, in proportion to the darkness remaining in them. This includes deluding their reason, falsely accusing them, and terrifying their consciences, and so on. 

The Apostle excludes only final despair and revenge against God, which is the "sin leading to death" <note>original: "sin unto death"; often interpreted as the unforgivable sin or total apostasy</note>. Having mentioned that sin in 1 John 5:16, he adds in verse 18 <note>the original text cites verse 17, but this quote appears in verse 18 in modern Bibles</note> that *he who is born of God does not sin*—meaning he does not commit *that* specific sin. He further adds: *But he keeps himself so that the evil one does not touch him.* This means Satan cannot infect the believer’s spirit with even the slightest drop of the venom of that sin, which is specifically "his sin" <note>original: "his sin, John 8:44," referring to the devil being a murderer and a liar from the beginning</note> and which he uses to infect those who become the "Serpent's seed." 

Therefore, any instances we find that show how he has worked on the spirits of carnal <note>original: "carnal," meaning worldly or unspiritual</note> people because of their total darkness can be used to show, by comparison, what he may also work in believers for a season, because of the darkness that partly remains in them. *All things happen alike to all.* <note>original: "All things hapning alike to all," a reference to Ecclesiastes 9:2</note> This is the general rule.

## CHAPTER VII.
### *More specifically, how Satan works upon those three principles within us: First, on carnal reason.*

---

Since the exercise of his power lies in the darkness within us, let us look more specifically at how able and powerful he is to work upon those several principles of *carnal reason, guilt of conscience, jealousies, and fears.*

First, let us look at *carnal reason* <note>original: "carnall reason"; refers to human logic and intellect when it is influenced by worldly impulses or sinful nature rather than divine wisdom</note>. This is the primary area he works upon in this kind of temptation. Its strength lies in false reasonings, and it is here, if anywhere, that he has the greatest advantage.

<margin>
§. 1.
Satan's abilities to invent false reasonings.
</margin>
First, his ability to forge and invent false reasonings and arguments to overthrow our faith must be understood as exceedingly great. Because of his vast knowledge, he is called *Daimon* <note>original Greek: Δαίμων; meaning "the knowing one" or "learned," from which the word "demon" is derived</note>, just as he is called *Satan* for his malice. For his cunning in outsmarting us, he is called a *Serpent*. Even when he was "young" (at the beginning of the world), he outwitted our first parents. The Apostle says he beguiled *Eve* through subtlety (2 Corinthians 11:3) even then, when their reason was not yet corrupted. 

<margin>
Increased by such a long time of experience,
</margin>
But now he has grown into that *old Serpent* (Revelation 17:9 <note>While the text cites Revelation 17:9, the "old serpent" is famously identified in Revelation 12:9 and 20:2</note>), while *we have become like children, easily tossed to and fro* (Ephesians 4:14). He has had more than enough time to perfect his knowledge; he is a student of 5,000 years' standing who has lost no time. Just as it is said that he *accuses day and night* (Revelation 12:10), he is able to study both day and night. He has made it his chief—if not his entire—study to equip himself to tempt us and to plead against us...

...is his trade. Therefore, just as men are called *lawyers* or *theologians* <note>original: "Divines"; refers to those skilled in divinity or theology</note> because of their professions, so he is called "the tempter" and "the accuser" because of his work. Through this long experience and observation, he has his *designs* <note>original Greek: νοήματα (noēmata); refers to thoughts, purposes, or schemes. See 2 Corinthians 2:11</note>—his fixed and composed machinations; his *schemes* <note>original Greek: μεθοδεία (methodeia); refers to craftiness or systematic deception. See Ephesians 6:11</note>—his methods of temptation, which are studied, artificially molded, and organized. He even has systems and methods for these, much like tutors and professors of arts and sciences have and read over and over again to their students <note>original: "Auditors"</note>. The Apostle calls them *darts* (verse 16), and he has a whole shop and armory of them ready-made and forged. Because of the sharpness and subtle sophistry <note>original: "subtill sophistry"; refers to clever but deceptive reasoning</note> within them, they are called the *depths of Satan* (Revelation 2:24).

<margin>
and his continual exercise in this great controversy through all ages:
</margin>
If these *depths* are to be found in any area, they are most present in this: he is more especially skilled in this great question and dispute—whether a man is the child of God or not—than in any other. He has dealt with all other controversies only in particular eras as they were occasionally started; but this has been the standing controversy of all ages since God has had any children on earth. With nearly every one of them, he has had formal disputes about it at one time or another. Thus, he knows all the advantages, windings, and turnings in this debate, along with all the objections, answers, and discussions involved in it. And just as other controversies become larger, more refined, and more subtle the longer they persist, so must this one—especially in this current, knowledgeable age of the world—and also because of that seemingly close resemblance <note>original: "neare similitude"</note> which hypocrisy...

<margin>
This is the most subtle and intricate of all controversies.
</margin>
...which pertains to the truth and power of grace (a subject that has puzzled <note>original: "fazled"</note> and entangled this controversy). The objections and difficulties a believer encounters while trying to determine the true state of their soul <note>original: "estate"</note> are greater than in any controversy the world has ever known. They present more unusual problems <note>original: "stranger knots"</note> and require as sharp logical distinctions to resolve them as any known to the academic Schools <note>original: "the School"; referring to the complex logical systems of Scholastic theology</note>. Indeed, these problems are such that if the Holy Spirit did not sometimes cut and sometimes untie them for believers—by testifying with our spirits that we are the children <note>original: "sons"</note> of God—pure reason alone could never resolve them. Now, through long experience and observation, *Satan* has all of these at his fingertips; he organized them into systematic categories <note>original: "common places"; referring to the "commonplace books" scholars used to organize arguments and information</note> long ago. He has constantly observed and recorded which answers have comforted the spirits of believers when he cast certain doubts into their minds; he then prepares a further rebuttal for the next time, or for the next believer he encounters.

<margin>
§. 2.
Satan knows how best to suit his false reasonings to all sorts of believers.
</margin>
Secondly, just as he has thoroughly studied this controversy and knows all its twists and false reasonings, he also knows how to best apply those arguments to specific people and specific times by daily *studying* and *observing people*. It is the primary business of these evil spirits to study human beings; to this end, they travel *back and forth across the earth*. Satan has categorized types of people and their various temperaments and spiritual states, just as he has categorized temptations. He knows all the different ranks and classes of people within the "state of grace" and knows which sort of temptations to use against them based on their status. For people's temptations are various and manifold (see *1 Peter 1:6* <note>original citation: "1 Pet. 2. 6."; however, 1 Peter 1:6 discusses "manifold temptations," which fits the author's context</note>), just as the gifts...

...and the operations of the Spirit are (1 Corinthians 12:4–5). Having thoroughly debated this issue <note>original: "beaten out this controversy"</note> with people of all kinds, he knows how to frame the dispute. He knows how to arrange, organize, and apply objections, wielding his attacks with the greatest success and advantage. Just as physicians observe how various medicines work on different ages and physical constitutions, and the different results they produce, they prescribe specific treatments based on the unique conditions of their patients, even if they suffer from the same disease. In the same way, through observation, Satan finds that some men's hearts reflect others' "as face reflects face in water," as Solomon says (Proverbs 27:19). Furthermore, he remembers which arguments have always been most effective with specific types or "strains" of Christians—those whose flaws <note>original: "corruptions"</note> and virtues <note>original: "graces"</note> were similar to the person he is currently dealing with. He then applies those same arguments once again.

Some have experienced a false or superficial religious experience <note>original: "counterfeit work"</note> before their true conversion; others were never spiritually enlightened until they were actually saved. # A Child of Light

<margin><unclear>Christians</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear></margin>

...provides a starting point and opportunity for several temptations. In this way, the specific spiritual experience one Christian has had can easily become a reason for another who lacks it to feel excluded. 

"I was never humbled like that," says one. 

"Nor was I comforted like that," says another. 

"I had a sudden, powerful experience indeed, which came in like a spring tide, but now the tide has receded and my first love has faded," says a third. 

"I had some spiritual insights and enlightenments in the past," says another, "but I was deceived then, and I might be deceived now as well." 

Thus, a person is given the massive task of trying to distinguish a counterfeit experience from a true one. In this way, every different method of God’s working is vulnerable to various objections. Just as we say that every earthly profession <note>original: "calling"</note> has its own specific and proper temptations, so too do the different ways and manners of bringing about this heavenly calling have their own distinct patterns and currents of temptation. Satan knows all of this and has tracked it often. Consequently, he knows how to tailor these temptations to specific individuals and pursue them in the most effective <note>original: "advantagious"</note> way.

Similarly, he takes the measure <note>original: "takes the compasse"</note> of every person’s knowledge, ideas, and understandings. Depending on whether our knowledge is great or small, we are susceptible to different kinds of temptations. Many arguments and objections—which, like small buckshot <note>original: "haile-shot"</note>, could not reach or make any dent at all in people of great ability and learning—fail to affect them. This is both because their knowledge allows them to soar high above the range of such attacks, and because they wear "the whole armor of God," as the Apostle says in *Ephesians 6*. That is, they are in complete armor, abounding in all faith and knowledge. Yet those same arguments might be the most effective ones to aim at those who are more ignorant, and...

...spiritual <note>Completing the word "spiritual" from the previous page.</note>) which he forges and invents, and does so in such a way as to deceive us by them and make us accept them.

<margin>
1. To suggest even the most subtle and abstract reasonings about spiritual matters.
</margin>

First, he is able not only to put into the heart suggestions and temptations toward sensual and worldly objects. Examples of this include the suggestion put into *Judas's* heart to betray his Master for money (*John 13:2*), and his tempting of married couples who are apart to lack self-control <note>original: "incontinency"</note> (*1 Cor. 7:5*). But he can also insinuate and share the most subtle and abstract reasonings concerning spiritual things, which are completely beyond our physical senses. He does this according to each person's level of understanding. 

Therefore, we are said to wrestle with them about heavenly things, and our interest in those things is often the point of conflict and the central question. For instance, consider the phrase in *Ephesians 6:12*, "in the heavenly" <note>original Greek: "ἐν τοῖς ἐπουρανίοις" (*en tois epouraniois*)</note>. When it says, "*We wrestle with spiritual wickedness in heavenly*," it should be understood as "concerning heavenly things" rather than "in heavenly places." The word signifies something "above the heavens" <note>original: "supercelestiall"</note>—the highest heavens—where (if the word referred to places) the devils have never gone since their fall. 

Furthermore, the word is used elsewhere to mean "heavenly things," as in *Hebrews 8:5*. The preposition "in" <note>original Greek: "ἐν" (*en*)</note> is also sometimes used to express the subject matter that a thing is concerned with <note>original: "conversant"</note>. For example, in *Matthew 11:6*, "*Blessed is he that is not offended in me*" means "offended *with* or *about* me, and for my sake." It may be appropriately understood in the same way here. This indicates to us that the prize—the stake for which we wrestle with Satan—is not worldly things like honors or riches, but *heavenly* things, which concern our souls and our future state <note>original: "estates"</note>. This happened because of the folly and darkness remaining in them, as the apostle implies when he says: *O foolish Galatians, who has bewitched you, that you should not obey the truth?* (Galatians 3:1). And if believers were for a time deluded regarding the very doctrine of justification itself (which is rare), then they may much more easily—and often are—misled in the application of faith, specifically in believing in their own personal justification, which is the point we are discussing.

And yet, the language of Scripture goes quite far in ascribing this to Satan when it says of those who did not believe the Gospel that *the god of this world has blinded the minds of those who do not believe* (2 Corinthians 4:4). This indicates a blindness added on top of their own natural blindness. This is also seen when it says that the *Prince of the air* is *at work* <note>original: "ἐνεργῶν" (energōn), meaning working effectively</note> in the children of disobedience (Ephesians 2:2). It also mentions the **Corinthians** while they were still unregenerate <note>term: unregenerate: Not yet spiritually reborn or converted to Christianity.</note>, who were said *to be carried and led away after silent idols* (1 Corinthians 12:2). 

All these phrases suggest more than just a power to influence human judgment in the way one person might try to corrupt or persuade another through moral <note>term: moral way: Here meaning through human persuasion or external influence, rather than a direct spiritual or physical force.</note> means. This is because Satan suggests thoughts in a way that cannot be easily detected <note>original: "indiscernibly"</note>, and with greater frequency and persistence. He holds the mind focused on the object of temptation and presents a whole "army" of supporting reasons all at once. He is able to arrange <note>original: "marshal"</note> them so effectively that the mind can scarcely resist, and he thrusts all of these upon the spirit with a violent and commanding force. Furthermore, these phrases seem to imply some kind of physical <note>term: physical: Here, the author means a direct, mechanical, or internal influence on the mind's workings, as opposed to mere external persuasion.</note> operation; though he does not work directly on the "spring of the clock" <note>An analogy for the human heart or the primary source of the will.</note>, yet he works upon the

Fourthly, he is further able to follow and continue his reasoning as the occasion arises. He can keep up the debate, present arguments against us, and even out-reason us by introducing new replies to our answers. In this way, he maintains, manages, and carries on the dispute, bringing in fresh reinforcements. In this respect, it is called *wrestling* in **Ephesians 6:12**: *We wrestle not against flesh and blood, but against principalities and powers.* Like physical wrestling, this is conducted through repeated assaults and attempts to overcome and gain the victory. He acts like a wrestler trying to trip us up—that is, to knock the reasoning that supported us out from under our feet by using deceptive <note>original: "cavilling"</note> objections. How often have we experienced these kinds of spiritual wrestling matches during times of spiritual agony? 

In the hour of temptation, believers find themselves in conflicts and back-and-forth debates that are carried out logically, where pertinent objections are raised against the very answers that the believer is secretly meditating upon. For this reason, theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> advise people not to *debate* with that cunning deceiver <note>original: "sophister"</note>. 

Similarly, many people facing death have found that the reasons they relied on for their spiritual state—and the evidence of grace they turned to for comfort—were taken away and refuted as quickly as they could think of them. It is further evident that Satan has the dexterity and skill to manage such disputes with us in his framing of...

Therefore, Satan surely must have some way, more or less, of guessing or getting an inkling of what the heart’s replies might be. If this were not the case, the glory that God receives through the victory won over Satan in these temptations would be greatly obscured, and Satan’s own confusion would be less. Once the devil senses and perceives this resistance, he is confounded. When he is once aware and realizes that he is being resisted, he *flees from us*, as the Apostle says in **James 4:7**. The expression there implies he leaves of his own accord, like a defeated and disgraced soldier.

# A Child of Light

Some [rules for judging our spiritual state] are too narrow, allowing no room for human failings; others are too broad, including such gross corruptions and their constant practice that they cannot exist alongside God's grace. Satan uses both to deceive: he leads profane people to flatter themselves into thinking they are in a good condition when they are not, and he leads those with weak and sensitive consciences to think they are not in a good state when they actually are. In the same way, misunderstood passages of Scripture often become a source of great temptation to many. For example, some look at **Hebrews 6** after losing their "first love" and conclude they can never be saved. This is because the passage says, "*For those who were once enlightened... if they fall away, it is impossible to renew them again to repentance*" <note>Hebrews 6:4–6</note>. However, this should only be understood as referring to a *vengeful and total apostasy* <note>original: "revengefull totall Apostasie"; a complete, deliberate, and malicious rejection of the faith, rather than a temporary lapse or struggle with sin.</note>.

<margin>**Acts 13:10**</margin>
Just as **Elymas** <note>Elymas was a sorcerer in the Book of Acts who tried to turn a Roman official away from the faith; the apostle Paul called him a "son of the devil" for his deception.</note> *perverted the straight paths of the Lord*, so does Satan. Elymas was called a *child of the devil* because he did his father's work in doing so. Now, all such false reasonings founded upon mistakes about these matters—and about the rule itself by which we should judge our spiritual condition—are "false majors" <note>In logic, a "major premise" is the general rule or principle used to reach a conclusion. Satan provides a "false major" by misinterpreting Scripture or the requirements of grace.</note>. These belong to the previous category of *carnal reason* <note>original: "Carnall reason"; human logic guided by sinful nature rather than spiritual truth.</note>. But Satan has another wing of forces to join with these: false accusations against a person regarding the guilt of their own heart and actions. These are misconceptions and misapplications of the facts to oneself—another kind of argument where the "minors" <note>In logic, a "minor premise" is the specific fact applied to the general rule. Even if the rule is right, Satan can lie about the person's own heart or motives to reach a false conclusion about their salvation.</note> are false. So, even if a person is full of knowledge and has a correct judgment of the Scriptures and the nature of grace—meaning Satan cannot deceive them regarding the rules...

<summary>This page explains how Satan uses both "false majors" (misinterpreting God's rules/Scripture) and "false minors" (lying about a person's specific heart and actions) to cause spiritual despair. It specifically notes how misinterpreting Hebrews 6 can lead struggling believers to fear they have committed an unpardonable apostasy.</summary>

# Walking in Darkness

...[Satan may not] be too difficult for him with all his deceptive reasoning <note>original: "sophistry"; the use of fallacious arguments, especially with the intention of deceiving.</note>. Yet by misrepresenting a man to himself, and by perverting his own ways to him—making that which is straight seem crooked, and making everything in him appear to be hypocrisy—a man is brought to pass a false sentence upon himself. 

Just as if this subtle lawyer (so to speak) cannot deceive the Judge with false rules and mistakes in the Law itself, he then endeavors to do so by misrepresenting the party's case. He submits a false bill of accusation, so arranged and disguised as to secure a judgment against him. He places before the eyes of people's consciences their hidden motives <note>original: "by-ends"; secret or selfish purposes.</note>, the deadness and hardness of their hearts, and the falseness in various turnings of their lives. He ignores what is good in them and exaggerates what is evil, all to force a false conclusion from those facts. To give an example of this latter kind of false reasoning: Satan often argues and charges the conscience of a distressed person in this manner, or something like it:

<margin>
This makes me bold before His throne,
Yes, bold to face my foes;
I flee to Christ; Lord, make me stand
By Your almighty grace.
Is Jesus my almighty King?
Does He forbid my fear?
Rightly, though foes should haunt my soul,
His righteousness I wear.
<note>These verses appear to be a handwritten addition of a hymn, likely by Isaac Watts or a similar 18th-century writer, emphasizing trust in Christ's righteousness over one's own failings.</note>
</margin>

> *Those in whom any sin reigns, or in whose hearts hypocrisy and self-love are the dominant principles, are not in the state of grace.*

<margin>
Jan 30
</margin>

*But you are such a person, etc.* To prove this <term>minor</term> <note>In logic, the "minor premise" is the second step of an argument that applies a general rule to a specific case.</note> premise, he gathers and displays before the conscience a multitude of instances: sins committed so heinously and so often; duties omitted; or if performed, done with such pride of heart and selfish aims <note>original: "self-eimes"</note>, and so on. In this kind of reasoning, the <term>major</term> <note>The "major premise" is the general rule or first part of a logical argument.</note> (the first proposition) is true; but the minor, the <term>assumption</term> (that "you are such a person"), is completely false. And although there is some truth in the instances alleged to prove it—namely, that such sins have indeed been committed, even during the performance of religious duties—[the conclusion remains false].

### A Child of Light

For some, the standard is too narrow, allowing no room for failings; for others, it is too broad, including such gross corruptions and their constant practice that they cannot coexist with grace. Satan deceives with both: he leads profane people to flatter themselves that they are in a good spiritual condition when they are not, and he leads those with weak and tender consciences to believe they are not in a good state when they actually are.

In the same way, misunderstood passages of Scripture often become a source of great temptation to many. For example, Hebrews 6 might trouble someone who, having lost their first love <note>original: "fallen from his first love"; a reference to Revelation 2:4, describing a decline in spiritual passion.</note>, concludes they can never be saved because the text says, *it is impossible for those who were once enlightened, if they fall away, to be renewed again to repentance.* However, this is only meant to describe a *vengeful and total apostasy* <term>apostasy</term><note>The total abandonment or rejection of one's religious faith.</note>. Thus, just as *Elymas perverted the right ways of the Lord* (<margin>Acts 13:10</margin>), so does Satan. Elymas was called a *child of the devil* because he did his father’s work in that regard.

All such false reasonings—founded on misunderstandings of the facts and of the rules by which we should judge our spiritual state (*false major premises* <term>major premise</term><note>In logic, a general statement that forms the first part of a syllogism.</note>)—properly belong to the previous category of *carnal reason*. 

But he has another wing of forces to join to these: false accusations against a person's own self based on the guilt of their heart and lifestyle, misconceptions about oneself, and misapplications of the truth. These are a different sort of argument where the *minor premises* <term>minor premise</term><note>In logic, a specific statement that relates the individual case to the general rule (major premise).</note> are false. Consequently, even if a person is full of knowledge and possesses a correct judgment of the Scriptures and the workings of grace—by which a person's state is to be judged—and so therein Satan cannot...

It distinguishes between errors in general spiritual principles (major premises) and errors in how individuals view their own lives (minor premises).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Satan as a "subtle pleader" who uses logical fallacies to trap the believer's conscience. While the previous page established that Satan misrepresents a person's case to secure a "guilty" verdict, this page focuses on how he misuses the "minor proposition" of a logical argument—the specific facts of a person's life—to force a false conclusion of hypocrisy.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

In one's religious duties, such particular ulterior motives <note>original: "by-ends"</note>, and so on, do arise and are found in the heart. However, they are not there in the manner he [Satan] would charge—not as *reigning*, not as the swaying and prevailing principle in a person's whole life. That hypocrisy is present cannot be denied; but that hypocrisy *rules* there and is predominant, and that there is *nothing but* hypocrisy—this is false. Yet Satan overwhelms <note>original: "amazeth"</note> the conscience with this, to draw the conclusion from it all: *Therefore, you are a hypocrite*. We shall soon show how able he is to enforce that conclusion with terrors and fears. 

What we are currently addressing is showing how capable he is of these kinds of false reasonings. The deceit of these lies chiefly in the *assumption* and the *minor proposition*—that is, in misapplying truths to a person's own self. <note>In a formal logical argument (a syllogism), the "minor proposition" is the specific case being tested against a general rule (the major proposition). For example: 1. Hypocrites go to hell (Major). 2. You are a hypocrite (Minor/Assumption). 3. Therefore, you go to hell (Conclusion). Satan attacks the second step.</note> In this, he primarily deals with the *conscience*. The *guilt* of a person's particular ways, actions, and corruptions (the seat of which is the *conscience*) is turned into evidence and used as proof for those *minor propositions*. The defilement and errors of the *conscience* are the principles within us that he works upon when he enforces such a misunderstanding based on that evidence.

<margin>A difference between Satan's sifting us and the Holy Spirit's searching</margin>
In this, we may notice in passing a difference between the Holy Spirit's dealing with a believer—when He comes with the Word to search and test the heart and reveal our corruptions—and Satan's methods. This is the kind of searching that *David* prayed for: 

> *Examine me, O Lord, and try my heart, etc.* (Psalm 26:2; Psalm 139:23). 

The Spirit convinces and reproves us, sometimes with sharpness, for our ulterior motives, hypocrisies, and so on, when also...

<summary>This page explains how Satan uses the presence of imperfect motives to falsely claim that hypocrisy is the "reigning" principle of a believer's life. It distinguishes between Satan's accusatory "sifting" of the conscience and the Holy Spirit's redemptive "searching" of the heart.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Satan exploits a believer's conscience. While the Holy Spirit convicts a person of sin to bring about restoration, Satan uses the same facts to drive the soul toward the false conclusion that they are an unredeemed hypocrite.</meta>

<header>Walking in Darkness.</header>

<margin>Job 36:9, 10.</margin>
God opens the ear and shows us where we have overstepped, as Elihu says <note>Job 36:9 states: "Then he shows them their work, and their transgressions that they have exceeded."</note>. There is a difference between this and those other "siftings and winnowings" of Satan (as Christ phrases it in Luke 22:31). The Holy Spirit deals kindly in this, like a father who rebukes and convicts his child of misbehavior, but without adding a stinging conclusion that we are therefore hypocrites. The Spirit's purpose is not to suggest that sin reigns in us. However, in Satan's attacks, that is the very goal he drives toward. It is the refrain and the burden of all his accusations; it is the primary focus and argument that runs through his entire charge against us.

In spite of Satan or of sin.

Until you win His glorious kingdom.

<margin>
Section 1.
How Satan acts as an accuser by charging the guilt of sin upon the conscience. <term>Accuser</term> <note>original Greek: κατήγορος (katēgoros)</note>.
</margin>
Because he misrepresents our spiritual state and falsely exaggerates our sins to us, he is called "the Tempter" in general relation to all kinds of temptations. He is also called "the Accuser" (Revelation 12:10) or <term>empleader</term> <note>An "empleader" is an old legal term for someone who brings a formal lawsuit or legal charge against another in court.</note> against us. Just as he is the accuser who speaks against us to God in God’s court and before His tribunal (for the word "accuse" seems to imply a legal court), he also accuses us in the court of our own consciences. And just as he tempts us *into* sin, he also tempts us *for* sin and *by* sin—that is, he uses the guilt of it to draw us into despair. He who accused Job to God would surely accuse Job to himself even more.

<margin>A Caution</margin>
It can be truly said that neither Satan nor our own consciences can ever exaggerate the inherent sinfulness, the heinousness, and the vileness of our sins too much. We can never see them in their true colors or accurate severity enough to hate, loathe, and mourn for them as much as we ought to. Yet...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Satan and the conscience interact to overwhelm a believer. It specifically contrasts the malicious work of Satan as an "accuser" with the restorative work of the Holy Spirit as a "comforter."</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

<margin>
Comfort for Mr. Par <note>Likely a reference to a specific person the author was counseling, possibly "Mr. Parsons."</note> for a [distressed person]
</margin>

Satan and our own consciences, when presenting our sins to us, may attach such false understandings and such exaggerations to them that they cause us to worry excessively. For instance, he might suggest that these sins are incompatible with a state of grace, or that they are completely unpardonable. He may also use these sins as logical proofs to support a false conclusion about our salvation. 

Although our sins can never be too clearly presented if the goal is to drive a person further toward God’s free grace and toward Christ, presenting them in isolation and forcing the mind to focus only on them causes us to forget *our own mercies* <note>A reference to Jonah 2:8: "They that observe lying vanities forsake their own mercy."</note>. When Satan acts in this way, God's mercies and all comforts are hidden and concealed from us; this is Satan’s practice, and it is the cause of the deep spiritual bondage we are discussing here.

In this regard, the name <term>Accuser</term> <note>original Greek: κατήγορος (katēgoros)</note> is given to this evil spirit in direct and complete opposition to that special name and office of the Holy Spirit, the <term>Comforter</term> <note>original Greek: παράκλητος (paraklētos), often translated as Advocate or Counselor</note> or advocate who pleads for us. For just as the Holy Spirit intercedes in our own hearts *to God for us*, and—upon true repentance—helps us to offer a defense <note>original: "apologies"; in the 17th century, this meant a formal legal defense or "clearing of oneself," as in the Greek *apologia*.</note> for ourselves (as mentioned in 2 Corinthians 7:11), and comforts us by revealing *the graces given to us by God* (as in 1 Corinthians 2:12), and by presenting our evidence and witnessing with our spirits that we are the children of God; so, on the contrary, Satan is the <term>Accuser</term> <note>original Greek: κατήγορος (katēgoros)</note>. He acts by charging us with the guilt of our sins, by challenging our evidence of faith, and by misrepresenting our spiritual state to discourage us and *overwhelm us with sorrow*, as described in 2 Corinthians 2:7. 

Furthermore, because the goal of these accusations is—

<term>conscience</term>) is to misrepresent our spiritual condition to us and falsely disturb us. Therefore, he is even more specifically called the **Slanderer** <note>original Greek: διάβολος (diabolos)</note>, as one who falsely and lyingly slanders all our spiritual virtues, all of God’s dealings toward us, and all our dealings toward Him. He slanders our persons and our spiritual standing to us, accusing us of being hypocrites, unsound, worldly, and counterfeit Christians, always interpreting everything in the worst possible way. 

I believe these false slanders and charges of his are most accurately described as those "darts" mentioned in *Ephesians 6:11* <note>While the author cites verse 11, the "fiery darts" are famously mentioned in Ephesians 6:16</note>, which are said to especially oppose our faith; this is why *faith* is said to quench them. He earns his name, **Slanderer** <note>original Greek: διάβολος (diabolos)</note>, from this trade of forging darts of slander; the name comes from a word meaning **to strike through** <note>original Greek: διαβάλλω (diaballo)</note>. It is a metaphor for casting darts, for the slandering lies of the tongue *are like a heavy hammer, a sword, and a sharp arrow*, as Solomon says: *their teeth are spears and arrows*. 

Such are these kinds of Satanic temptations and accusations against us—even like darts and arrows that wound, pierce, and run through our passions and emotions, striking the soul through and through with fears. His name, **the Tempter** <note>original Greek: πειράζων (peirazōn)</note>, comes from a root meaning **to pierce** <note>original Greek: πείρω (peirō)</note>, because his darts are so sharpened and flung with such force that they are designed to pierce and run right through a person. Besides the sharpness of the darts themselves, they are said to be "fiery," creating a double path for themselves; for a piece of iron, even if blunt, will run through anything without resistance if it is fired red-hot.

conscience) he has some command over, as well as over the power of sin in other parts of our being. Therefore, just as he can muster up and incite fleshly lusts that fight against the soul, and provoke and support them in their assaults upon us, so too can he clap the chains of guilt and bondage upon our consciences.

<margin>
He also works by exploiting the lack of judgment in the conscience.
</margin>

And as he can stir up the guilt that is already within us, he also works upon the lack of judgment and the erroneous corruption that exists in the conscience when it tries to judge a person's own spiritual state. Satan works upon and abuses this. For just as he has the power to influence the corruption in the rest of our faculties, he also has power over the defilement and pollution of the conscience. He misleads it in its verdict of our condition, much like cunning lawyers mislead a simple jury. The mechanisms <note>original: "wards" – the internal parts of a lock that prevent the wrong key from turning</note> of the conscience are loose and naturally misplaced on their own, but he twists and perverts them even more with his false keys. The conscience naturally gives an uncertain sound, but he confuses its testimony much more by sounding false alarms and instilling panic. 

And how easy is it to trouble a soul that is already disquieted, and to exploit insecurities that have already been raised? We see how far a manipulative person can insinuate themselves with jealous natures to increase suspicions and doubts. When an "humor" <note>referring to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—once thought to determine a person's temperament and health</note> is stirred, how easily it is manipulated! Thus, when the Holy Spirit has already given us a sharp rebuke and examined our consciences, Satan often strikes in and elaborates <note>original: "descants" – a musical term for adding a counterpoint or melody above a simple theme; here it means adding further complex accusations to the Spirit's simple conviction</note> upon it all, leading to deeper terror and distress.

[He has the] sword of his word ready, drawn and brandished—that word by which he will judge people at the last day (John 12:48) and which is therefore called *kritikos* <note>original Greek: κριτικὸς — meaning a "discerner" or "judge"</note> (Hebrews 4:12), a judge of the thoughts, etc. He does this so that by its awesome terror, he might compel and drive those who hear the Gospel to *enter into that rest* (to which he had exhorted them in verse 11), which he has opened up for fallen people to enter.

Just as this sword has a double edge (as described there), so in his hand—and he alone can wield it—it serves two purposes. In a judge, two things are necessary to fully perform the office: 
1. The skill and knowledge to discover and examine the facts.
2. The power to punish and execute the sentence upon the wrongdoer once found guilty.

He shows how both of these qualities meet perfectly and exclusively in him by describing the power found in his *word*. This word is the emblem <note>original: "ensigne"</note> of his justice and the instrument of his power in judgment. It is described as a "discerner of the thoughts" and a sword that pierces and wounds the soul and spirit with unspeakable anguish. 

Some argue that this wounding power of the word is specifically described from the beginning of verse 12 up to the words "and is a discerner of the thoughts." They believe that from that point until the end of verse 13, the other quality—the searching and all-judging nature of God and his word—is explained to us. 

However, I believe that the Apostle, using one continuous metaphor, describes both qualities throughout the whole passage. While the first quality is more prominent in the first part and the second quality in the latter part, both are presented as the *Royalty of God* <note>This refers to God’s "Royal Prerogative" to know the heart, mentioned on the previous page.</note>, which is the subject we currently have in hand.

Neither should it cause anyone to stumble that this power is attributed only to the word of God, which he seems to speak of alone. For that is the same as ascribing it to God; for just as Solomon says in *Ecclesiastes 8:4, "Where the word of a King is, there is power,"* so where the word of God is, there is the power of God, and that is how it is to be understood here. Therefore, just as in other Scriptures his word is said to *create*, and *by it the heavens were established, etc.,* and also in *Galatians 3:8*, using a similar figure of speech, the Scripture is said to *foresee* (meaning God foresaw, who wrote the Scripture), so also here, the word is said to *know* and *wound* the heart. That this was the Apostle’s express intention here appears by the connection of verses 12 and 13. For whereas in verse 12 he begins by attributing this power to the word, yet in the end he closes his speech by transferring everything that was said about it upon God himself in verse 13: *with whom we have to do.*

To open the words a little more extensively, so as to clarify this assertion from them (which is necessary to establish first), the words are:
> *For the word of God is living <note>original: "quick"</note> and powerful, and sharper than any two-edged sword, piercing to the dividing asunder of soul and spirit, and of the joints and marrow, and is a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart; neither is there any creature that is not manifest in his sight, but all things are naked and opened to the eyes of him with whom we have to do.*

And first, concerning that power of searching the soul alone in this chapter, and that other power—the exclusive wounding power of the conscience—we shall have a similar occasion to establish in the next chapter.

The judging power of the word currently under discussion, he expresses through an allusion to the anatomy of bodies (which at that time, though not as common as it is now, was already practiced); or else he refers to the cutting open of sacrifices, whether those of the Jews or those used among the pagans—especially by the fortune-tellers <note>original: "soothsayers"</note>, who carefully searched every internal organ, as we find in the Prophet Ezekiel 21:21. 

His comparison is this: just as entrails are to a sharp sword, a sacrificial knife, or similar anatomical instruments in a strong and skillful hand, so are all the most inward and secret parts of the heart—even those most difficult to separate—before this sword in God's hand when He chooses to use it to search the heart and mind <note>original: "reins," literally kidneys, used biblically to refer to the seat of emotions and intent</note>, and to reveal and bring to judgment its secrets. 

He can use this sword not only to rip open and strip off the outer clothing of outward and formal religious actions, and so present the soul naked (as he says in verse 13), nor only to flay <note>original: "flea"</note> off all the skin—to strip it away <note>original: "excoriate"</note> to see what lies underneath (as the Greek word *tetrachēlismena* <note>original: τετραχηλισμένα; literally meaning "laid bare" or "exposed by the neck"</note>, translated here as "opened," sometimes signifies)—but further, to cleave and cut right to the backbone (for the meaning of that word reaches even that deep), so that all the internal parts may appear. These are so precisely divided and laid open that one can see and examine each part individually. 

*"It pierces even to the division of soul and spirit."* By this phrase, "grace and corruption" are not strictly intended here; for if that were the case, he would have likely said "*flesh* and spirit." Furthermore, the people he is speaking about are primarily those who will be revealed as secret unbelievers, who

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of Hebrews 4:12, specifically explaining how the Word of God distinguishes between the "soul" and the "spirit"—the lower and higher faculties of the human heart.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>Walking in Darkness</header>

<sig>N</sig>

<margin>
O Divine Spirit! Shine with power,
You who gave the hearing ear.
See my heart with love in prayer,
To You, my Lord, more fair.

Oh blessed day, oh happy hour,
That brought this joyful sound
Of pardoning love through Christ’s rich Grace
To heal my mortal wound.
Proverbs 20:27
</margin>

...do not have a **spirit** in that sense at all in them. Instead, these terms are used here to express the two main powers of the heart: 

1. The **soul**, which is the lower part—the more sensory <note>original: "sensual"; here referring to the part of the human psyche driven by emotions and physical senses rather than intellect</note> part where the affections reside (as it is also used in 1 Thessalonians 5:23 <note>The original text cites 1 Thess. 5:20, but the division of "soul and spirit" is famously found in 1 Thess. 5:23</note>). The Word "divides" this part by discovering how closely and excessively all those affections cling <note>original: "cleave"</note> to sin. 

2. The **spirit**, which is the higher <note>original: "superior"</note> part consisting of the **understanding, conscience, and so on.** The Word rips this open by discovering how these faculties plot and contrive the accomplishment of sin. 

**Dividing**, in this context, means "discovering apart" or differentiating how things are carried out separately in each part. Furthermore, it reveals what correspondence and intercourse there is between these two; it shows how sin and all our actions pass through them from one to the other, just as blood and vital spirits <note>original: "spirits"; in early medicine, these were believed to be refined fluids that carried life and sensation through the veins and arteries</note> flow through the veins and arteries in all parts of the body. 

In the body, there are several regions (as anatomists call them) divided by partitions. There are the **vital parts** in the "upper loft" near the neck, where the heart and lungs are lodged. Then there are the **natural parts** in the lower region. These are divided by the diaphragm <note>original: "midriffe"</note> as if by a floor between them. 

So it is in the soul (to which Solomon perhaps alludes when he calls its several powers **the chambers of the belly**, as some read it <note>Proverbs 20:27</note>). There is the sensory part of the affections—the **soul**—which is, as it were, in a distinct room from that more sublime and spiritual part, the **spirit**. And as the **spirit of man** (that is, the conscience and understanding of a man) **searches all those chambers** (as it says there), it **knows what is in man**, as the Apostle speaks in 1 Corinthians 2:11. Yet, when it does so, it is not by an innate light, but with "God’s candle," as Solomon expresses it; that is, by the Word and the light it provides.

</margin>

And in the *Spirit*, it is said to reveal what can be imagined as most secluded and hidden, so locked up that no eye could find it out. (These thoughts, above all others, we strive to hide and conceal.) These are like the *joints*, or (as the Greek word <term>ἁρμοὶ</term> <note>original: "harmoi"; meaning joints or fastenings</note> rather implies) like the ligaments, the sinews, and the tendons by which the joints move; so these are the things upon which our designs move and turn. so that even the smallest fault cannot pass uncensored by it. It acts as a careful critic in this review: <gloss>it is discerning <note>original: κριτικός (kritikos)</note></gloss>, judging exactly as critics usually do. So, by this dissection <note>original: "Anatomy"</note> that the Word performs, *all things in man—every creature, even the smallest fiber <note>original: Fibra</note>, the smallest sting in the heart that would escape the sight of the most precise anatomist—are all naked, opened, and cut open before the eyes of him with whom we have to do.*

<margin>The Reasons.</margin>
The reasons why God has reserved this knowledge to himself are: 

1. It was for the glory of God that he should have one private cabinet among his creatures which he alone should know and keep the key to, demonstrating his omniscience <note>the quality of knowing everything</note>. It also provides one place for him to be sanctified in where no creature's eye could pierce. In this way, the greatness of his glory might appear—namely, that he is not only worshiped outwardly, as great men are, but inwardly in *spirit and truth*. His glory is such that it commands the inward parts, which no eye sees but his own; thus, a person will respect God enough to sanctify him in secret even when no other creature looks upon him.

</margin>

<margin>1 Kings 8:39</margin>

2. That God alone might be the judge and rewarder of men's ways, and thus be the one to whom men must give an account. This draws the creature's eye toward him alone, as the strength and the most essential parts <note>original: "first-born"; a biblical metaphor for the best or most important portion</note> of all our actions are his subjects alone and come under his eye and view. Therefore it is said that *he rewards men according to their works, whose heart he knows*. It was appropriate that he alone should take it upon himself to reward, since he alone could—

<header>A Child of Light</header>

know the underlying motives <note>original: "principles"</note> of all actions, in which the essence <note>original: "chiefe"</note> of the good or evil in the action lies. This is the great glory of God and Christ at the day of judgment, that *they will reveal the secrets of all hearts* (1 *Corinthians* 4:5). It is not said so much that men's actions shall then be revealed, but rather the secrets of their hearts. For God's glory lies therein, which he will not give to any other.

<margin>
§. 2.
How Satan has material <note>original: "matter"</note> to accuse and object against us.
Two general considerations: 1. That he knows what corruptions are in all human natures, and may object them to everyone.
</margin>

This having been stated as a most necessary caution, I come to the investigation <note>original: "disquisition"</note> of that question <note>original: "Quære"</note> mentioned: how, and to what extent, can Satan come to know so much against us that he can accuse us?

1. In general, it may be considered: 1. That he knows what goals <note>original: "ends"</note>, intentions, thoughts, and desires <note>original: "lusts"</note> such corrupt hearts as ours usually produce and bring forth in all people. Therefore, he can imagine what ulterior motives <note>original: "by-ends"</note>, and so on, might be stirring in such and such actions. He lays these to our charge and often hits the mark, speaking of a person's heart at random. For our natures are inclined to produce *all kinds of sinful desire* <note>original: "concupiscence"</note>, as the Apostle says in *Romans 7*. Therefore, if there were nothing more than the fact that he knows all temptations common to human nature, he might go far in accusing every person. Having keys of all sorts, fitted to all human spirits, he tries every one to see which will enter. And just as David’s elder brother accused David when he came to the wars—*“I know the pride and the wickedness of your heart”* <note>1 Samuel 17:28</note>—guessing at his ulterior motives <note>original: "by-ends"</note> in it, so does Satan. He often accuses us in the same way, by guesswork. This is what he did to Job: *“Does Job serve God for nothing?”* <note>Job 1:9</note>. He knew such ulterior motives <note>original: "by-ends"</note> existed in some men's hearts, and so he ventured to charge Job with them as well.

# Walking in Darkness

<margin>2. By planting a suspicious thought based on one specific thing he knows about us, he might set the heart to work examining all the rest.</margin>
2. Even if he knows very little about us, he may use one specific thing he knows or suspects to plant a suspicious thought about a person’s spiritual condition <term>estate</term>. This sets the jealous heart to work searching for more evidence against itself. It is like a case of treason: the smallest hint given by someone sets the government <note>original: "State"</note> to work investigating the depths of the matter until everything is revealed. In this way, Satan often plants just a single doubt <term>scruple</term>, which serves as a theme for the heart to expand upon. Upon investigation, the conscience finds evidence against itself to prove and increase that suspicion. This is the general way he works.

<margin>1. More specifically, he may know:</margin>
Secondly, he may more specifically know much about us to accuse us of, framing formal charges <note>original: "bills"</note> against us based on what he knows. Let us first assume he has no access to our inner thoughts and no more way of knowing us than people have of knowing one another—since God set the limit of human knowledge when he told Samuel *to judge by outward appearance* (1 Samuel 16:7). Even then, he has all the advantages that humans have to know one another, but to a much greater and more prominent degree than any man could.

<margin>1. All physical acts committed.</margin>

Now the promise is enjoyed,

They learn daily. For example, the Bible says that through the Church, the good angels learn things they never knew before about the *mysteries of the Gospel* (Ephesians 3:10). Although these mental images <term>species</term> in them and their way of knowing physical things differ from ours, they nevertheless they <note>A small burn hole appears here in the original document.</note>

## A Child of Light

Their powers of perception are analogous to ours, and we know no more about the manner in which they receive the mental images of physical things <note>original: "species à rebus corporeis"</note>—the representations of everything done by physical substances—than a blind man can imagine how those who see perceive colors. Yet we can be sure of this: anything that the human senses or mind can know, they can know as well. All natural things are appropriate objects <note>original: "debita objecta"</note> created for them to observe; for these things were made to be understood by intelligent creatures—and if by any, then by the most superior and intellectual natures.

<margin>
2. Which knowledge he improves through study.
</margin>

2. They make it their business to study people; it is their trade to go back and forth and observe men. As God says to Satan, *“Have you not considered my servant Job?”* Satan is accustomed to considering and studying people; and just as the Apostle urges us *to consider one another to stir up love,* so Satan considers people to stir up sin, and to tempt us to despair because of our sin.

<margin>
3. He may be aware of our spoken confessions to God.
</margin>

3. He may be aware of our spoken <term>vocal</term> confessions of sin to God or others. He may overhear us revealing our own hearts to God in private prayers, or to others when we are in spiritual distress <term>trouble of conscience</term>. Therefore, he can and does know whatever part of the heart is revealed in this way. And why might God not permit him, giving him the opportunity and advantage to accuse us, even using what he learns through this means? This is allowed for the testing <term>trial</term> of His servants—especially in cases where they have returned to the very sins they confessed but have not truly abandoned. It is only just that if the guilt of former sins returns to us in such a case, Satan should be allowed to charge us with them once again. In such a situation, a person should lose the privilege of the *seal of confession* <note>original: "sigillum confessionis," a theological term for the absolute duty of a priest not to disclose anything learned during confession</note>...

For now, this much is certain: although all the powers of the rational soul are locked away from him (as we shall show), and the immediate acts that exist within the soul itself are utterly hidden from him; and though no devil can discern these thoughts directly or *intuitively* <note>original: "intuitive"; immediate, direct knowledge without the need for external signs</note> (any more than one angel can discern the thoughts of another), yet he can discern them *by deduction* <note>original: "arguitive"; reasoning through evidence or signs rather than direct perception</note>. He sees them as they *transpire* and appear, being expressed through the body and physical organs—either outwardly in actions or inwardly in the images of the imagination or the passions—and so, he sees them *as if through another medium* <note>original: "quasi in alio"</note>,

# A Child of Light

<margin>The Devil cannot search the heart</margin>
...and indirectly, they may be discerned from a distance and looked into by angels. This does not at all contradict that prerogative given to God, where He is said alone to know and search the heart, but rather gives it full allowance. Nor does it diminish that privilege given to the soul itself to enjoy—namely, that <margin>1 Corinthians 2:11</margin> *no one knows the things of a man except the spirit of the man that is in him*—as we shall have occasion to show in the Appendix to this discourse.

Therefore, besides these methods and ways of knowing—which are somewhat common among us men toward one another—angels have a further and closer way of knowing the acts of the rational powers (the understanding and the will) than we men can have. They even have a way of communicating their thoughts to us in a more intimate, close, and secret manner; yet this still falls short of an *intuitive* knowledge of them. They can go further into a room than we can, even into a room next to the <term>privy chamber</term> <note>original: "privy chamber"; a private apartment in a palace, used here as a metaphor for the deepest parts of the soul where only God has access</note>, which yet remains fast locked up against them. As their power in all other things reaches a degree higher than ours, so it is in this also. To explain this a little:

Those rational powers and faculties in us—*the understanding and the will*—whose immediate <term>immanent acts</term> <note>original: "immanent acts"; actions that begin and end within the soul itself, such as a thought or a choice, without being expressed outwardly</note> are thus fast locked up in themselves, are nevertheless in this life drenched in the body and its physical organs, upon which their operations depend. First, the understanding is joined to the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "fancy"</note>, which creates <term>reflections</term> <note>original: "parelii"; literally "mock suns," used here to mean mental reflections or shadows that appear in the mind</note>, resemblances, and shadows of those thoughts the mind secretly conceives and forms. Consequently, hardly any thoughts are stirred without the imagination imitating them and acting them out as...

...as far as it is able. 

Secondly, the will is also conjoined with the <term>affections</term> <note>original: "affections"; what we would now call emotions or passions</note>, which are drenched in and express themselves through bodily organs and <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "spirits"; referring to "animal spirits," which historical medicine believed were fluids in the body that carried signals between the brain and the heart</note>. As a result, not a single motion of the will puts itself forth without some degree of bodily affection stirring with it. Therefore, affections are defined as much by their motion in the body as by their seat in the will itself. For example, when anger is defined as "anger is a boiling of the blood around the heart" <note>original Latin: "ira est ebullitio sanguinis circa cor"</note>, the affections are simply the flow and reflow of these spirits to and from the heart.

Now, all theologians grant that the devils may know both of these—both <term>phantasms</term> <note>original: "phantasmes"; mental images or pictures created by the imagination</note> and passions. To know them, these spirits have a nearer access to us than humans can have to one another. Indeed, they may discern them **intuitively** <note>original: "intuitivè"; knowing something directly and immediately as if it is right in front of them</note>, just as we perceive things that are present before us. How else could they work upon the <term>fancy</term> <note>original: "fancy"; the imagination</note>? Otherwise, there would be no diabolical dreams, nor angelic dreams caused by good angels. 

But we find that a good angel dictated a great article of faith to **Joseph**—**Christ’s divinity and nativity**—and this was done in a dream, and therefore through his imagination. Similarly, they inspired the **Sibyls** <note>ancient prophetesses believed to be inspired by a deity</note> and dictated prophecies, as has been said. In the same way, the evil angels prompted **Saul’s** imagination. They do this not by creating new <term>species</term> <note>original: "species"; here meaning the mental forms or images of physical objects</note> and images, but by "calling them forth" <note>original Latin: "evocando"</note> from the images already stored there. Because the images of things in the imagination are physical forms, these spirits can no more create a new physical image than they can create a brand-new body. Therefore, all the power of the angels cannot cause a man born blind to dream of colors.
