His suggestions to us in this matter must occur by discerning the <term>species</term> <note>original: "species"; in historical psychology, these were the mental forms or "mental pictures" of objects and words</note>—that is, the images of all words heard or read—that already lie in the <term>fancy</term> <note>original: "fancy"; the imagination</note>. He orders and composes them just as a <term>Compositor</term> <note>original: "Compositor"; a person who sets type for printing</note> in a printing house arranges his letters (which lie confused before him) into words and sentences to represent to the reader's eye what he wants them to read. 

In the same way, he presents these to the understanding, which naturally "prints" and takes an impression from the imagination of whatever is inside it as quickly as he arranges them. For the same reason that he can call these <term>phantasms</term> <note>original: "phantasmes"; mental images or visual thoughts</note> forth, and so view the mental images and forms stored there already to arrange them as he pleases, it must also be assumed that he is able to discern any of them in the imagination at any time—even when reason itself calls upon them and makes use of them, as happens whenever the mind sets itself to think or ponder. 

These, and all other operations of the <term>sensitive powers</term> <note>the physical senses and the parts of the brain that process sensory information</note>, they may view and see as truly (as far as I know) and as intuitively as we see colors and the reflections of objects in a person's eye. Therefore, when God permits, these evil angels may enter the head and see all the images and forms in the imagination, including those that are in direct connection with the understanding while it is thinking and pondering. This is just as a man sees what images are in the <term>apple of the eye</term> <note>the pupil</note> of another man. By discerning those phantasms which the understanding is actually viewing and using at that moment, the devil can judge what the mind is pondering.

Furthermore, just as we discern people's passions when they manifest and affect their outward appearance—as when shame dyes the face red or fear paints it white—so may...

The angels more secretly discern the motion of these <term>species</term> <note>mental images or forms</note> within us, which is the cause of this outward alteration. They can go further than we men can; they can see the inward commotion of the <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "spirits"; in Early Modern medicine, these were subtle fluids or vapors that carried life and sensation through the body</note> in our internal parts—even in their channels and springs, such as in that physical heart we carry within us, and in the veins and arteries—and so know what <term>affections</term> <note>emotions or passions</note> are stirring.

And this is evident because they are able to work upon the passions as well. Now, their power of working upon these emotions arises from their knowing them, and their skill to move and stir those spirits and <term>humors</term> <note>original: "humors"; the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) thought to determine a person's temperament and health</note> *selectively* in which these passions are seated. And in this, their power of discerning us exceeds *that of us men* in discerning other men, just as their power of communicating their minds to us also does. For as they can communicate secretly through the <term>fancy</term> <note>the imagination</note> itself, while we can only communicate by outward words and signs to the outward senses of others, so they can discern more secretly what is in the imagination, and not only what appears in the outward parts. This is just one "room" further that they get into, which we men cannot reach. 

So, in like manner, their power over our passions also exceeds ours; they can see into the passions and discern the least rising of the tide, the least turn of the stream of emotions in our veins and in the physical heart. Satan can discern those smaller <term>aguish fits</term> <note>original: "aguish fits"; fever-like spikes or tremors</note> of passion that accompany any act of the will, which men do not notice. Likewise, they can stir those passions by working upon the humors and spirits they float in, which men cannot manage to do. But concerning this great and necessary <term>query</term> <note>original: "quare"; Latin for "inquiry" or "question"</note>, as well as how he uses this to communicate all his temptations to us, I will speak more largely in an *Appendix* to be annexed to this treatise.

<header>A Child of Light</header>

### Chapter IX.
#### *How capable Satan is of working upon that third principle: the passions and corrupt emotions; and how he uses them to drive home his false conclusions with terrors.*

Thus we have seen how capable Satan is of working upon those two previously mentioned principles: carnal <note>meaning worldly or unspiritual</note> reason—abusing it with false <term>majors</term> <note>original: "Majors"; in logic, the major premise of a syllogism or argument</note>—and also upon the conscience, by accusing us of our sins and misrepresenting our spiritual condition. It now remains only to show how he can stir and influence our passions and corrupt emotions, and use them to reinforce all those false conclusions—such as "We are hypocrites"—that he derives from them, accompanied by hideous and horrific fears and terrors.

<margin>
§. 1.
That Satan can raise up terrors.
Isaiah 38:13.
Psalm 104.
</margin>

Regarding these terrors, just as he is called a *serpent* (as we mentioned) for his clever maneuvers, cunning reasonings, and wiles, he is also called a *lion*—the strongest of all beasts. A roaring *lion* is the most terrifying of all, especially when he roars; in Scripture, his roaring is often used to express the onset of dread and horror: *"The lion has roared, who will not fear?"* (Amos 3:8). And as some have observed—and the *Psalmist* <note>The author of the Psalms</note> seems to suggest—by his roaring, he strikes such horror and amazement into all other animals that they stand still as if <term>exanimated</term> <note>original: "exanimated"; paralyzed with fear or appearing lifeless</note>, allowing him to seize and prey upon them as he wishes. It is also in this regard—his influence over our passions—that the "darts" mentioned earlier are primarily called *fiery*. This is because of the <term>dolour</term> <note>original: "dolour"; intense sorrow or mental distress</note>, anguish, inflammation, and inner turmoil they cause by unsettling our emotions.

<summary>This page explains how Satan acts as both a cunning serpent and a roaring lion to manipulate human emotions, using fear and distress to convince believers they are hypocrites.</summary>
<keywords>Satan, spiritual warfare, passions, emotions, fear, fiery darts, serpent, lion, hypocrisy, conscience</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Satan influences human passions, specifically by intensifying natural fears into overwhelming spiritual terrors, comparing his tactics to poisoned arrows.</meta>

<header>Walking in Darkness</header>

<margin>A point to consider</margin>
<margin>How the fire burns</margin>
Those fears which our own hearts engendered <note>created or gave birth to</note> within us were but as smoke; these darts of his put a fire into them and cause them to flame and blaze. The allusion is to the poisoned darts which the Scythians <note>An ancient nomadic people of Central Asia and Eastern Europe known for their skills in archery and warfare</note> of old and other nations now use in war, dipped in the blood and gall of asps and vipers. The venomous heat of these darts, like a fire in the flesh, killed those wounded by them with torments that are the most like hell of any other. *Job* also alludes to this in *Job 6:4*: *The arrows of the Almighty are within me. The poison—*or (as others read it) *the heat and fervor* (we may use both and read) *the hot poison thereof—drinks up my spirit, even as fire preying upon moisture.* And what were those arrows he speaks of there but terrors? So it follows: *the terrors of God,* etc.

<margin>Swallowed up <note>original: "καταποθῇ"; Greek *katapothē*, meaning to be consumed or swallowed up</note></margin>
<margin>1794</margin>
<margin>The 2nd point</margin>
This uses the same phrase by which *that Corinthian* <note>The man mentioned in 2 Corinthians who was being disciplined by the church</note> is said to have been in danger of being "drunk up" (as the word signifies) with excessive sorrow when Satan had to deal with him (2 *Corinthians* 2:7). The same word is used again regarding the devil in 1 *Peter* 5:8: *Seeking whom he may drink up* <note>original: "drink up"; often translated in modern Bibles as "devour"</note>.

<margin>James 3:6</margin>
So, just as Satan inflames other members of the body and the inordinate lusts within them with an added natural intensity and violence—as the tongue, which (though of itself full of poison) is said to be *set on fire from hell* (that is, from Satan, who is called *hell* in the same way the phrase *the gates of hell* is used, just as the good angels, the noblest creatures, are called *heavens* in *Hebrews* 7:26)—he inflames men's tongues with an excess of venom and malice to wound men's reputations with. Even as, on the contrary, the Holy Ghost set the Apostles' tongues on fire with zeal, so (I say) he likewise can and does put fire into those darts...

<summary>This page explores how Satan intensifies human fear into spiritual torment, comparing his "fiery darts" to the poisoned arrows of the Scythians and explaining how he "swallows up" the soul with sorrow and malice.</summary>

<header>A Child of Light</header>

he wounds the conscience with; and thereby increases our fears and griefs, and causes such disquiet and pangs that hellfire, as it were, begins to flame in a person’s conscience. Just as *Christ* is that *Brazen Serpent* <note>original: "Brazen Serpent"; a reference to the bronze snake Moses lifted in the wilderness to heal the Israelites (Numbers 21), here representing healing and salvation</note>, so *Satan* is that *fiery serpent* that can sting us with the guilt of sin.

<margin>A caution</margin>
And here I must introduce the same caution I used in the previous chapter: namely, that he does not produce these terrors by direct impressions upon the conscience. In that regard, the conscience is subject to God’s blow alone, just as it is subject to His knowledge alone.

As I hinted, I take this to be the other principal part of the meaning of those words in *Hebrews 4:11-12*: *The word of God is living and powerful...* <note>original: "quick and powerfull"; in the 17th century, "quick" meant "alive" or "living"</note> For there, the author presents Christ to us (as has been shown) as a Judge completely capable of executing vengeance against us. This is not only because of His omniscience <note>all-knowing nature</note> to find us out in all our evasions, but also because a judge would not be feared much—no matter how much skill and knowledge he had to search out the deceit and guilt of criminals—if he were not also armed with the power to punish and torture them. Therefore, the Apostle’s goal is also to strike terror into their hearts regarding the vengeance He can execute.

Consequently, his aim is to urge them not to trifle with God or His word—regarding which He had *sworn* in the previous verse that those who did not believe should not enter into His rest. Thus, the purpose of the words must also be understood as showing the dreadful power of God and His word in avenging itself upon those who despise Him and it, and not merely to describe His omniscience and knowledge of the heart,

<summary>This page argues that while Satan can aggravate a person's sense of guilt and fear, he cannot directly strike the conscience; only God possesses the absolute power and omniscience to judge and punish the human soul.</summary>

<header>Walking in Darkness</header>

Indeed, *that* extensive illustration of His knowledge is brought in only as a clearer demonstration of *His* power to punish—He *who* can dive so deep into our hearts. From this we might argue and fear the stroke of that sword in His hand, whose eyes are so piercing. 

Accordingly, all those expressions used there are intended to set forth the dreadfulness of His power just as fully as they set forth His knowledge. He likewise uses a comparison that—both by the nature of the things and according to the more usual phrasing of Scripture—more properly and abundantly suggests this slaying and wounding of disobedient souls by His sword, rather than just searching the soul and spirit. 

First, he says this *word* is *living and active* <note>original: "quick and lively"; in the 17th century, "quick" meant "alive," and "lively" meant "full of energy or power"</note>. It is called this not only regarding its duration (as it abides *forever*), but regarding its action and execution. We call things that are exceedingly active "quick," even if they are not alive. For example, we speak of quicksilver <note>original: "quick-silver"; mercury, known for its rapid, fluid movement</note>, which runs through a person's bowels like hail-shot <note>original: "haile shot"; small lead pellets fired from a gun</note>. Conversely, we call drugs and drinks "dead" when they have lost their potency <note>original: "vertue"</note> and are ineffective. 

In respect to this energy and power to work upon human hearts, we should understand John 6:63: *The words I speak (says Christ) are spirit and life*, that is, they are full of an active principle. We often use the term "spirit" for an active, working principle, such as the "spirit of wine" <note>original: "spirit of wine"; concentrated alcohol or ethanol, used here as a metaphor for a potent, active essence</note>. Therefore, when he says the word is *quick*, he notes that this word is inspired with a principle that is most quick, spirited, and active, and ready to work as the occasion requires—even with the

And then, 3. he further gives examples of its operations, showing the effects of that power which are most dreadful, as the comparisons he uses imply. *Sharper than any two-edged sword.* 

Now, just as elsewhere the Word is compared to an arsenal of all kinds of weapons and engines for war and vengeance—*The weapons of our warfare are mighty, etc.* (1 Corinthians 10:4 <note>The author likely intends 2 Corinthians 10:4</note>)—in the same way here, he more specifically compares it to a sword, the most common and most terrible of all the weapons of death used at that time. The mere brandishing of a sword strikes paleness and horror into a man before the blow even reaches him. This imagery is usually used in Scripture to express vengeance, especially in the prophecy of Ezekiel. It is also found in Psalm 7:13: *If he does not turn, God has sharpened his sword and prepared his weapons of death*; that is, to inflict torments, including eternal torments, as in Deuteronomy 32:42. 

Indeed, whatever causes torment, pain <note>original: "dolour"</note>, or anguish is called a "sword" in Scripture; and piercing with a sword is used to express the most intense pains <note>original: "exquisite dolors"</note>, as in Luke 2:35: *Yes, a sword shall pierce through your soul also.* This was spoken to the blessed mother of Christ, referring to the anguish and grief that would cut her even to the heart when she would behold her Son upon the Cross. # A Child of Light

<margin>Hebrews 4:12–13</margin>

But "and also of the spirit" <note>original: "$\tau\varepsilon$ $\kappa\alpha\grave{\iota}$ $\pi\nu\varepsilon\acute{\upsilon}\mu\alpha\tau o\varsigma$" (te kai pneumatos)</note>, which is expressed with an emphasis: his meaning is not so much that it divides the *soul* from the *spirit* (as some have understood it), but that it divides the soul and the spirit also. It is a two-edged sword and can, at one blow, strike through both. This axe *strikes at the root*; it strikes at the spirit, which, when wounded, who can bear? as *Solomon* says <note>Referring to Proverbs 18:14: "The spirit of a man will sustain his infirmity; but a wounded spirit who can bear?"</note>. 

And then he concludes in verse 13: *As before him all things are naked; so also* they are laid bare and exposed <note>original: "$\tau\varepsilon\tau\rho\alpha\chi\eta\lambda\iota\sigma\mu\acute{\varepsilon}\nu\alpha$" (tetrachēlismena). While modern bibles translate this as "laid bare" or "open," the author notes a more graphic literal interpretation: having the neck bent back or the throat exposed.</note>. That is, they lie with their throats cut, if he but strikes them—dead and speechless at his feet, as *Theodoret* <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–457 AD) was an influential theologian and bishop of the Early Church.</note> explains that word, which is translated as *opened*. 

Now, thus far—that is, to this *spirit* in man—no created sword can reach; their edges turn against it. But even this, the word reaches, and it alone. So the summary intent of everything here is the same as what Christ expressed elsewhere in other words: to exhort them to *fear that God* whose sword and powerful word is able alone to wound in this way. We are *not to fear those who can only wound and kill the body*, and can only reach the sensual soul that is drenched in it, but cannot wound or kill the *Spirit*—which God alone can do, and no mere creature whatsoever. 

Therefore, in all our thoughts and fears of Satan’s power to know our sins, or his power to trouble or disquiet our spirits (as throughout this discourse), we must set such bounds that this exclusive privilege <note>original: "incommunicable royalty"</note> of God and of his word may remain unencroached upon. Specifically, that He alone knows and can immediately wound the spirit and conscience. This passage held both of these truths out to us at once, which made me...

## § 2.

<margin>
Though he cannot directly wound the conscience, yet:
</margin>
But even though Satan cannot directly <note>original: "immediately," meaning without any intervening cause or agent</note> wound the conscience and make impressions of God’s wrath upon it—for just as no created being can pour out God’s love and cause a person to taste its sweetness, neither can they cause them to taste the bitterness of His wrath; God is the only one who can report on both.

<margin>
1. He can rake in those wounds the Spirit has made.
</margin>
Yet, first: when the Holy Spirit has lashed and whipped the conscience, making it tender and stripping away the skin, Satan may then irritate it more and more. He continues to rub the sore by casting in his horrible suggestions and false fears.

<margin>
2. By renewing the memory of those terrors impressed by the Spirit, he can strike the soul with a fresh fear of something worse.
</margin>
And second: by renewing the experiential memory of those lashes which the soul has received from the *Spirit*, he can overwhelm the soul with fears of an infinitely more painful vengeance yet to come. He flashes representations of hellfire into their consciences based on those real glimpses they have already felt, doing so in a way that drives the soul into vast and unimaginable horrors.

<margin>
3. He can apply all the threats against hypocrites, etc.
</margin>
And then third: he can apply all the threats that are thundered forth in the Word of God against hypocrites and unregenerate <note>Those who have not experienced spiritual rebirth or conversion.</note> men, discharging them all with great violence and noise upon a poor, doubting soul. He can and does present and show his prisoners those terrible chains, racks, and other *instruments of death* <margin>Psalm 7:13</margin> (as the *Psalmist* calls them) which God has prepared against sinners. These are stored up in the great armory of His Word, *having them ready to punish all disobedience* (2 Corinthians 10:6). With the rattling of these chains and other instruments, Satan can create a noise in the conscience of a poor sinner to frighten them. He is even more capable of doing this because of the experience he has of such terrors within himself.

<summary>This page explains that while Satan cannot directly inflict the sense of God's wrath on a soul, he can aggravate the "wounds" already made by the Holy Spirit. He uses the memory of past spiritual conviction and the threats of Scripture to terrify doubting believers.</summary>

...himself: *being bound up in everlasting chains, under darkness, until the judgment of the great day (Jude 6).* And just as a "son of consolation" and child of light is enabled to comfort others more effectively because of the comfort he himself has received from God, so this *Prince of Darkness* is more powerful at terrifying weak consciences—those trapped by the ties of their own sins—because of the terrors he himself has experienced from the Lord. Therefore, in Scripture, 

<margin>Hebrews 2:14</margin>
<margin>Power of Death</margin>

just as a power over sin is attributed to him, so is the *power of death (Hebrews 2:14).* In this context, "death" refers not so much to physical death, but to that eternal death which is the proper punishment for sin, and to which our guilt binds us. This power of his is not the authority of a Judge who sentences one to death or casts people into hell; that authority is a unique honor belonging to Christ’s crown <note>original: "special flower of Christs Crowne," meaning a specific, beautiful prerogative of his sovereignty</note>. He is the one who holds the keys of death and hell at His belt (Revelation 1:18). This power belongs to God alone, who is the only one to be feared because He alone can cast both body and soul into hell. Nor is it as if he were the primary tormentor and executioner of human souls after the Great Day of Judgment, since those souls are to be tormented by that same fire which was prepared for the devils themselves. 

And who is it that torments them? This "power" refers principally to: 

1. The power and advantage he gained over sinners once he had seduced them. This allowed him to come forward boldly as an accuser <note>original: "pleader," acting like a prosecutor in a legal sense</note> against them, using the authority of God’s righteous law and Word. He calls upon and provokes God's justice to condemn poor sinners and sentence them to death—at least until Christ, that righteous Advocate, stripped him of those arguments and that power through His own sacrifice <note>original: "satisfaction," referring to Christ's atonement for sin on the cross</note>, which satisfied the demands the law had...

Secondly, the meaning is that just as he has this power in God’s court over the sentence of death upon poor sinners, he also has it within our consciences. There, he can urge the Law upon us and argue everything that the *Law says against those who are under the Law*, and throw us into the fears of the death threatened there. He increases those fears by presenting the terrors of the Law, to which—because of natural conscience—people are by themselves *subject all their life long*. 

This second kind of power—influence over the conscience—is what the phrase *power of death* specifically refers to here. This is clear because the next verse says: *and deliver those who through fear of death were subject to bondage all their life long* (verse 15). 

Because the children of God in this life *know only in part*, they also *love only in part*. And so far as their love remains imperfect, the <margin>1 John 4:18</margin> *fear that brings torment* keeps its hold on them. *For it is only perfect love that casts out all fear.* Therefore, as long as slavish fear remains, they may still be subject to terror from him who has the power of death—which he exercises over everyone who is, to any degree, subject to that fear while in this life.

<margin>4. He can excite the passions of fear and trembling of spirit.</margin>

4. He can directly, by his own power, stir the passions of fear and grief. He can excite them beyond their natural levels, just as the winds can raise waves in the sea and make the floods roar; in this way, he can create a tumult in the emotions and throw the entire soul into a state of hurry and violent disturbance <term>original: "perturbation"</term>. He is the *Prince of the airy part* of the *little world* <note>original: "little world," the microcosm, or the individual human being</note> in man, just as he is prince of *that elementary region* in the *great world* <note>original: "great world," the macrocosm, or the physical universe</note>. Thus, he can raise unnatural storms and vapors

One would wonder that a man so holy and wise could be so carried away as to do an act so foolish (as he himself saw afterward, saying in *verse 10*, *“I have done very foolishly”*). Indeed, it was so grossly sinful that it was abominable even in the eyes of *Joab* (1 *Chronicles* 21:6), a man who seems by his other behaviors to have possessed only a common nature <note>original: "but nature in him"; meaning Joab was a worldly man without special spiritual grace, yet even he saw the sin.</note>. But the Devil was in it; as *verse 1* says, *Satan provoked David to count the people* by raising up such a desire and inclination in him. 

The same appears in the emotion of love. This is evident in history by many examples. When he has distempered <note>original: "distempered"; meaning to throw out of balance or make ill, particularly regarding the bodily humors.</note> and disordered everything within a person, making them prone to fear, he then comes with his suggestions. He speaks of nothing but wrath, terrors, threats, the heinous nature of the person's sins, and the fearfulness of God’s wrath to that troubled conscience. Then (just as when a person's choler <note>original: "choler"; the humor associated with anger and irritability.</note> is high, every little thing provokes them), when fear and melancholy <note>original: "melancholy"; the humor associated with sadness and darkness, thought to be caused by an excess of "black bile."</note> are excited, every suggestion and every suspicion strikes the soul through and through with horrific fears and jealousies. 

Thus, although he does not act directly, he works upon the conscience by means of these raised mists, vapors, and fogs that surround and darken this sun <note>The "sun" here represents the light of reason or the conscience, which is obscured by emotional turmoil.</note>. ...workshops, he greatly deceives with false colors and glosses <note>original: "glosses"; meaning superficial interpretations or deceptive appearances.</note>. And when once passions <note>original: "affections"; in this context, intense emotions or inclinations that influence judgment.</note> are stirred up and cloud the mind, then a multitude of troublesome thoughts arise, and every suggestion suitable to that passion take hold and prevail with a man's spirit. This appears in the speech of Christ in *Luke* 24:38: *Why are you troubled (or afraid), and why do thoughts arise in your hearts?* Passions, like heavy weights hung upon a clock, do not only make the wheels—the thoughts—move faster, but also pervert and wrest <note>original: "wreſts"; meaning to twist or distort.</note> them the wrong way. So, to a heart thus distempered <note>original: "diſtempered"; meaning out of balance, particularly in one's physical or mental health.</note>, all things come to be presented incorrectly, even as to a bloodshot eye all things seem red.

In a word, just as he deludes his Enthusiasts <note>original: "Enthuſiaſts"; a term used in the 17th century for people who claimed to have direct, private revelations from God, often viewed with suspicion by mainstream theologians.</note> by encouraging and backing their false opinions and illusions with joys and raptures of spirit (which differ as much from the joys of the Holy Spirit, which are *unspeakable and glorious*, as heaven differs from earth), so he can and does back his false reasonings and accusations to holy men about their estates <note>original: "eſtates"; meaning their spiritual standing or condition before God.</note> with an abundance of terror and disturbance. This also differs as much from the impressions of God's wrath made immediately by the Spirit upon the conscience as those false joys are found to do.

<page-num>107</page-num>

...us, I will only mention some of those many great advantages he has over us in all these false reasonings and accusations, in order to urge them on <note>original: "set them on"; meaning to incite or drive them forward.</note>, to fasten his slanders and the false conclusions drawn from them, and to persuade the mind of their truth. I bring these points in here because they are common to all the specific cases previously mentioned.

<margin>§. 1. 1st Advantage: He can and does suggest thoughts frequently and familiarly.</margin>

First, it is no small advantage that he can familiarly and frequently suggest these things to us over and over again. The frequency of any thought that recurs repeatedly—that stays with us and haunts us—secretly has the force of an argument to persuade us that it is true. We often say, "I have thought so again and again." A cunning flatterer, who is continually making suggestions and taking every opportunity to do so, may eventually strive hard to undermine <note>original: "work out"; to displace or damage a relationship through persistence.</note> a close and dear friend and make someone jealous of him. Just as the judge yielded to the widow’s persistence in *Luke 18:5*, so is the mind apt to yield to a suggestion that haunts it and persistently presents itself—even if it means passing a false judgment against oneself.

<margin>§. 2. 2nd Advantage: He presents a multitude of false reasonings, etc., all at once.</margin>

Secondly, he can and does also present a multitude of reasons and considerations all at once, all tending to confirm the same belief. He will sometimes bring in a "cloud of witnesses" and examples to prove we are hypocrites, surrounding the mind with them so that whichever way it looks, it sees nothing else. Just as he showed to *Christ all the glory of the world in the twinkling of an eye*, he can do the same with a person’s sins. This causes a person to have a general view of them and see nothing else, no matter where they look. And what

Experience shows what power this must have to prevail upon the mind and judgment to give its assent. For instance, when a man who is uncertain about a disputed truth reads the arguments of an opposing party—where only everything that can be said for that side is presented—it often staggers him. For the moment, it wins over his opinion to that side until he reads and considers what is said to the contrary. Indeed, even if a man is confirmed and settled in the truth, he may sometimes face an army of arguments from the other side. These are so ranked and ordered that they shake and stagger him for the time being. This is inevitably what happens during the agitation of this great controversy over a man’s spiritual state <note>original: "estate"; referring to one's standing before God or state of grace.</note>, when Satan musters and marshals an army of objections all at once, rather than scattered one by one, as he is capable of doing.

<margin>
§.3. 3rd Advantage: He can hold the mind and its focus on these objections alone.
</margin>

Thirdly, he is able to fix the mind’s attention on these things so exclusively that he keeps away anything that might provide comfort. He can fold down that column in the pages of our heart where grace or anything comforting is recorded and turn over the leaf, holding our eyes fixed so we read nothing but the other side where our *errors* <note>original: "Errataes"; a Latin term for mistakes or printing errors.</note> and *sins* are written. This causes a man’s soul to forget all good, just as the Church is said to do during spiritual desertion in *Lamentations 3:17*, and to forget *his own mercies*, as *Jonah* says. He can multiply suggestions so quickly and approach with *such a storm* that, as Job complains in *Job 9:17*, he will *not allow them to catch their breath*. This is why the Apostle calls them the *buffeting of Satan* in *2 Corinthians 12*, because like actual blows, they strike the spirit repeatedly and rapidly—"thick and threefold"—so that a person’s spirit cannot catch its breath. He rains down temptations some...

He does not only suggest reasons intended to persuade, but he drives them home with forceful declarations. In this way, just as in an argument, a weak spirit is often overwhelmed by a stronger one—not so much by the force of the argument itself, but by the strength and violence of the other's spirit. (For many, when the *iron is blunt* and their arguments lack a sharp edge, simply *apply more strength*, as *Solomon* says in *Ecclesiastes 10:10*, and so they prevail). Satan does the same; he is a spirit of greater natural strength than we are by creation, and our own guilt further weakens us when we argue with him.

Skilled lawyers <note>original: "Cunning pleaders"</note> may argue a case with such intensity and confidence that, as *Socrates* said after his accusers had finished, if he had not been very innocent, he would have suspected himself of being guilty. How much more likely is this when the accusation falls upon people who are actually as guilty as we all are, and when the crime being charged <note>original: "impleaded"</note> is something we are already suspicious of in ourselves? A person easily believes what they already fear, just as they believe what they hope for; for *what they fear, they easily believe* <note>original: "quod metuunt, facile credunt"</note>. We see that in our opinions, there is often a preconceived notion <note>original: "preconceit"</note> that greatly influences the mind—an inclination <note>original: "a giving of mind"</note> to think that a thing is definitely one way or another. Now, just as Satan confirms Enthusiasts <note>term: Enthusiasts; in this period, the term referred to people who claimed to receive direct, private revelations from God apart from Scripture</note> in their opinions and notions through such false revelations, he also joins with the suspicions <note>original: "jealousies"</note> of believers and puts weight into the balance, strongly swaying them to judge their spiritual state <note>original: "estates"</note> incorrectly.

<margin>§. 5. 5. That his reasonings are backed with a feeling <note>original: "sense"</note> of terrors.</margin>

And fifthly, because he (as was said) backs the false conclusions he deduces from these things with terrors, etc., this becomes an argument based on feeling <note>original: "sense"</note>. Such arguments carry great weight in shaping our judgment and our opinion of things. A notion that comes to us accompanied by joy, we are apt to conclude is true; and in the same way, we react to what comes with terror. Such impressions act as a seal to what is suggested, serving to confirm it in our minds.

<page-num>111</page-num>

And just as the Holy Spirit <note>original: "holy Ghost"</note> seals his instructions (Job 33:16) with feelings <note>original: "impressions"</note> of joy and so forth, so does Satan seal his temptations with feelings of fear and restlessness <note>original: "disquietment"</note>. If a person has a dream with a strong impact <note>original: "impression"</note>, they are likely to pay attention to it and think there is something to it. This is what made Nebuchadnezzar think there must be something significant in his dream (Daniel chapters 2 and 4), even though he had forgotten what it was: it was because the dream made him afraid, and his thoughts troubled him (verse 5).

<margin>
§ 6. 
6. That he suggests everything without us noticing <note>original: "undiscerned by us"</note>.
</margin>

A sixth advantage is that he suggests and works all these feelings in a way that is completely unnoticed <note>original: "undiscerned"</note> by us as coming from him. As a result, we do not know but that they are our own thoughts—indeed, we sometimes think they are from the Holy Spirit, acting as the "spirit of bondage" <note>term: spirit of bondage; a biblical phrase from Romans 8:15 referring to a state of religious fear and legalistic anxiety, as opposed to the "spirit of adoption" or freedom</note> within us. This is also an exceedingly great advantage: it is like an enemy obtaining the opponent’s own watchword or their own colors <note>term: colors; the flags or banners used by an army to identify its units</note>. This causes us to readily yield and open the gates to him. 

And although we perceive his deception <note>original: "delusion"</note> once the temptation is over, we still cannot distinguish his suggestions from our own thoughts while they are happening to us—while we are in the "mist and eclipse." Therefore, he can come back again and again with the same temptation—today, tomorrow, and the next day—and we do not realize it. If we did, we would not listen to it, any more than we would listen to someone who had formerly deceived us. Thus, Ahab’s prophets did not know that Satan was a lying spirit within them; for one of them said to Micaiah, "When did the Spirit of God go from me to speak to you?" (1 Kings 22:24). Those strong delusions (2 Thessalonians 2:11) <note>original text cites verse 10; the phrase "strong delusion" appears in verse 11</note> could not have persuaded their minds to believe a lie if the source had been recognized <note>original: "discerned"</note>.

A poor soul fighting with Satan in this darkness is like a man assaulted by someone carrying a dark lantern <term>dark lantern: a lantern with a sliding shutter that can hide the light, allowing the bearer to see their target while remaining in total shadow</term>. The attacker can see the one he is assaulting and knows exactly how to strike <note>original: "buffet"</note> him, following him wherever he goes. Meanwhile, the poor victim cannot see his attacker, does not know who is hitting him, and cannot tell how to ward off <note>original: "award"</note> the blow. <margin>1 Corinthians 12</margin> Therefore, when the Apostle [Paul] was struck <note>original: "buffeted"</note> by Satan, he did not know what to do except to seek help from God through prayer. He could no more avoid or run away from those suggestions than he could from himself. Nor could all the saints on earth have freed him in any other way; no one could, until God caused the enemy to depart.

§ 1.

First, what if God chooses to exercise His absolute authority <note>original: "absoluteness"</note> and prerogative in His dealings with His child, proceeding without following any established case or precedent? He may do this, and (as is generally thought) He did so in the case of Job. Job is considered by some to have been set up as a type <note>original: "type"—a person or event in the Old Testament believed to foreshadow something in the New Testament</note> among the Gentiles <note>non-Jewish people</note> of Christ at His crucifixion, who was to be abandoned by His apostles and forsaken by God, etc. And though Job’s desertion <note>the sense of being abandoned by God</note> began only with his property, children, and body, it eventually pierced further and seized his spirit. (Though it did not go so far as to make him question God’s love for him—we read of this nowhere in his story—it was nonetheless seen in God withdrawing the comfort of His presence and in Satan making him a target <term>butt: a target used in archery practice</term> for his arrows.) 

And yet, although the Lord had sufficient reason to act against him, no specific cause (as I recall) is pleaded. Instead, it is resolved as an extraordinary dealing in which God took the liberty to glorify Himself by singling out one of His strongest, most courageous <note>original: "stoutest, valiantest"</note> champions and setting him hand-to-hand to wrestle with the powers of darkness. And because Satan was (as it were) not a match for him, **God became his enemy Himself** (Job 13:24 <note>Job 13:24: "Wherefore hidest thou thy face, and holdest me for thine enemy?"</note>). No one was more righteous than Job was previously; you know the Lord glories in him. No one ever led a stricter life (read Chapter 31); no man lived more in awe [of God], and that by fearing

God, indeed, never lacks a cause, nor does He act this way where there is no sin. Yet, just as it was said of the young man who was born blind—*not for his own sin, nor his parents’* (though not entirely without sin) *but for the glory of God*—it was an act of God’s prerogative. 

So it is here. God has higher purposes for glorifying Himself through the patience, the victory, and the conquest of such a champion as *Job* was, and for refuting the devil, who accused him of *serving God for nothing* <note>original: "serving God for nought"—referring to Satan's claim in Job 1:9 that Job only followed God because of his wealth and health</note>. 2. When God intends to make a man wise and able to comfort others.

A second extraordinary case is when He intends to make a person a wise, capable, skillful, and strong Christian—specifically "wise" in what is the greatest learning and wisdom in the world: knowing how to comfort others through personal experience <note>original: "experimentally"</note>.

This seems to be the reason for His dealings with *Heman*. *Heman* was raised in this school of temptation and kept in this grade <term>original: "forme"—a level or class in a school</term> from his youth (Psalm 88:15). Yet in the end, when God raised him up again, this *Heman* (who lived around the time of *David* and *Solomon*) was counted among the wisest of his age, and one of the four who were closest to *Solomon* in wisdom (1 Kings 4:31). Similarly, that great Apostle [Paul] was a man exposed to the same struggles <note>original: "combats"</note> as others; he was buffeted by *Satan* (2 Corinthians 12) and filled with inward terrors as well as outward ones. What was the purpose of this? Not so much...

<margin>
Like John Bunyan
</margin>
It is not every word that will comfort a weary soul, but only *a word in season* (verse 4 of this 50th chapter of Isaiah); that is, a word which is fitted to the person's specific case. Now, who are those who are furnished with such apt, fit, and seasonable considerations to comfort others, but those who have had the same temptations and have been in the same distresses? This art of speaking peace and words of comfort in *season* is the greatest wisdom in the world, and it is not learned except in *Heman’s* school <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the author of Psalm 88, was famous for his wisdom and his lifelong struggle with spiritual depression.</note>. Temptation was one of *Luther’s* <note>Martin Luther, the Protestant Reformer, famously said that "prayer, meditation, and temptation" make a theologian.</note> masters. 

And therefore, of all the abilities of the ministry, Christ gives an example of this in this chapter (verse 4) and calls the tongue of him who is able to speak seasonably to weary souls *the tongue of the learned*. Therefore, in Job 33:23, to raise up one whose soul draws near to the grave is said to be the work of *one in a thousand*. This is easily granted if you consider the danger of such distress. In Scripture, it is called the *breaking of the bones* (Psalm 51:8) because the strength of a man's spirit that should uphold him—just as the bones uphold the body—sinks within him. Now, to be a bone-setter is not every man's skill; he must have special art and skill <note>original: "cunning"</note>, and furthermore a "lady's hand" (as we often say)—that is, meekness and pity. These qualities are never genuine <note>original: "kindly"—meaning natural or fitting</note> except when we have tasted the same suffering ourselves, or have reason to fear the same.

<margin>Restore <note>original: "Καταρτίζετε" (Katartizete)</note></margin> 
Galatians 6:1. The Apostle commands them to **set such a person in joint again** (as the Greek word signifies), "lest you also be tempted." It is the work of one who is spiritual: *You who are spiritual, restore such a person.* It requires skill to remove every splinter <note>original: "shiver"—referring to fragments in a wound or bone</note>, to address every doubt, and to set everything straight again. This state is also called the wounding of the spirit; as Solomon says, *A wounded spirit who can bear?* (Proverbs 18:14). Just as the power of sin wounds, so does its guilt—and one can be as incurable as the other. Since it is the spirit of a person that is wounded, the healing must involve something dropped into the heart that can reach the spirit. There must be specific, chosen remedies <note>original: "elective plasters"</note> to heal these wounds, because these wounds often differ. 

There are some objections that even the most learned men have never encountered in books. Satan has devised "methods" (Ephesians 6) for tempting souls who feel deserted, which he uses over and over. A person will not know those depths or be able to fathom them unless they have been **in the depths** themselves, as Heman <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the author of Psalm 88, who wrote of his deep spiritual suffering.</note> speaks. Then he shall see such **wonders of God in those depths** that no one else ever saw, and thereby gain such wisdom as to be able to encourage others by his example to trust in God and call upon Him, as David did in Psalm 32:5–6.

<margin>3. In the case of an abundance of revelations and comforts. I.</margin>
The third extraordinary case is that God deserts a person if they have had, or are about to have, an abundance of revelations and comforts from God.

First, in the case where they have already had an abundance of revelations from God. Just as after that glorious testimony was given to Christ at His baptism—*This is my beloved Son, etc.* (Matthew 3:17)—then was Jesus led aside to be tempted (Matthew 4:1). The Evangelist points out the timing of this...

and to this very purpose. In the same way, God often deals with the members of Christ regarding the timing and duration of their desertions and temptations. This was also the case for that great Apostle in 2 Corinthians 12:7: *Lest I should be exalted above measure through the abundance of revelations, a messenger of Satan was sent to buffet me.* I believe that what he calls there the "thorn in the flesh" that pricked him refers to a spiritual desertion—leaving him in distress of spirit—rather than a lust <note>original: "lust"—referring to a sinful desire or sexual temptation.</note>. For his intent <note>original: "scope"</note> is to glory in his afflictions (verses 9–10). Now, if it had been a lust, it would not have been something to glory in. Furthermore, it was a "messenger of Satan," which implies something external; and it "buffeted" him, meaning he was merely a passive recipient <note>original: "mere patient"</note> of it, just as a man being struck is passive; whereas in the exercise of lusts, our spirits are active. Besides, he prayed that it might depart, a phrase which seems to indicate something external. 

God had taken him aside into heaven and spoken wonderful things to him; and when he came down again, Satan had to take him to task and batter him. His human nature <note>original: "the flesh"</note> would have grown proud if it had not been beaten black and blue in this way. He had been in heaven and heard the language of angels and saints—*things not to be uttered*—and now he must hear the language of hell from devils. I understand this "buffeting" to be the injection of satanic thoughts <note>original: "Satanicall injections"—intrusive, distressing, or blasphemous thoughts often attributed to demonic influence in this period.</note>.

<margin>2.</margin> Secondly, before God bestows great revelations and comforts, He sometimes withdraws His presence <note>original: "desert"</note>. Just as He fills the hearts of His children with "joy unspeakable and glorious" to strengthen them against an approaching conflict before leading them into great distresses—thus God heartened His Son before that great agony in the garden,

So, on the contrary, sometimes before great revelations and comforts—to make them sweet and more welcome—God is accustomed to withdraw Himself most at those times. By doing so, He prepares the heart for them, just as physicians prepare the body for heart-strengthening medicines <note>original: "cordialls"—in the 17th century, these were medicinal drinks intended to stimulate the heart and spirits.</note>. The greatest spring tide of comfort comes in after the lowest ebb of distress. Distress expands <note>original: "enlargeth"</note> the heart and makes it thirst after comfort all the more, which makes it more capable of receiving consolation. For that rule usually holds true in 2 Corinthians 1:5 <note>original: "2 Cor. 5"—the author cites chapter 5, but the verse quoted is actually 2 Corinthians 1:5: "For as the sufferings of Christ abound in us, so our consolation also aboundeth by Christ."</note>: *That as sufferings abound, so comforts shall abound also.*

## CHAPTER 12.
### *The common cases in which God leaves His people in darkness.*

<margin>A general rule established: *God is varied in these spiritual dealings <note>original: "dispensations"</note>.*</margin>
**N**ow secondly, we come to the more *common cases* in which God distributes <note>original: "dispenseth"</note> this darkness. Before I name specific details, I will establish this general rule concerning them: we shall find that God does not constantly follow the same rule in how He distributes these experiences. Therefore, no one can say that in such and such cases, God will certainly desert people, or that He always does so; rather, He is varied in His dealings in this matter. For some people, He leaves them in darkness for a while during and immediately after their conversion; their sun rises in an eclipse and stays that way until noon—or even until their night. On the contrary, toward others, He sometimes never shines more comforts upon them than at their first con- <note>The text continues on the next page with "version."</note>

<summary>The author explains that God often uses periods of intense spiritual distress to prepare the heart for even greater comfort, establishing the general rule that God's timing and methods for spiritual desertion vary for every individual.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how God varies His dealings with believers. It emphasizes that spiritual comfort is not guaranteed for every faithful person in the same way, as it is a temporal blessing rather than an essential requirement for salvation.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>A Childe of Light</header>

...again: some He deserts upon the commission of a grave <note>original: "grosse"</note> sin; to others, He never reveals Himself more at any time than after they have been humbled by and repented for a grave sin, thereby showing the freeness of His grace. So likewise, some who have less grace and have lived more carelessly <note>original: "loosely"</note>, He fills their sails at death, and they have an abundant entrance with full sails into the *Kingdom of Christ*. Others who have walked more strictly with God—and whose deaths <note>original: "ends"</note> you would expect to be most glorious—He leaves to fears and doubts, and their sun sets in a cloud <note>This metaphor describes a believer dying in spiritual uncertainty or distress rather than in peaceful assurance.</note>.

The reasons why God is so varied in these dealings are twofold: first, because spiritual comforts do not contribute simply to *existence* <note>original: "ad esse"—to the essential being of a thing</note>, but to *well-being* <note>original: "bene esse"—to the flourishing or comfortable state of a thing</note>. That is, they are not necessary for the absolute existence of a Christian, but only for their comfortable well-being. Second, because regarding their distribution <note>original: "dispensation"</note>, they are to be categorized as temporal rewards. Although light and assurance are not *earthly* but *heavenly* blessings, they are nonetheless *temporary* blessings. Therefore, just as the promises of other temporal good things are not absolute, neither are the promises to give assurance to a believer absolute, in the way that the promises to give them heaven and salvation are.

Therefore, on the contrary, darkness and distress of conscience are only *temporal* chastisements, just as outward trials <note>original: "crosses"</note> are. They differ from outward trials only in their subject matter; the one deals with things of the "outward man" <note>The physical body or worldly circumstances.</note>, while this deals with the "inward man," namely, a person's spiritual state. Hence, in the distribution of both, although God always follows a rule—as He does in all His other dealings—He still varies and deals differently with His children in this regard, just as He does in distribut...

...shuffling outward prosperity and adversity, *setting the one against the other, so that man should find nothing after him,* as Solomon says in Ecclesiastes 7:14. He does this so that no one can say certainly and infallibly what He means to do in such and such cases. Indeed, in the world to come, He makes things even with all the world, however different His distributions <note>original: "dispensations"</note> of rewards or punishments have been here; and whatever is owed to anyone, He then pays with respect to what they have already received. 

Thus, in the matter of spiritual joy and assurance, God may grant it to one who has not feared and obeyed Him as much as someone else who walks in darkness. But then, if someone has received more "earnest pennies" <term>earnest pennies</term> <note>An "earnest" was a small sum of money paid in advance as a deposit to confirm a contract; here, it refers to spiritual joys given as a foretaste of heaven.</note> beforehand and has not lived accordingly, God considers it a worsening <note>original: "aggravation"</note> of his sin. This was the case with Solomon, whose sin is emphasized as being worse in 1 Kings 11:9–10 because he sinned against God, *who had appeared to him twice.* Otherwise, if these comforts make a person more productive <note>original: "fruitful"</note> in some proportion to such "cost," I see no reason why God—who crowns His own graces—will not reward them even more; this being one means set apart <note>original: "sanctified"</note> for some to produce more grace, just as afflictions are for others.

<margin>1 Corinthians 4:8–10.</margin>

Thus, one person with more grace—or someone whom God intends to give more grace to—shall be afflicted and forsaken, while someone with less grace "shall reign as a king," as it is said of the Corinthians in the case of worldly prosperity. <margin>2. Putting too much confidence in signs.</margin>
Or, secondly, when we put too much of our confidence in signs—even if they are true—and trust too much in comforts, former revelations, the witnesses of God's Spirit, and our own graces. These are all merely created things <term>creatures</term> <note>In this context, things created or produced by God within the soul, rather than God Himself.</note>, acts of God upon us and in us. When we allow the entire weight of our support to hang on these, God often leaves us in this state, *so that no person <note>original: "flesh"</note> should boast <note>original: "rejoyce"</note> in His presence.*

<margin>3. When we neglect going to Christ to sustain our graces.</margin>
Or thirdly, when we think graces and comforts are so rooted in ourselves that we neglect God and Christ for their maintenance, increase, and practice. Then God withdraws the light of these so that we may return to the spring and fountain-head. Just as too much confidence in the power of inherent grace <term>inherent grace</term> <note>Grace that is present within a person's character, as opposed to the external help of God.</note> caused Christ to leave *Peter* to the power of sin, a similar confidence in the power of grace causes God to leave us to the guilt and the terrors that come from sin.

<margin>Case 2. For neglecting opportunities for spiritual comforts.</margin>
The second case is for neglecting such precious opportunities for comfort and refreshment as God has granted <note>original: "vouchsafed"</note>; for example, the neglect of holy duties in which God offered to draw near to us, such as the Sacraments, Prayer, and so on. This is seen in *Song of Solomon 5:4–7* <note>original: "Cant. 5. 4, 5, 6, 7." Canticles is another name for the Song of Solomon.</note>. Christ stood at the door and knocked; that is, He moved the heart of the Church there to pray or perform a similar duty...

Thirdly, God withdraws in cases where a person does not exercise the graces they possess—not stirring them up, and so on. When Christians are, as it were, between sleeping and waking—which was the Church's condition in *Song of Solomon <note>original: "Can." for Canticles</note> 5:2*—then Christ also deserts them. To perform duties with the inner self <term>inward man</term> half awake and half asleep; to pray as if we were not praying (just as, on the contrary, we are to use the world as if we were not using it): doing the work of the Lord negligently in this way provokes God to absent himself, as he did in *Song of Solomon 5:2*. 

And so it is in *2 Peter 1:9*: *He who lacks these things*—that is, he does not use them, neglecting to add grace to grace (as the preceding words explain that phrase, which agrees with similar passages used elsewhere, such as *Matthew 25:29: He who does not use his talent <term>talent</term> <note>In Jesus's parable, a 'talent' was a large unit of currency; here it refers to spiritual gifts or abilities.</note> is said not to have it*). A blindness soon falls on such a person, and he forgets everything he ever had (as was explained previously). And indeed, there is no reason a person should have the current comfort of future grace when they neglect the *use* of their current grace. In *Isaiah <note>original: "Esa."</note> 64:7*, God complains that *there was no one who stirred himself up*, and because of this, *God was angry* <note>original: "wroth"</note>. Otherwise, as verse 5 says, *God meets him who works righteousness and rejoices in him who rejoices to work.*

<margin>4. In the case of some gross sin.</margin>
Fourthly, in the case of some gross sin committed against their conscience <term>against light</term> <note>original: "against light"; this refers to sinning while knowing better, acting against the "light" of one's own knowledge and God's word.</note>, for which they have not yet humbled themselves, or which has become public and scandalous, or even old sins long forgotten. <margin>1. Against light (conscience).</margin>

Although he was a man after God's own heart, we often find him complaining as someone who frequently experienced these spiritual desertions. He means this in regard to the sense of God's favor, *which is better than life*. This is also the meaning of the phrase "his soul clung to the dust"—that is, he was brought to the expectation of death. Therefore, in *Psalm 22:15*, Christ on the Cross (of whom the Psalm is written) cries out that *God had forsaken him and brought his soul to the dust of death*. In 1 Samuel 21:2, David very bluntly tells two or three lies together when he fled from Saul and came to Ahimelech. Ahimelech, fearing to shelter him because of Saul, asked him why he was alone; it was a suspicious thing that so great a man should have no larger entourage <note>original: "traine"</note> to attend him. David continued here in a pattern of lying; these were all fabricated and deliberate lies.

Other similar statements of his, such as in 1 Samuel 27:8, 10, occur where he told Achish *that he went against the South of Judah, and against the South of the Kenites* (verse 10), when in fact he went against the *Geshurites* and the *Amalekites* (verse 8). Some offer an excuse for this because those nations bordered the *South of Judah and the South of the Kenites*, and so they try to find a truth in his speech. Yet the last verse, which says that *Achish believed David*, implies that David meant it exactly as Achish understood it: as if he had gone up against his own countrymen. In that case, it can in no way be excused. These, therefore, being gross sins—sins "against light" <term>against light</term> <note>Sinning "against light" means acting contrary to the moral truth or "light" that one clearly understands in their own mind.</note> (as lying must be supposed to be above all other sins, because it is against the truth that rises up in the mind)—and

[Lying] is a sin in which a man's mind shows art, cunning, and wit; it is a sin that, when the truth is discovered, proves to be exceedingly shameful and scandalous. <margin>
2. In the case of some sin not thoroughly humbled for.
</margin>
Regarding the second point: in the case that *a sin is not thoroughly humbled for* and confessed; or if, when we committed it, we had excuses <note>original: "shifts"</note> to keep us from thinking it was a sin, or at least not so heinous; or if we were doubtful whether it was a sin or not, and so were reluctant to acknowledge it as a sin and burden ourselves with it in our confessions, our hearts instead trying to clear ourselves of it. This is likely what *David* did in the case of his murder of *Uriah*. He had done it so cunningly that he thought he could clear himself and wash his hands of it, or, as he said, *it was but the chance of war that cut him off; the sword devours one as well as another* [2 Samuel 11:25]. And so he excused it in 2 Samuel 11:25. In this case, God brings him to "the rack" <note>This refers to the intense psychological and spiritual distress God inflicted on David to bring him to repentance.</note> in *Psalm 32*. (It is thought that Psalm 32, as well as *Psalm 51*, was written because of that murder; and indeed it seems so, as they are tuned so closely together, as could be shown in many details.) Because these sins became known and scandalous, *David* had to confess publicly. In the end, he did so when, in writing *Psalm 51*, he stood to do penance "in a white sheet," <note>This is a metaphor for a public act of humiliation and repentance, referring to the practice where offenders stood in a white sheet before the congregation to show their shame.</note> so to speak.

# A Child of Light

Now **David** was reluctant <note>original: "loath"</note> to come to this point. Because the murder had been committed so cunningly, he could hardly be brought to confess it, even in secret, much less publicly. In this situation, God laid His hand so heavily on him that his natural moisture was **dried up**, as that **Psalm** tells us. (For in men with troubled consciences, their mental distress often throws their bodies into heats as intense as burning fevers; so in **Psalm 102:3**, during a similar episode, he says **his bones were burnt like a hearth**.) 

This continued without interruption **day and night**, and thus he lay **roaring**. This is how he describes his behavior during his torture, like a criminal on the rack <note>A torture device used to stretch a person's limbs; here it refers to David's intense psychological and spiritual agony.</note>. Although he perhaps <note>original: "haply"</note> cried out to God for mercy, because it was not with a broken heart, God considered it nothing but **roaring**—that is, the voice of a beast, as it were, rather than the voice of a man humbled for his sin. 

And why was **David** put to the rack in this way? He would not confess and humble himself for his sin: **I kept silent, and yet I roared** (verse 3). A quiet, broken-hearted confession might have spared him all this torment. But when at last **I said I would confess my sin** (verse 5), and he resolved in his heart to fully disclose that sin of murder and adultery in all their circumstances, then God pardoned him (as you know He did). For when **Nathan** came to him, he told David as soon as a single word of confession began to fall from his lips that his sins **were pardoned**. 

And yet even after that, as appears in **Psalm 51**, God did not yet **restore comfort** and the **joy of his salvation** to him (for there he prays for it because he feels its absence). He did not receive it until he had also publicly confessed and thoroughly humbled himself; it having

It illustrates how true peace and the "joy of salvation" were only restored after he moved from animal-like "roaring" to a sincere, public humiliation for his sins.</summary>

# Walking in Darkness

caused the **enemies of God to blaspheme**, God would have a public satisfaction given.

So when the incestuous person had committed that sin (1 Corinthians 5:1, 9), for which he was not yet humbled (for afterwards in 2 Corinthians 2:7, when he was **humbled** indeed, the Apostle tells them to **comfort him**), yet until his humiliation was apparent, he orders them **to deliver such a one to Satan**—to the jailer, the tormentor—and to the prince of darkness to terrify him and afflict his spirit. Now, the meaning of delivering him up to Satan was that he should be solemnly excommunicated. When this is performed as it ought to be, **In the name of the Lord Jesus** and with the **power of the Lord Jesus**, then, just as the Church cuts them off from communion with themselves, so God cuts them off from communion with Himself. He withdraws all fellowship from their spirits (as was stated before) and so leaves them alone in darkness and to spiritual desertion <note>original: "desertions"; the feeling of being abandoned by God's presence.</note>. 

Not only that, but He **delivers them up to Satan**, not with a commission to carry them on to more sin (for the purpose proposed by the Apostle was thereby to **destroy the flesh** [verse 5], not to nourish it by provoking him to more sin), but to terrify and afflict his conscience, and to stir up the guilt of sin and the terrors that come with it. God sanctifies this process to humble a person and to mortify <note>original: "mortifie"; to put to death or subdue the sinful nature.</note> the flesh. This is how Satan dealt with that Corinthian when he was excommunicated and given up to him; for in 2 Corinthians 2:7, he was nearly **swallowed up by too much sorrow**. This was brought about by Satan, **whose devices we are not ignorant of**, says the Apostle in verse 11. Now, as every ordinance <note>original: "ordinance"; a religious ritual or decree established by God, such as excommunication.</note> has a specific and peculiar work it is

# A Child of Light

appointed for; an inward effect to accompany it in a person's spirit. So this, and that proper effect, inward working, and outcome of this great ordinance of excommunication, is terror, sorrow, and desertion of spirit <note>original: "desertion of spirit"; the feeling of being abandoned by God's presence and comfort.</note>, intended to humble a person. This is just as the proper effect of Sacraments is to convey comfort and assurance, and to convey the **seal of the Spirit**. 

When this ordinance is neglected or omitted, even when gross and scandalous sins require it, then God Himself often works this way upon a person belonging to Him, inflicting this state without that formal ordinance. He dealt this way with **David** and others after gross <note>original: "grosse"; obvious, flagrant, or serious.</note> sins. God inwardly excommunicates them and casts them out of His presence, and away from all comforts in His religious practices, even though they are not refused by men to come to them. 

In this, He acts like a father who is also a public magistrate dealing with an unruly child after some great misdemeanor. Though he does not disown him, he may still send him to the **jail** <note>original: "Gaole"</note> to be imprisoned as an example. The **jailer** takes him and puts him in irons, leading him down into the dungeon where he sees no light, and into the **little-ease** <note>original: "little ease"; a notoriously cramped prison cell where one could neither stand upright, sit, nor lie down comfortably.</note>. In that place, he is in such a cramped <note>original: "strait"</note> condition that he can neither sit, nor stand, nor lie down, as **Elihu** expresses it in **Job 36:16**. He calls it **bringing them into a cramped place and binding them in fetters and cords of affliction; and then He shows them their work and their transgressions—how they have behaved defiantly (verses 8, 9).**

<margin>And thirdly, this occurs for sins committed long ago.</margin>

Yes, and thirdly, God does this not only immediately after the sins were committed, but sometimes a long while afterward, even after they have been frequently confessed. Indeed, it can happen after God has already pardoned them in our consciences <note>original: "consciences"; our internal sense of guilt and peace with God.</note>, as well as...

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how God may use the memory of past sins to humble a believer, comparing God’s discipline to a jailer's punishment, even after those sins have been forgiven.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>

Walking in Darkness.

Even though a sin is forgiven in heaven, the guilt may return again and leave us in darkness. We see this in **Job 13:26**: ***For the sins of his youth***—which he had undoubtedly humbled himself for and had assurance of being pardoned for—***yet*** God did ***write bitter things against him*** because of them many years later, and ***made him possess them***, as he himself says. God gave him over to the jailer and put him into the "little-ease" <note>original: "little ease"; a cramped prison cell designed to prevent any comfortable position.</note> in prison. ***You put my feet into the stocks***, he says in **verse 27**. 

Just as the power of sin and the **law of sin** <note>original: "law of sin"; the natural inclination toward wrongdoing that remains even in a believer.</note> is only partially removed from our physical nature <note>original: "members"</note>, so in our consciences, the guilt of sin is also only partially removed in terms of our own perception of its pardon. Therefore, just as those lusts we thought were dead—and that we thought would never rise again—sometimes revive and trouble us afresh with new assaults, the guilt of those sins we thought had long ago been pardoned may revive in the same way. After the commission of some new act, or because we have forgotten the old ones and become complacent about them, God may let those old sins loose upon us afresh so that we look at them as if they had never been pardoned at all.

<margin>The reason for all this.</margin>
Now, the reason for all these details—both why gross sins (especially those committed against the light of one’s conscience) that were not thoroughly confessed might still throw us into such fits of desertion many years later—is because in doing so, we rebel against God’s Spirit. And that Spirit deals with us as we deal with Him <note>original: "Ita nos tractat, ut à nobis tractatur"; He treats us as he is treated by us.</note>. If you grieve Him, He grieves you; if you rebel against Him, He fights against you like an enemy. So says **Isaiah 63:10**: ***They rebelled and distressed His Holy Spirit; therefore He was turned***...

<summary>This page explains how the psychological weight of "pardoned" sins can return to haunt a believer as a form of divine discipline, comparing the experience to being placed in a spiritual prison or stocks.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page that God may treat a believer as an enemy due to unconfessed or minimized sins, specifically focusing on how "sinning against the light" leads to spiritual darkness.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>

<header>A Child of Light</header>

...turned to be their enemy, and he fought against them. Now, to sin against the light is called rebellion, as in **Job 24:13** <note>original: "Job 24. 11"; the author likely intended verse 13, which speaks of those who "rebel against the light."</note>. When men go about to extinguish and darken the light of direction which God had set up in their hearts to guide their paths, God puts out the light of comfort and so leaves them to darkness. 

This happens especially when our hearts are so full of guile <note>original: "guile"; deceit or hypocrisy.</note> that we plead that they are no sins, or extenuate <note>original: "extenuate"; to minimize or make a sin seem less serious.</note> them. *David* in all likelihood did this, according to **Psalm 32**; in reference to this, he says in **verse 2** of that Psalm, *“Blessed is the man in whom there is no guile.”* And in **Psalm 51:6**, he says, *“You desire truth in the inward parts.”* *David* had dealt guilefully and deceitfully regarding that sin. If a man keeps a sin "under his tongue" <note>An allusion to Job 20:12, meaning to keep a sin secret or to savor it privately.</note> and will not be convinced of it nor bring it forth through confession, God in that case brings him to the rack—just as they do to make traitors confess. 

If any of our old sins revive and cause these terrors, it is because we began to look on them as past and gone, and thought we did not need to continue humbling ourselves for them any longer. we assume they are so buried that they will never rise again, when in fact the remembrance of them should keep us low and humble us all our days. It is a charge laid against the people in **Ezekiel 16:22** *that they did not remember that they lay in their blood.* We are apt to think that time wears out the guilt of sins, but to God they are as fresh as if they had been committed yesterday; therefore, nothing wears them out but repentance. *Great sins forgiven must not be forgotten.*

to God. This may be part of the case mentioned in **Isaiah 57:16** <note>original: "Esay 57. 16"</note>. There, God alleges the reason why he contended with a poor soul of His; He gives an account of it in **verse 17**. You shall see where the quarrel began: *“For the iniquity of his covetousness I was angry”*; that is, for some inordinate affection, which we call <term>concupiscence</term> <note>Strong desire or lust, often used in theology to describe the human tendency toward sin.</note>. He mentions not a gross act of sin committed, so much as some lust harbored, for which God began to be angry and to show the effects of that anger by striking him—perhaps with some outward <term>cross</term> <note>A trial or suffering.</note> first. *“I was angry and struck him,”* and when that did no good, God began to be more angry and to hide Himself: *“I hid my face.”* 

He speaks here of inward affliction, which He also calls in **verse 16** “contending with the soul,” and leaving it so far that the “spirit was ready to fail.” It came to inward affliction in the end. He further indicates the cause of all this: *“He went on stubbornly <note>original: "frowardly"</note> in the way of his heart.”* When lighter and outward strokes will not turn us away from sin, God leaves and deserts our spirits and wounds them. 

The reason is this: in such a case, what other course should God take? He must either give the person up to hardness of heart and leave him to his stubbornness, and so He would have lost His child—but God is resolved He will not do that. *“I will heal him,”* He says in **verse 18**. When, therefore, the heart remains stubborn under other strokes, God has no way left in His ordinary course and progress of using means but to lay strokes upon the person's spirit and wound *that*. And this yoke is likely to break and tame him, if any can; for this he cannot bear. A man's natural spirit, <term>stoutness</term> <note>Pride, resilience, or stubborn strength.</note>, and stubbornness may bear other outward afflictions—

<margin>
§. 6.
Case 6.
For deserting His truth when called to profess it.

Sixthly, in the case of deserting His truth, and not professing it or standing up for it when He calls us to do so. In this situation, He left many of the Martyrs; many of whom—especially until the reign of Queen Mary <note>Mary I (r. 1553–1558), whose reign saw the execution of many Protestants, often called the Marian Martyrs.</note> (when, along with the spread of the Gospel and its light, God also gave more strength)—even then, some deserted the truth for a while. Because of this, God deserted them in regards to their comfort. When they recovered God's favor again through repentance and a new resolution to stick to the profession of the truth, no matter what happened, *their previous desertion made them even more bold and resolute.* 

This was also partly Jonah's case. Having been given a sealed commission to go to Nineveh with a message from God, he withdrew himself and went another way. In the midst of his false security, God cast him into a whale's belly; and while he was there, God withdrew Himself from him, as if He meant never to acknowledge him again. This went so far that Jonah said in Chapter 2, verse 4: *“Then I said, I am cast out of...”*

And there is a certain fairness <note>original: "equity"</note> in God dealing with us in this way: that just as when we are **ashamed of Christ**, the fitting punishment is **that Christ will be ashamed of us**; so when we will not witness for God, there is no reason His Spirit should witness to us. And so, when we seem to evade persecution for the **Cross** of **Christ**, then it is fitting that God should confront us and take us in hand Himself—which is far worse.

<margin>
Section 7.
Case 7.
For unthankfulness for former comforts.
</margin>

Seventh, in the case of unthankfulness, and when we hold too casual a regard for the assurance and light of God’s favor <note>original: "countenance"</note>, and for our freedom from those terrors and doubts that others experience. This is a sin **Christians** are prone to fall into. For just as the light of the **Sun** is often ignored because it is so common—no one notices or looks at the **Sun** except (as it is said) when it is in an eclipse—so also, enjoying the continuous sunshine of God’s favor causes us to have only a casual regard for it. 

In this case, God withdraws those comforts and that assurance. Because these are the greatest and sweetest of all comforts, abusing them or failing to value them provokes God more than almost anything else; therefore, in this situation, God takes them away. For as it says in **Hosea 2:9**, when people were unthankful for outward mercies, God took them away and did not restore them until the people valued them more and acknowledged where they came from. God deals in the same way with spiritual assurance, light, and comfort.
