### CHAPTER XIII.

*The third general head: The purposes <note>original: "ends"</note> for which God leaves His children in this darkness. First, those purposes drawn from God and His faithfulness, etc.*

<margin>Deep calls unto deep <note>A reference to Psalm 42:7, suggesting that one profound sorrow or mystery leads to another.</note></margin>
Now let us come to those purposes which God may have in His dealing with one who fears and obeys Him, which are many and holy.

First, to show His power and faithfulness in upholding, raising up, and healing a spirit that has been long and mortally <note>original: "deadly"</note> wounded with inward terrors. This is as great an evidence of His power as any other. Therefore **Heman** <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the author of Psalm 88, which is known as the most somber and sorrowful of the Psalms.</note> says in **Psalm 88:10–11**, "*Will You show wonders to the dead? Shall Your faithfulness be declared in destruction?*" That is, in raising my soul up again to joy and comfort—which is as much as to raise up a dead man; indeed, it is even more, like raising up a soul already in hell. For the same terrors, he says, that destroy the lost, seize upon me in the same way. 

In **Ephesians 1:19**, it is said that the exceeding greatness of God’s power was seen in raising **Christ** from death to life. And wherein did the demonstration of that power principally lie? It was not simply in raising His body up again; that was no more than He did for others. Rather, in **Acts 2:24**, the power is said to be shown in this: that God, having loosed the pains <note>original: "paines," often translated as "birth-pangs," referring to the agonizing labor of death.</note> of death because it was impossible for Him to be held by it, He was raised up again. His soul was heavy unto death with terrors, and those pains in themselves were deadly—though not to Him, because since He was God as well as man, it was impossible for Him to sink under them. Now there—

<meta>catchword: fore</meta>

<summary>This page explains that God allows spiritual darkness to demonstrate His power and faithfulness by restoring a soul that feels utterly destroyed. The author argues that reviving a despairing spirit is a miracle comparable to the resurrection of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>God's power, divine faithfulness, spiritual restoration, Heman, Psalm 88, Resurrection of Christ, spiritual terrors, Acts 2:24</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of why God allows a believer's soul to experience spiritual darkness, focusing on the immense power required to heal a wounded spirit.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>Walking in Darkness.</header>

Therefore, to raise up and glorify *that* soul of his that was so *bruised*, wounded, and pierced through and through—herein lay the wonder. And such a wonder God showed in recovering **Heman** <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the biblical figure associated with the deep lament of Psalm 88.</note>. To show the greatness of this work, let us consider for a moment the depth and deadliness of this kind of distress. It is compared to the *bruising of a reed*, which once it is *bruised*, who can make it stand upright again? It is called *the wounding of the spirit*, **Proverbs 28** <note>The author likely refers to Proverbs 18:14: "A wounded spirit who can bear?"</note>, which no creature knows how to reach to heal—none but God, who is the Father of spirits; He who made them alone knows how to mend them. 

It is not only called the *sickness of the spirit*, as in **Isaiah 33:24** (where the lack of the assurance of the *forgiveness of sins* makes poor souls say, "*I am sick*"; to heal this is made the prerogative of the *Sun of Righteousness, arising with healing in His wings*, **Malachi 4:2**), but it is also called *death and destruction*. For so in that **88th Psalm**, **Heman** describes the distress that he was in. And the reason is this: God's favor is *our life*, by which we live and are upheld; therefore, when that favor is withdrawn, the soul is ready to fail and faint, to come to nothing, and to sink into destruction (**Isaiah 57:16**). 

Furthermore, the pains of those terrors are more violent and more powerful to hold us down than are the pangs of death. The wounds caused by the guilt of sin are as deadly and as strong as the lusts produced by its power, and it requires just as great a power to dissolve and scatter them. For all the strength that the Law and God's justice possess, sin also has that same strength to back it up; *For the strength of sin is the Law* (**1 Corinthians 15:56**).

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that spiritual darkness is a way for believers to share in Christ's own experiences, specifically focusing on how God conforms believers to the image of His Son through both outward trials and inward spiritual distress.</meta>

[re]surrection, so also the fellowship of His sufferings: so that the soul may be made more **conformed to Him**, as it says in **Philippians 3:10**. Just as there are sufferings *for* Christ, so there are also the sufferings *of* Christ; and God <margin>1</margin> <margin>2</margin> **makes His people** partakers of both: *persecutions from the outside,* and *terrors from within.* 

With these terrors Christ’s soul was filled when, as the text says, *He was heard in what He feared* <note>Hebrews 5:7: "who in the days of his flesh... was heard in that he feared."</note>; and *His soul was heavy even to death* <note>Matthew 26:38.</note>; and *My God, My God, why have You forsaken Me?* <note>Matthew 27:46.</note> and also **Isaiah 53**, *It pleased God to bruise and wound Him.* Now then, to conform us to His image, we who are His brothers and sisters—and are the guilty ones—must suffer somewhat in spirit just as He did, and have a portion in that as well. And therefore, as Christ suffered both inwardly and outwardly, so do many of His members. *If you have suffered with Him, you shall also be glorified with Him.* 

The sons of **Zebedee** <note>The apostles James and John.</note> wanted to be glorified in Christ's kingdom more than the rest of the Apostles. But Christ says in **Matthew 20:22–23**, *Are you able to drink of the cup that I shall drink?* He means that cup delivered to Him at His crucifixion—*Let this cup pass*—the bitter cup of God's anger. And *are you able to be baptized with the baptism I am baptized with?*—namely, outward afflictions and persecutions for the name of God. These are called *baptism* because they set God's mark on us, showing that we are God's (just as baptism seals to us that we are His); and because the Church then owns us and takes notice of us as sincere when we have believed and suffered, just as the Church receives us at baptism. Of this baptism, Christ speaks in the present tense because He was already being baptized with outward persecutions; but the *cup*, which was inward...

[in]ward affliction of his spirit; this he was to drink fully during his agony <note>Referring to Christ's suffering in the Garden of Gethsemane.</note>, which he said, "I shall drink of in the future"; this was the cup that cast him into that sweat <note>The "bloody sweat" mentioned in Luke 22:44.</note> before he even reached the bottom of it. Although no creature <note>original: "creature"; here meaning any created human being.</note> was able to drink this cup to the bottom, they might still taste it; and he tells them they should in verse 23: *“You shall drink of it,”* etc. This means they would taste inward affliction and desertion <note>The feeling of being abandoned by God.</note> as well as outward persecution; terrors within and without. All of this is to make us conformed to him, so that we come to know in part what he endured for us.

<margin>
§. 3.
3. To show the different state of God's children here and hereafter.
</margin>

Thirdly, to put a greater difference between the state of God’s children here and that which is hereafter in heaven: to this very purpose is that speech of the Apostle in **2 Corinthians 5:7**: *“That here we walk by faith, not by sight.”* He had said before that the state of believers in this life is a state of *absence from the Lord,* wherein we lack his presence and so do not enjoy the sight of him; and therefore we are to exercise faith the more. Faith is peculiar to this state, a grace given for the purpose of helping us walk while we live here. 

And though sometimes here we have some light and glimpses of him and his presence, yet *we do not always walk by sight, for we walk by faith, not by sight.* We shall have enough of the sight of God hereafter, when we shall *see him as we are seen, face to face,* and be *evermore with the Lord;* when *in his light we shall see light,* and be *satisfied with his image.* We may therefore be content to lack it here sometimes; you may well endure over-cloudings here, and sometimes that all sight should be taken away; for in the world to come there will not be one cloud for all eternity. *Your inheritance is light*

<summary>This page explains that believers experience spiritual darkness to share in Christ's sufferings and to emphasize the distinction between our current life of faith and the future eternal life of perfect sight in heaven.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, agony, Gethsemane, spiritual desertion, 2 Corinthians 5:7, faith vs. sight, heaven, light of God, inheritance</keywords>

# A Child of Light

**Colossians 1:12–13.** *Light is your portion*, but now is the seed time; and *light is sown* (**Psalm 97:11**) *for the righteous*. You must be content to let it lie underground; the longer it does so, the greater crop and harvest will come up in the end. You must endure the alternation <note>original: "vicissitude"</note> of day and night here—*sorrow overnight and joy in the morning*—for hereafter you shall have continual day and no night. This difference is established between earth and heaven to make heaven sweeter and to exercise faith. Our state in heaven is like a state of perfect and continual health; so that we may prize it, we are often *sick* here, and feelings of unease <note>original: "qualmes"</note> come over our consciences, along with fears that our sins are not forgiven. But when we arrive there, *the inhabitants will no longer be sick, for their sins shall be forgiven them* (**Isaiah 33:24**).

<margin>
§. 4. 
4. Purpose: To show the source of all spiritual comforts and our dependence on them.
</margin>

The fourth purpose <note>original: "end"</note> is to let us see the source of spiritual comforts and refreshments: that God alone keeps the keys of that cupboard <note>A metaphor for God's storehouse of grace and spiritual provisions.</note> and alone dispenses them how and when He pleases. This is so we may know (as it says in **Isaiah 45:6–7**) that it is the *Lord who formed the light and creates darkness, evil, and peace*; and that just as *affliction does not rise out of the dust* (as Job says), neither does comfort rise out of our own hearts. If we enjoyed comfort continually, we would be inclined to think otherwise. God will let us see that our hearts are nothing but darkness, and that to cause any spiritual comfort is as significant as creating light at the beginning of time. Therefore He says, *I create the fruit of the lips: peace* (**Isaiah 57:19**), and that He is the one who *commands light to shine into our hearts*, just as He first *commanded light to shine out of darkness* (**2 Corinthians 4:6**). This can be demonstrated in no fuller way than...

<meta>catchword: by</meta>

<summary>This page explains that spiritual peace is a future harvest currently "sown" in darkness and asserts that God alone is the source of all spiritual light and comfort to ensure our dependence on Him.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual comfort, seed time, God's sovereignty, Isaiah, Colossians, Psalm 97, 2 Corinthians 4, faith, darkness and light</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the purposes of spiritual darkness, specifically focusing on how God uses the withdrawal of comfort to teach believers about their total dependence on Him and to begin testing their faith.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>

<header>Walking in Darkness</header>

...by sometimes withdrawing that light and leaving us in darkness. For example, why does He sometimes assist us in prayer and "fill our sails" [with the wind of the Spirit], and at other times leave our hearts empty? Is it not so that we may learn the lesson of **Romans 8:26**: *That it is the Spirit who helps us in our weaknesses, for we do not know what to pray for as we should.*? This is a lesson we find difficult to learn, even though He sometimes restricts us <note>original: "straitens us"</note>; nor are we easily brought to acknowledge our dependence on Him for His assistance. 

In the same way, for this same purpose, He sometimes hides and sometimes reveals Himself to show that He is the immediate fountain of comfort—*the God of all comfort* (**2 Corinthians 1:3–4**)—so that we might know whom to thank, whom to depend upon, and to whom we should go for comfort. It is just as difficult for us to look away from ourselves and from created things for comfort and turn to God alone, as it is to look away from ourselves and turn to Christ alone for righteousness. By this we also see that even if we have many outward comforts, the comfort of our spirits still depends on God alone! For if **He** withdraws **Himself** in the midst of those outward comforts, they all prove to be nothing but "miserable comforters."

***

## CHAPTER XIV

### A second set of purposes: the testing and discovery of graces, especially faith.

God has other purposes <note>original: "ends"</note> in mind: to test our spiritual qualities <term>graces</term> and to bring them to light. He has the same purpose that He had when leading His people through the *great wilderness, where there was no water and where scorpions stung*...

<summary>This page explains that God withdraws spiritual comfort to teach believers that the Holy Spirit is the true source of prayer and peace. It marks a transition into discussing how spiritual darkness serves to test and reveal the strength of a person's faith.</summary>

<header>A Child of Light</header>

...them (Deuteronomy 8:16), which was to test <note>original: "prove"</note> them, and so on. God has the same purposes in allowing His people to go through this desert, barrenness, and darkness where no light is, and where the terrors of the Law sting them. For all of His dealings back then were types <term>symbolic foreshadowing</term> of God's dealings with His people now—specifically to test them and to make a trial of their hearts.

<margin>||</margin>
For the same reasons that He left *Hezekiah* to the power of sin in the matter of sanctification <term>the process of being made holy</term>—namely, *to know what was in his heart* (2 Chronicles 32:31)—He also leaves others of His children to the guilt of sin in the matter of justification <term>being declared righteous before God</term>, to discover what is also in their hearts. This is understood to have been His purpose in deserting *Job*: to show what strong patience and unconquered faith was in him.

<margin>}</margin>
There are many gracious dispositions that do not actually have the opportunity to reveal <note>original: "discover"</note> themselves except in cases of this kind of spiritual desertion. Some of the highest acts and purest fruits of grace—which are the surest evidence of the truth of that grace—would never appear except in such a state of desertion. 

For instance, it is then known whether a man loves *God* for Himself, and for those excellencies of wisdom, holiness, and goodness that are in Him, even when he does not know whether he himself will ever be the better for them or not. Then also, his obedience is manifested as pure, sincere, and unfeigned. Then his repentance is seen to be true, when he "does not repent of his repentance" <note>meaning he does not regret turning to God</note>, even when he is beyond the hope of any reward for it. Then his sorrow is seen to be godly sorrow when—even though the sentence of condemnation is read to him in his own understanding <note>original: "apprehension"</note> and conscience, and he truly thinks he is taking his leave of God forever—

But as "nature, when vexed, reveals itself" <note>original: "Natura vexata prodit seipsam"</note>—nature, when experiments <note>original: "conclusions"</note> are tried upon it and it is put out of its normal course, then reveals itself (just as anger reveals itself when a man is vexed). If you want to know the properties within herbs, you must test them through experiments; so also does God do with a man's graces, and then they reveal <note>original: "discover"</note> their most secret <note>original: "occult"</note> and hidden properties.

<margin>Especially for the trial of faith.</margin>
It would be needless to go over every specific grace; I will only more clearly give an example in that glorious grace of *Faith*. In this trial, faith deserves more attention than all other graces; and though it helps us in all the various conditions we pass through, in times of desertion <term>a state of feeling abandoned by God</term> it alone does wonders. It stands like *Samson*, encountering and conquering alone when there is no one to help. It is also the grace which is called for in the text:
> *Let him trust in the name of the Lord* (Isaiah 50:10).

> *You are in heaviness through various trials, so that the trial of your faith, being much more precious than gold...*

*...the trial of your faith, being much more precious than gold which perishes, though it be tested with fire, might be found to result in praise, honor, and glory.* <note>original: 1 Peter 1:7</note> That is, it results both to the honor of God, who is believed in, and also to the honor of faith itself, which is the most glorious grace a Christian possesses. God loves to test it so that its glory may be revealed. In the fifth verse, the apostle said that *we are kept by the power of God for salvation*. If anyone should now ask, "In what way is that power of keeping us most clearly shown?" he answers: in and through faith. *You are kept by the power of God through faith.* And if you ask, "When and in what is the power of God through faith seen most?" he points to *manifold temptations, so that the trial of your faith, etc.*

Now then, just as God would have faith tested above all other graces:

<margin>2. Of all trials, this of darkness is the greatest, for three reasons.</margin>
So, secondly, of all temptations, none test it more than the desertion of God’s countenance—this state of darkness and of terrors. Other temptations only strike at faith indirectly, but these launch a direct assault <term>original: "battery"—a military term for a continuous heavy attack by artillery</term> on our faith; for they strike at its immediate aim and object:
<margin>1.</margin>
namely, the belief [that God is a person's *God*]. These temptations speak the direct opposite of what faith endeavors to grasp, and they do so directly, not merely by implication.
<margin>2.</margin>
Furthermore, other temptations are easily endured and answered while the assurance of God’s favor remains unshaken. That assurance answers them all and shakes them off, just as Paul <note>original: "He"—referring to the apostle Paul in Acts 28:5</note> shook the viper off his hand. But when that assurance begins to be questioned (as it is in this case), who is able to stand? And what is able to strengthen a person then, except the power of faith? As *Solomon* says of the spirit of a

...man, that it will bear *all kinds of infirmities, if it itself be whole; but if it be wounded, who can bear it?* <note>original: Proverbs 18:14, "The spirit of a man will sustain his infirmity; but a wounded spirit who can bear?"</note> So I say of assurance: if it be weakened and battered, *the very foundations thereby are shaken;* a man’s <term>freehold</term> <note>original: "freehold toucht"—a legal term referring to the permanent ownership of land; here, it means one's secure claim to salvation is being challenged</note> is touched; the root is struck. For in these, a man encounters *God himself, perceived as an enemy.* God called out *Job* to test him by fighting a single combat with Satan, and Job became (as I may say) more than a match for Satan alone; then God joins the battle against him also. Now then, to bear the brunt and shock of God's wrath and yet to stand upon one's feet—this, to the utmost, proves the strength of faith.

<margin>Hosea 12:3</margin>

And this is done by faith, by the power of which (*God's power* rather supporting it) a man relies on *God* even when all God's dealings would suggest He had forsaken him. Though *God* puts on ever so angry a face and looks ever so sternly, yet faith is not <term>dashed out of countenance</term>; <note>disheartened or made to look away in shame</note> rather, it can read love in His angry looks and trust God beyond what it sees, it being the *evidence of things not seen.* <note>Hebrews 11:1</note> Then, faith goes wholly out of itself, seeing nothing in itself but merely a capacity for mercy and plenteous redemption, which it knows to be in *God.* This faith is a miracle...

## Chapter XV.
### Six More Purposes: For the Increasing of Various Graces and the Destroying of Corruptions.

Sixthly, just as this spiritual darkness serves for the trial and discovery of graces, it is also a means <term>sanctified</term> <note>set apart by God for a holy purpose</note> to increase them and to eat away at corruptions.

<margin>1. To destroy corruption.</margin>
First, it is a means to *destroy the flesh*. The incestuous man of Corinth <note>referring to the sinner mentioned in 1 Corinthians 5:5</note> was to be *delivered to Satan*—that is, to be terrified—in order *to destroy the flesh*. Just as <term>corrosives</term> <note>original: "corrasives"—medicinal substances used to eat away dead or diseased flesh from a wound</note> eat away dead flesh, so do these terrors eat away dead corruptions. The reviving of the guilt of old sins kills the seeds of those sins that remain in the heart. For if an outward affliction, which merely blocks the satisfaction of a <term>lust</term>, <note>a strong, sinful desire</note> is a means sanctified by God to kill that desire, then the inward terror felt by the conscience—which arises immediately from the guilt of a sin—must surely be an even more powerful means.

<margin>2. To humble.</margin>
Secondly, it is a means to *humble* us. So in Deuteronomy 8:16, the purpose of the Israelites being bitten by scorpions...

<meta>catchword: (which</meta>

<summary>This page explains that spiritual terrors and the loss of comfort serve to destroy sinful "fleshly" corruptions and humble the believer, comparing the process to medicinal corrosives that eat away dead tissue.</summary>
<keywords>faith, justification, King of Fears, sanctification, corruption, Corinthian, corrosives, humility, Deuteronomy 8:16</keywords>

... (which were the <term>types</term> <note>original: "types"—foreshadowing symbols or patterns</note> of these stings and terrors) were intended as much to humble them as to test them. For this purpose, Paul was <term>buffeted by Satan</term> <note>original: "buffeting by satan"—harassed or afflicted by the devil (2 Corinthians 12:7)</note>—a passage we have often mentioned—to keep him from being <term>exalted above measure</term> <note>becoming overly proud or conceited</note>. <margin>1 Peter 5:6</margin> So it is also said, *Humble yourselves under the mighty hand of God*; and if His mighty hand lies heavily in any other affliction, it surely lies heaviest in these spiritual trials.

<margin>3. To increase assurance in the end.</margin>
Thirdly, it is a means to bring you into greater assurance and <term>establishment</term> <note>original: "establishment"—becoming firm or settled in faith</note>. 1 Peter 5:10 says, *May the God of all grace, after you have suffered a while, establish and strengthen you.* He knew they could not be settled until they had suffered in this or some other way. *The tree roots itself more deeply the more it is shaken.* *Comforts abound the more that sufferings abound.* That light is clearest and strongest which arises out of darkness, because God creates it. Those things which people doubt the most, God gives the greatest evidence of in the end.

<margin>4. The fear and obedience of God.</margin>
Fourthly, it trains you to fear God more and to obey Him. Therefore, in the text, these are added as the <term>concomitant</term> <note>naturally accompanying</note> dispositions of the soul in such a case. For of all others, these qualities of fearing God and obeying Him appear most eminently and noticeably in that state. Hebrews 5:8 says, *Christ himself learned obedience by what he suffered.* Just as a yoke tames the wildness in beasts and makes them serviceable by "breaking" them, so these trials break the stubbornness of a person's spirit.

<margin>5. To pray more and more earnestly.</margin>
Fifthly, it sets believers' hearts to work, praying more and more earnestly. Just as the Apostle’s buffetings in 2 Corinthians 12 made him pray "thrice" (that is, many times), so Christ, in Luke 22:44, being in an <term>agony</term> <note>intense mental or spiritual struggle</note>, *prayed more earnestly*; and being in fears, he did lift up...

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

...offered up strong cries (Hebrews 5:7). Similarly, Heman, because of his terrors, was a man who prayed much (Psalm 88:1): *I have cried out day and night before you.* When Christians are not enjoying communion with God, if they think they have not lost Him, they often become complacent and lazy in prayer. But once they realize that their *beloved is gone*—or that they are in danger of losing Him—then they will seek Him across the whole world until they find Him (Song of Solomon <note>original: "Cant. 5. 6, 7, 8" — Canticles is an alternative name for the Song of Solomon</note> 5:6–8). They will raise a <term>hue and cry</term> <note>a loud public outcry used to pursue a criminal or recover something lost</note> after Him, just as the Church did in that passage.

---

<margin>6. To value the light of God’s favor.</margin>
Sixthly, this experience causes them to value the light of God's <term>countenance</term> <note>original: "countenance"—God’s presence, smile, or favor</note> more when they obtain it again. Consequently, they set a higher price upon it and strive to keep it by walking closely with God as *children of light*. They value it more than <term>corn and oil</term> <note>a biblical metaphor for material wealth and physical abundance</note>. In Song of Solomon 3:2, the woman loses him; but in verse 4, she finds him again, and then she holds him and will not let him go.

# A CHILD OF LIGHT WALKING in DARKNESS.

->ISAIAH 50:10<-

> 10. *Who is among you that fears the Lord, that obeys the voice of his servant, that walks in darkness and has no light?* —

## USE I.
<note>original: "USE I" — In Puritan preaching, a "Use" is a practical application of the doctrine previously discussed.</note>

<margin>To those that fear not God, nor obey him. What darkness for such!</margin>

IF those who fear God and obey Him are exposed to such a condition as has been described—if they can walk in such deep spiritual darkness—then, *who is among you that does [not] fear the Lord, nor obeys the voice of his servants?* You who live in known sins and in the neglect of known duties which God’s servants, your Ministers, tell you that you ought to perform; you who do not pray with your families, who do not exercise <term>conscience</term> <note>moral judgment or integrity</note> in your speech or your dealings, etc. Where shall you appear? *If the righteous are scarcely saved* <note>A reference to 1 Peter 4:18: "If the righteous is scarcely saved, what will become of the ungodly and the sinner?"</note>; if those whom God has loved with a love as great and unchangeable as Himself yet suffer His terrors here in this life, what shall you do? You are those whom He has set Himself to hate, and upon whom He intends to show the power of His wrath without

The "green tree" represents Christ or the righteous, while the "dry tree" represents those ready for judgment.</note> The dry tree is fitted for wrath and the fire, just as *stubble is fully dry*, as the Prophet speaks. If such a state of darkness and horror happens to those who are *children of light*, whose <margin>Colossians 1:12.</margin> *inheritance is light*, then what is reserved for you who are *darkness*, and *love darkness more than light?* 

And if this happens to them for *not stirring up the grace* which they already have, what will happen to you who are utterly devoid of it? And not only that, but you also despise and mock it. If this happens to them for *not humbling themselves for old sins*, even though they were committed long ago, what will happen to you for continuing to greedily add new sins to the old? If this comes upon them for *neglecting the opportunities to draw closer to God*, what will come upon you for neglecting the offer of grace and trampling the blood of Christ underfoot? 

All of you who think there is no hell—or if there is, that it is not as dark as it is usually described—look at *Heman* <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the author of Psalm 88, which describes a state of total spiritual despair.</note>, who was ready to go insane through terrors and to die at any moment (*Psalm 88*), even though his body was strong and his outward circumstances were healthy. Look <margin>Psalm 51.</margin> at *David* lying on the rack <note>original: "wheel"; a metaphor for extreme spiritual or mental agony, likening it to the torture device.</note>, with the Spirit of God *breaking his bones*, even though as a King he had every outward comfort he could want. 

Look at holy *Job*, in chapter 6: *“Oh, that my grief were weighed; it is heavier than the sand, and my words are swallowed up”*—meaning, I am not able to express and voice my grief. *“The arrows of the Almighty are within me; their poison drinks up my spirit. The terrors of God set themselves in battle formation against me”* (verse 4). He is so overwhelmed that in verse 8, he wishes *God would cut him off*. He says, *“Is my strength the strength of stones?”* (says

# Walking in Darkness

...he) or, "is **my flesh brass**?" <note>A reference to Job 6:12: "Is my strength the strength of stones? or is my flesh of brass?"</note> as he complains—wondering how he should be able to hold out against such fierce encounters? My brothers, God’s people find pains beyond those of kidney stones, gout, or toothaches. The falling of God's wrath on the conscience is more painful than the dropping of a little scalding rheum <note>original: "rheume"; a term for a painful discharge or fluid, once thought to cause ailments like toothaches.</note> on a tooth. And yet these pains, which **Job** and **David** felt, are but a taste of that cup which those of you who do not obey must drink down to the bottom; and it is an eternity to the bottom (Psalm 75:8). 

**"There is a cup in the hand of the Lord, and it is full of mixture"**—that is, all the bitter ingredients in the world are in it; the very essence <note>original: "quintessence"; the most concentrated or purest form of a substance.</note> of evils is strained into it. And here indeed God pours from it, as the passage continues; that is, in this life some few drops fall from the top of the cup, which his own people taste and drink. But the bottom, the dregs of it, all the wicked of the earth shall drink and drain dry <note>original: "wring them out"; to squeeze or drain every last drop.</note>—meaning they shall leave nothing behind. Instead, the vials of it, which will never be emptied, shall be poured forth even to the very last drop. 

And if God’s people begin to taste it—as **Christ** himself did, for it could not pass him by, and **Zebedee’s** sons <note>James and John, the sons of Zebedee, whom Jesus asked if they could drink from his cup (Matthew 20:22).</note> were to drink with him, as was observed—then as God says through **Jeremiah** (chapter 25, verses 27, 28, 29): **"If my people have drunk of it, and begun the round with you, and I have brought evil upon the city that is called by my name; then certainly you shall drink of it, and be drunk, and vomit, and fall, and never rise again."** 

If God’s people are thus shut up in darkness, what darkness is reserved for you? It is even as **Jude** says in verse 13: **"The blackness of darkness."** Darkness where there is **"weeping, and wailing, and gnashing of teeth"** (Matthew 22:13). It is called the "blackness of darkness" because there is not a single glimmer <note>original: "cranny"; a small opening or crack.</note> of light, nor one beam of comfort...

...comfort that shines into all eternity. And this is not for a moment, or a few years, but **forever**. You that live many days in pleasure here and rejoice in them all, "Remember the days of darkness, for they are many," says Solomon in Ecclesiastes 11:8. They are many indeed; days of an eternal night that shall know no end, and which no day shall follow.

### Application 2 <note>original: "Use 2"; a standard section in a Puritan sermon where the speaker applies the biblical doctrine to the lives of specific groups in the audience.</note>

<margin>To those who have been brought out of darkness into light, and yet have never walked in darkness in the way described.</margin>
Who among you fears the Lord and has been brought out of the state of darkness, and yet was never in this darkness of **spiritual desertion** <note>original: "desertion"; the spiritual experience of feeling that God has withdrawn his presence, favor, or comfort from a believer.</note> which I have described to you? You have been free from those terrors of conscience which are beyond all the miseries the world has—for just as the joy of the Holy Spirit is **indescribable and glorious**, so these terrors are unutterable and unbearably heavy. Yet souls that fear God, and have obeyed him more than you, have been made the **anvils** <note>The author uses the metaphor of an anvil to describe believers who are repeatedly struck by the heavy blows of spiritual despair and God's perceived anger.</note> of these pains. 

You have been coddled <note>original: "cockered"; meaning to be pampered or treated with excessive indulgence.</note>, and pampered, and fed with delicacies; you have been led into the wine cellar and have had the entire Trinity **dine with you** <margin>John 14:23; Revelation 3:20</margin>, while others have eaten **gall and wormwood** <note>A biblical expression (Lamentations 3:19) for extreme bitterness and suffering.</note>, as it says in Lamentations 3:19. 

And likewise, you <margin>Romans 5:1</margin> who—though you do not enjoy much overwhelming joy or "peace which passes all understanding"—yet being justified **by faith, you have** a solid **peace with God**. And so you walk in freedom of spirit in the use of God’s ordinances <note>The formal religious duties or rites of the church, such as prayer, preaching, and the sacraments.</note> and the performance of holy duties. Let me, based on this doctrine, give all such people this great instruction: <margin>To take notice that such a condition exists, which is useful.</margin> Take notice that these kinds of troubles do befall God’s people, far beyond what you have experienced yourself. There are many who do not think so; **Job's** friends did not, and therefore they judged him.

<summary>The author addresses believers who have enjoyed consistent spiritual comfort, warning them to recognize that other faithful people may suffer through intense periods of "spiritual desertion" and despair.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on why it is necessary to instruct believers about "spiritual desertion." The author explains that being aware of these trials prepares the soul for future hardship and fosters a deeper dependence on God.</meta>

-> Walking in Darkness. <-

him. And this is a necessary instruction.

<margin>1. To prepare them for it, should it happen to them later.</margin>
For this very knowledge prepares people for such a condition, should it happen to them. Therefore, to prepare those he wrote to for afflictions, the Apostle tells them *not to think it strange concerning the fiery trial* (1 Peter 4:12). For if trials seem strange <note>original: "strange"; in this context, it means unfamiliar, alien, or unexpected.</note> to anyone, then they are much more painful when they occur. It is like a strange disease that strikes a man—one he has never heard of before and which no physician has the skill to treat—it shocks him and makes him feel hopeless. But if he has heard that others have had it as well as himself and have recovered, this helps somewhat to lessen its bitterness. Job's trial was a "strange" trial to his friends; therefore, you see how clumsily they tried to heal it, and so they left the wound worse than they found it. So, to prepare you for it, it is good to recognize that such a condition exists. In a similar way, in 1 Corinthians 10:13, the Apostle says for the same purpose—regarding other kinds of trials—that *nothing has happened to them except what is common to man*. There is great relief in knowing that a trial is common and that others have been in the same situation.

<margin>2. To be kept more dependent on God.</margin>
Secondly, take notice of this so that you may be kept in greater dependence upon God and may fear him more while you live in this world. People who do not know of any afflictions in this life beyond what they see with their eyes and feel in their physical bodies <note>original: "outward man"; referring to the physical body and worldly circumstances as opposed to the internal soul or "inward man."</note>—nothing beyond the loss of friends and reputation—often fear God less (even if their faith is sincere). When they are called to part with any of these things for God, they are less willing, such as when they must endure

# A Child of Light

...endure a cross rather than sin, and are more inclined to *choose* affliction rather than sin, as *Job* says. But when they shall hear and know that *God's wrath is beyond Pharaoh's wrath, as Moses knew it*—who, in his vast apprehension of its greatness, cries out in *Psalm 90: "Who has known the power of your wrath?"*—then they will obey God and fear him more than they would all the kings of the earth. This is just as *Moses did, not fearing the wrath of Pharaoh* (Hebrews 11:27).

When men enjoy an abundance <note>original: "confluence"; a coming together or gathering of many things.</note> of all physical and worldly comforts, and think their "mountain is strong"—well-built with a wife and children around them, and riches, health, and honors—they think they are then safer from God's reach <note>original: "out of Gods danger"; in this context, it refers to being beyond the reach of his judgment or power.</note> than other men. They are apt to say, "*Soul, you have goods stored up for many years.*"

But know that God, without taking either your goods or your soul away, can in this life put your spirit into such a condition of darkness that you would give all the world to have a moment's ease. At that point, all other comforts shall be to you but as the *white of an egg*, as *Job* says <note>Job 6:6. Job describes tasteless food as a metaphor for his joyless life.</note>. Just as He has *joys the world does not give*, so He has *afflictions the world does not inflict*. Therefore, fear Him more than the loss of everything; obey Him rather than trying to keep everything. For God can find you in the midst of it all; thus He met with *David*, though he was a king, and then all his wives and kingdom could not comfort him until *God would heal the bones that He had broken* <note>A reference to Psalm 51:8: "Let the bones you have crushed rejoice."</note>.

<margin>3. To learn not to judge <note>original: "censure"; to judge harshly or find fault with.</note> others.</margin>

Thirdly, take notice that there are such troubles, and learn not to judge others when they are in this condition. You may walk in the light, while you see another in a dungeon; yet he may be dearer to God than you are. This was the fault of *Job's* friends, who—having no experience of such a condition in themselves...

<summary>The author warns that God's spiritual judgment can outweigh any worldly comfort or royal power, using the examples of Moses, David, and Job to illustrate the need for holy fear. He also cautions those currently experiencing "light" not to judge fellow believers who are suffering through spiritual darkness.</summary>

...themselves, and concluded he was a hypocrite; if you judge this way, then (as Asaph says) *you condemn the generation of the righteous.* <note>Psalm 73:15. Asaph is the traditional author of Psalm 73, where he struggles with seeing the wicked prosper while the righteous suffer.</note> And in this, Satan is also gratified, and the disciplined <note>original: "strict"</note> ways of grace are slandered. If God treats <note>original: "use"</note> his children this way, "Curse God and die," says Job's wife, and so say the foolish men and women of the world.

<margin>4. To fear God the more.</margin>
Fourthly, *spend your time here as foreigners in fear* and *serve him with fear* (1 Peter 1:17). *For even our God is a consuming fire* (Hebrews 12:29). <note>original: "ult."; short for the Latin *ultimo*, meaning the last verse of the chapter.</note> Keep the heart in awe with the knowledge of such a state. This kept Job in awe and made him such a disciplined man all his days. Read the thirty-first chapter throughout, and you shall see what a righteous man he was; then see the reason for it all in verse 23: *Destruction from the Lord was a terror to me.* And to the same purpose, he says in chapter 3 <note>The original text cites "Chap. 4. ult.," but the quote "that which I feared is come upon me" is from Job 3:25.</note> that *he had always feared that which had now befallen him.* The distress of his spirit was the greatest evil; this he feared might happen to him even when he had the most assurance.

<margin>5. Be thankful God has spared you.</margin>
Lastly, be thankful that God spares you. Perhaps <note>original: "haply"</note> your body is weak, and he knows you are *but flesh*, and so he *does not stir up all his wrath.* If he should fall upon you as he has on others, it would destroy you. But consider that you had a stone in your heart as well as anyone else. Has God cured it by gentle medicine <note>original: "draughts"; liquid medicine or potions.</note> and so dissolved it and carried it away, while he has performed surgery <note>original: "cut others"; referring to the historical surgery for bladder or kidney stones (lithotomy), which was agonizingly performed while the patient was "bound" or held down without anesthesia.</note> on others, binding them and putting them to much pain in taking it out? Oh, be thankful! You who are healthy and have strong bodies—are you not thankful when you see others sick and bedridden, roaring from the Stone, toothache, or gout, from which you are free? And should you not be much more thankful for the health of your spirit (cheerfulness be—

-> USE 3 <-
<note>In Puritan preaching, a "Use" is the practical application of the doctrine to the listener's life.</note>

<margin>To those who have been in darkness and are now recovered out of it. Job 33.</margin>
Who among you fears the Lord and has been in darkness, but is now out of that eclipse and walks in the light again? You who have been in the dungeon and have been set free again; you who have had the wounds of your spirit healed, your souls raised from the deepest <note>original: "nethermost"</note> hell when they drew near to the grave, and have found a ransom—learn your duty also.

<margin>1. To be thankful to God and Christ.</margin>
First, be thankful to God and *Jesus Christ*, and love him more; for you know and have tasted what he did for you. You know how bitter just a few sips of the cup were, which he drank entirely <note>original: "drank off"</note> and swallowed down; therefore, you must necessarily love him more. You also have more experience of God’s power and faithfulness, and what a miracle God has performed in raising you up again. *He has shown you wonders among the dead*, as Heman says <note>Psalm 88:10. Heman the Ezrahite is the traditional author of Psalm 88, a prayer of one in deep distress.</note>: Be thankful. Thus David says in Psalm 116:3–4, compared with the first verse: *I love the Lord, and why? The sorrows of death surrounded me, the pains of hell took hold of me. I found trouble and sorrow; I was brought low, and he helped me.*

<margin>2. To pity others in that condition. Hebrews 2:18.</margin>
<note>original: "Heb. 2. ult."; the last verse of Hebrews 2.</note>
Secondly, learn to pity others in that condition. Who can do it better than you, who have experience of the same? If you hear of any soul in distress, it is expected of you to pray for them more than for anyone else. *Christ* learned to pity us in all our <meta>catchword: infirmities</meta>

To that end, God raised you up so that you might be able to comfort others with the comfort you have received, and so you might pray for them. The author is likely referring to the promise in the following verse, Isaiah 57:18: "I have seen his ways, and will heal him... and restore comforts unto him and to his mourners."</note> says, when any poor soul is stricken, God is moved to restore him again, both for the sake of those who mourn for him and for his own sake.

You have been in hell; give warning to others so they do not end up there. *Knowing the terror of the Lord, we persuade men* <note>2 Corinthians 5:11</note>. You should tell similar stories. David says that once his "broken bones" were healed, he would then teach sinners God’s ways.

Fourth, be careful of anything that might serve as fuel for such a condition. The devil may come and throw you back into your old fits of despair if he finds the same materials to work with—such as blatant <note>original: "grosse"</note> sins, acts of impurity, lying, unfair dealings, and so on. You know what led David to his "broken bones" <note>A metaphor for the spiritual agony David felt after his sin with Bathsheba, from Psalm 51:8.</note>. Likewise, be careful not to perform your religious duties formally, coldly, or hypocritically, or to rely on them for your salvation. These are like a "hollow tooth," as Solomon says, which is broken and better out of the head than in; these can cause the "toothache" of the soul to return. Take care not to sin against the light of your conscience. If the devil found no such things in you, he would not be able to trouble you. Also, get those small, stray doubts answered; do not leave them neglected, for they may...

### APPLICATION 4.

#### *The fourth and main application, for those who fear God and yet walk in darkness.*

4. *Who among you walks in darkness and yet fears to offend* God *as much as they fear Hell, and endeavors and desires to obey him in all things as much as they desire to go to heaven?* 

When such people find God withdrawing from them and their hearts left without comfort—their spirits feeling dead and hard—they begin to question God's love and their own spiritual standing <note>original: "estates"</note>. This is especially true if they were in the *sunshine* before but now *sit in the valley of the shadow of death* <note>A reference to Psalm 23:4.</note>. If they were previously "dandled" in God’s lap <note>original: "dandled"; meaning to be held or bounced affectionately like a child.</note> and kissed, but are now whipped with terrors and his sharpest rods—hitting them in the most sensitive place, the conscience—to have their *songs in the night* <note>Job 35:10</note> turned into God *writing bitter things against them* <note>Job 13:26</note>, how bitter it is for them! 

They say that once they could never come to the throne of grace without their hearts being welcomed and their heads stroked; they rarely went away without receiving a "white stone" <note>A reference to Revelation 2:17; in the ancient world, a white stone was often a token of acquittal in a trial or a sign of special favor.</note> or an "earnest penny" <note>A small down payment or deposit given to confirm a contract; here, a spiritual assurance of future glory.</note> placed in their hands. But now *God* is a terror to them. When they finish praying or performing a similar religious duty, their hearts condemn them even more than when they began. Once, they say, they never looked toward heaven without seeing a smile; now they may cry day and night and not receive a single kind look from him. They say they once never "hoisted their sails" to perform any duty without having a fair and good wind; God

<meta>The catchword "went" indicates the sentence continues on the next page: "God went with them..."</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>Walking in Darkness</header>

...things went along with them. But now they have both wind and tide—God himself and the deadness of their own hearts—against them. In a word, God is gone, and light is gone. God *answers them neither by vision nor by prophets, neither in praying nor in hearing.* <note>A reference to 1 Samuel 28:6, describing King Saul’s desperation when God stopped communicating with him.</note> Therefore, they believe he has forsaken them and cast them off; they cry out that *he will never be merciful again.* "Oh, woe to us," they say, "we are undone!"

<margin>Hosea 5:15</margin>
*You err, poor souls, not knowing the Scriptures* <note>original: "Scriptures"; a reference to Matthew 22:29.</note> and the manner of *your God* and his dealings with his people. You are wrong to think that his mind has changed just because his facial expression has, and so you run away from him just as *Jacob* did from *Laban*. You think he has cast you off, when *he has only returned to his place so that you may seek him more earnestly.* <note>Hosea 5:15: "I will go and return to my place, till they acknowledge their offense, and seek my face."</note> Like children who start crying as if they were ruined when their mother steps away for a moment, you are always in extremes. If he shines on you, then you say *your mountain shall never be moved*; <note>Psalm 30:6.</note> if he hides his face, then you say *he will never be merciful.* <note>Psalm 77:7.</note>

Just as this is a foolish <note>original: "fond"; in the 17th century, this often meant foolish or naive.</note> and childish mistake, it is also beastly and brutish to judge in this way. I call it that because you are led by your senses; like animals, you believe nothing but what you feel and see. You measure God's love only by his looks and outward behavior. When *Asaph* did this during other afflictions, just as you do in this one, he cried out that *he was ignorant and like a beast* (Psalm 73:22). Will you trust God no further than you can see him? One day you will be ashamed to think of how much trouble your childishness caused the Spirit of God. Consider how much trouble it is for a wise man to have a foolish and naive wife who, if he is merely away on necessary business—perhaps for her maintenance— <note>original: "mainte-nance"; the text continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>The author rebukes believers who equate God's temporary emotional withdrawal with permanent abandonment, comparing their lack of faith to the shortsightedness of children or animals.</summary>

This is how you deal with God. Even though he has given you so many fair and clear evidences of his love—and these have been so often repeated and renewed—yet still you are jealous, never quiet, always doubting, and questioning everything at the least frown. It seems that either God must ruin you by letting you go on in your sinful dispositions without ever rebuking you, or else he must lose the acknowledgment of all the love he previously shown, having it called into question by your irritable and jealous misconstructions every time he expresses a small amount of anger toward you.

Worldly <note>original: "Carnall"</note> men, having other comforts, can bear the lack and absence of him well enough. ...himself (1 John 4:8–10). Our perceptions and opinions may change, and God’s outward dealings and actions toward us may change, but his deep-rooted love does not. We are not the same today as we were yesterday; <margin>Hebrews 13:8</margin> but *Christ is the same yesterday, today, and forever*. To say that he has cast you off because he has hidden his face is a logical error <term>original: "fallacy"; a false or misleading argument.</term> drawn from the devil’s *Topics* <term>original: "Topicks"; a reference to Aristotle’s work on logic, suggesting the devil uses deceptive reasoning.</term> and is insulting to him. For Isaiah 54:8 says: *“In a little wrath I have hidden my face for a moment, but with everlasting kindness I will remember you.”* 

First, I have only *hidden my face*, not cast you out of my mind. Second, although it is *in anger, it is only a little anger*. Third, it is *not for long, but only for a moment*. Fourth, all that while *I am not forgetful of you; I remember you,* and so on. Fifth, *this is done with kindness that lasts from everlasting to everlasting.* When the sun is eclipsed—an eclipse that affects the earth’s view rather than the sun itself, which continues to shine as it always did—foolish people think it will never regain its light, but wise people know it will.

<margin>Objection.</margin>
But you will say: if this desertion were only for a moment, it would be one thing; but mine has lasted for many years.

<margin>Answer.</margin>
How many years? This life is but a moment, and God has an eternity of time to show his love in—time enough to make amends for a few frowns with *everlasting kindnesses*. Remember the text says, *One who fears God may [walk] in darkness*; not just for a step or two, but for many weary turns within it. <margin>Psalm 77</margin> Heman was afflicted from his youth; David was afflicted so long that he thought God had forgotten mercy, asking, *“Does his promise fail forever?”* Remember what is said in another case, Luke 18:8, that though he...

God waits long, yet he comes speedily—that is, though he seems slow in our eyes, he is quick in his own eyes. He has all time before him and knows exactly how much time remains to be spent in embraces with you.

It was the same for Job (Job 13:24): *“Why do you hide your face and consider me your enemy?”* So too in Isaiah 63:10 and Psalm 88:16. All these are merely the effects of a temporary anger. There is a wide difference between being a *child under wrath* and a *child of wrath*. You may be a child under anger without being a child of anger <note>A "child of wrath" is one who is naturally an enemy of God, whereas a "child under wrath" is a beloved child currently experiencing God's discipline.</note>. Just as God may afflict your finances and your body, he may also afflict your spirit as a Father; for Hebrews 12:9 says *he is the Father of spirits.*

### Ten Directions for those who are more deeply troubled: and the means to be used to recover light and comfort.

For the sake of those who are so deeply troubled, I will prescribe some directions for how they should behave in such a condition, so they may emerge from it more comfortably and more quickly. These long and great sicknesses of the soul are like those of the body: people are kept in them longer for lack of the right directions and prescriptions, just as we see with long-lasting agues <term>original: "agues"; fevers or shivering fits, often occurring in cycles.</term> and fevers and similar diseases.

### Direction 1.

<margin>Be careful to avoid rash, impatient, and unbelieving speeches and wishes.</margin>
First, be careful of rash, desperate, impatient, and unbelieving speeches and wishes; you will eventually be forced to take these back with sorrow. For example, when *David* was in fear, he uttered <insert>a</insert> desperate speech—specifically, that *Samuel’s* prophecy concerning him, and the message sent to him *from God* that *he* should be King, would prove false. He said not only that *one day he would perish by the hand of Saul* (1 Samuel 27:1). (The reason for this speech was that he found himself in danger of his life every day; and so, even though God had preserved him again and again, he thought that some of those many arrows constantly shot at him—which had so narrowly missed him—might eventually hit and kill <note>original: "speed him"</note> him; it seemed a miracle otherwise.) But he says further in Psalm 116:11, *“I said in my haste, all men are liars,”* meaning the Prophet *Samuel* and everyone else. He thought it was merely the promise of a deceptive <note>original: "vaine"</note> man; but he soon corrected himself and added, *“I said this in my haste.”*

So likewise in Psalm 31:22, *“I said in my haste, I am cut off.”* As he admits, these were rash speeches spoken in panic. In the same way, many poor souls cry out—even after they had strong hopes at their first conversion that a kingdom is theirs, that heaven is theirs, and that it is reserved for them, and they are kept for it through the power of God. But (good souls) you who are in trouble: oh, beware of such impatient wishes or speeches—such as saying that all you have experienced was nothing but hypocrisy, or crying out, "Oh, that God would cut me off! This is "the crime of treason" <note>original Latin: *crimen læsæ Majestatis*; literally "injured majesty," referring to an offense against the dignity of the sovereign.</note>.

It is the same when you deny God’s handwriting in your own hearts—where He has written joy, fear, love, zeal, and more by His Spirit—and say, "It does not look like His hand."

So if you deny the seal of the Spirit after he has sealed you for the day of redemption, and say that all the down payments <note>original: "earnest penies"; a small deposit paid to confirm a contract, here referring to early experiences of God's grace</note> of heaven are only worthless tokens <note>original: "counters"; metal pieces used for counting or as imitation coins</note> or fake gold <note>original: "alchimy"; base metals treated to look like gold</note> and worth nothing—in doing so, you take his name, his love, and his mercy all in vain. Indeed, you lie against the Holy Spirit, as the Apostle said in another context <note>referring to Peter’s rebuke of Ananias in Acts 5:3</note>. Thus, although God gives you full permission to test and examine all his gifts <term>graces</term> in you and his dealings with you, you should not desperately—at the first glance—say they are not true gifts or that he will never be merciful, just because of a small mistake or flaw. You mistreat him when you do so; be careful of it.

### *Direction 2.*

<margin>To perform a diligent search and examination.</margin>
Secondly, let the troubled soul *make a diligent search*. Establish an investigation in your heart. This is what David is said to have done when he felt abandoned <term>desertion</term> in Psalm 77, verse 6: *I spoke with my own heart and conducted a diligent search.*

<margin>Two things to be searched into.</margin>
Now, in this search, inquire into two things.
First, what might be the true cause that provokes God to leave you and hide himself from you?
Secondly, what is the main doubt, objection, reasoning, or fear <term>apprehension</term> in your own heart that makes you afraid and causes you to question everything?
These are two distinct things. For even though God has a just cause and reason to leave us in this trouble, the thing that actually worries and disturbs us is often a mere mistake or misunderstanding. It is like a father who sees a good reason to frighten his child; yet the object the father allows him to be scared by...

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword is "with."</meta>

<summary>The author warns believers not to dismiss their past spiritual experiences as false during times of doubt, as this dishonors God. He advises a "diligent search" to distinguish between the actual reasons God might withdraw His presence and the personal misunderstandings that cause the believer fear.</summary>

...with, is nothing but a mere bugbear <note>original: "bugbeare"; an imaginary object of fear, used here to describe a groundless worry</note>. It is necessary to inquire into both.

<margin>1. What is the true cause that provokes God to leave you in this distress.</margin>

First, examine what might be the true cause that provokes God thus to leave you. As Lamentations 3:40 says, *Let us search and test our ways*; this was spoken by the Church during a time of abandonment <term>desertion</term>, as appears from the earlier part of the chapter. To help yourselves in this, go over all the cases which have been proposed: have you been carnally confident <note>original: "carnally confident"; relying on human logic, physical feelings, or outward appearances rather than spiritual faith</note> in false signs, or rested too much on true ones, to the neglect of Christ and God's free grace? Did you not previously neglect to stir up your own graces? And so on. Go over all those cases mentioned; something or other will be found to be the cause. 

This is necessary, for until the cause is known, the heart does not submit; neither will it sanctify God’s name, nor will the trouble cease until that which provokes God to inflict it is confessed and forsaken. And if it is a particular sin that God aims at, then usually God uses the horror of—and the guilt from—that very sin to afflict you. Then that sin itself is made the cause of your trouble in your own perception <term>apprehension</term>. In that case, it is easily found out; you will find your sin to be the thorn in your foot, the stone in your shoe, that grated, galled, and vexed you. 

David easily knew in Psalm 51 what it was for which God "broke his bones." His very sin was the iron mace <note>original: "iron Mace"; a heavy, blunt weapon used to crush armor</note>—the instrumental cause itself of God executing judgment upon him. God used the horror of that murder <note>referring to King David’s orchestration of the death of Uriah the Hittite</note> as the hammer to break him and as the rod to whip him, as seen in verse 3: *My sin (he says) is ever before me*; it was always in his sight. Indeed, in outward afflictions, it is more difficult to find out the cause why God [afflicts]...

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how a person in spiritual "darkness" can identify the cause of their distress, moving from identifying the sin itself to examining the faulty logic that leads to despair.</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

...afflicts a man. Unless, sometimes, through God’s wise and disposing hand, you may find and read the sin within the punishment because they resemble one another so closely. A man might say, "this cross <note>original: "cross"; a burden or suffering</note> lay in the womb of such a sin," they are so similar: *we are punished in the very thing through which we sin* <note>original: "in quo peccamus, in eodem plectimur"</note>. But in those inward distresses of conscience, God often uses the very guilt of the sin that is the true cause and which moves Him to afflict you to terrify you. To cast a man into distress and keep him there, that sin is both the primary cause <term>pro-catarcticall</term> <note>original: "pro-catarcticall"; an old medical or philosophical term for an initial or predisposing cause</note> and the executioner as well.

But if you cannot find the cause—as Job (it seems) did not, and as Elihu supposed he might not—then follow the counsel Elihu gives him until God shows you the cause. Job 34:31–32 advises saying to God, *"That which I do not see, teach me; and if I have done iniquity, I will do no more."* And if you do find the cause, say also, as in verse 31: *"I have borne chastisement for such a sin, I will never offend anymore."* Until then, God will not let you go <note>original: "let thee downe"; here meaning to release from the trial or lower the pressure</note>.

<margin>
2. What is the main reasoning in your heart that causes you to question your spiritual state?
</margin>

The second thing to investigate is this: What is the chief and main reasoning in your heart that makes you call everything into question and doubt whether God is your God? What is the reason you think this? What leads you to that conclusion?

For this, you must consider that although God hides Himself from you, terrifies you, and lashes your conscience because of some sin committed, the thing that causes this specific feeling <term>apprehension</term>—that God has cast you off—is usually some false reasoning or misunderstanding. It is often a mere mistake, or a device and sophistry <note>original: "sophistry"; clever but false arguments</note> of Satan.

<summary>The author explains that while specific sins are often the cause of spiritual distress, the feeling of being "cast off" by God is frequently rooted in the false logic or "sophistry" of Satan. He advises readers to pray for God to reveal their hidden sins while they endure these trials.</summary>

<keywords>Job, Elihu, conscience, spiritual desertion, pro-catarcticall, punishment, Satan, sophistry, repentance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how a person in spiritual "darkness" can identify the cause of their distress, moving from identifying the sin itself to examining the faulty logic and "tricks" used by Satan to induce despair.</meta>

# Walking in Darkness

<sig>A a</sig>

When the *Corinthian* <note>Referring to the man in the church at Corinth who was disciplined for sin, as described in 2 Corinthians 2.</note> was excommunicated for his sin, *Satan* was permitted to terrify his conscience for it. But *Satan* went further; he wanted to *swallow him up with sorrow* by persuading him that such a sin was unpardonable and that God would never claim him as His own again. Now, the reasoning *Satan* used to bring this upon him was a false one—a certain trick and *device* (2 *Corinthians* 2:7, compared with verse 11). If a person is ignorant of these tactics, they may go on mourning for a long while as if they were a castaway.

Therefore, take your soul aside and seriously ask it, and examine it: *Why is it thus troubled? What reason, what ground do you have to think that God is not your God?* And then examine whether that ground is true or not.
<margin>1 Peter 3:15</margin>
Just as the Apostle tells us to *give a reason for our faith*, so you should ask of your soul *the reason for its doubting.* This is what *David* did in *Psalm 42:5: "Why are you cast down, O my soul?"* and because doubts arise again and again, he asks the reason again in *verse 11*: *"Why are you cast down?" David* knew that the way to dissolve these doubts was to search into and examine the reason for them. For whenever he had thoroughly examined them, he found them needless and groundless—not enough to put him into such desperate fears. 

The child of God is often cast into prison—into fears and bondage—and after he has lain in them for a long time and begins to read over the legal writ and the *warrant for commitment* <note>original: "Mittimus"; a Latin legal term for a warrant issued by a magistrate to commit a person to prison.</note>, he finds it to be false imprisonment, a mere trick of *Satan* his jailer. For just as carnal <note>original: "carnall"; here meaning people who are worldly or not yet spiritually reborn.</note> men, when they think their spiritual state is good and that they are in the favor of God, rely on some delusion or false reasoning as the basis of their opinion—such as, because they prosper in the world, therefore God... 

<summary>This page explains that spiritual despair is often caused by the "tricks" of Satan rather than reality, urging believers to logically examine the reasons for their doubts just as David did in the Psalms.</summary>
<keywords>Satan's devices, spiritual doubt, 2 Corinthians 2, Psalm 42, Mittimus, false imprisonment, spiritual examination</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the false reasoning that leads to spiritual despair, contrasting the false confidence of the hypocrite with the groundless fears of the believer.</meta>

# A Child of Light

[The hypocrite believes God] loves them because they perform certain duties or have some good impulses <note>original: "motions"; internal spiritual stirrings or feelings</note>—grounds they cannot endure to have examined. Conversely, when someone who fears God feels they are out of His favor, that feeling is likewise based on false reasoning. When examined, this reasoning is shown for what it is; and once it is exposed, the soul is freed from its fears and doubts.

<margin>Psalm 88:14</margin>
*Heman* <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the author of Psalm 88, a prayer of one in deep distress who feels abandoned by God.</note> thought and said that God had cast him off. What reason persuaded him to think so? Verse 14 says it was *because God had hidden His face.* But it does not follow, *Heman*; a father may hide his face from his son and yet not cast him off.

So *David* also reasons in Psalm 77:2–3: *"I have sought God, prayed, and used the means, and yet I am troubled; and yet God does not reveal Himself."* What does he conclude from this? In verse 7, he asks, *"Will the Lord cast me off forever?"* He thought, "If God loved me, He would have heard me immediately" <note>original: "presently"</note>; he thought his soul would not have felt worse after praying. This was false reasoning, for in Psalm 70:4 <note>The author likely refers here to the sentiment found in Lamentations 3:44 or Psalm 80:4, regarding God "covering himself with a cloud" so prayer cannot pass through, or being "angry against the prayer of his people."</note> we see that *sometimes God shuts out His people’s prayers.* A father may sometimes seem so angry that he may throw away his child’s petition, and yet still resolve to be his father.

It would be an endless task to count all the false reasonings that souls in distress have. Sometimes these come from a passage of Scripture that is misunderstood and misapplied. For example, some people who are troubled by blasphemous thoughts against God, Christ, and His Spirit—even though these thoughts are their greatest affliction—have thought they have sinned against the Holy Spirit. They do this by misapplying that passage in Matthew 12:31: *"That blasphemy against the Holy Spirit shall never be forgiven."* However, that passage is meant only...

<summary>The author explains that spiritual despair often stems from "false reasoning" and the misapplication of Scripture, using the biblical examples of Heman and David to show that God hiding His face or delaying an answer to prayer does not mean He has abandoned the believer.</summary>
<keywords>Heman, David, Psalm 88, Psalm 77, false reasoning, spiritual desertion, unanswered prayer, blasphemy against the Holy Spirit, Matthew 12:31</keywords>

...but of one specific kind of blasphemy, which is indeed a willful blaspheming of God and the work of His Spirit out of a spirit of revenge (*Hebrews 10:29*). 

Likewise, because some have sinned after being enlightened and tasting [the heavenly gift] and have fallen into some gross sin, they think they shall never be restored <note>original: "renewed"</note>. They base this on that passage in *Hebrews 6:4*. However, the author there is speaking of a willful and revengeful falling away—a state of mind so full of vengeance that they would, if they could, *crucify Christ again* (verse 6). For otherwise, *David* would never have been restored, for he sinned 

<margin>*2 Samuel 12:9*</margin> 

presumptuously and *despised the commandment.* 

Similarly, if some hear of a frightening example of **God's** severe dealings with others—how He rejected <note>original: "cast off"</note> someone for a particular sin, as He did with *Saul*—they conclude that because they committed a similar sin, God has rejected them as well. But there is no certain ground for such a thought; for *secret things belong to God* <note>A reference to Deuteronomy 29:29: "The secret things belong unto the Lord our God: but those things which are revealed belong unto us..."</note>. 

Also, because some hear that there is a time after which God sometimes offers grace no more, but instead "swears against" certain men <note>A reference to God's oath in Psalm 95:11, that the rebellious would not enter His rest.</note>, they assume that their time for mercy is also past. They can have no ground for this conclusion. Though it is true that God does this with many who hear the Gospel, the Word of God gives us no certain rules to judge that He has done so to any of us specifically. It is good to fear so that you do not provoke Him to it, but you have no sign to fear that He has already done so with you. 

Indeed, herein lies the main and first task in helping <note>original: "raising up"</note> a troubled soul: to find out the basis of their doubting, examine the truth of it, and disprove <note>original: "confute"</note> it. If a man is falsely imprisoned or loses a lawsuit <note>original: "cast in a suit at Law"</note>, what does he do to remedy it? He seeks to find an error in the <term>writ</term> <note>A formal written order or legal document.</note>. In the same way, you must search out the basis

<page-num>172</page-num>
### A Child of Light

...of your trouble: go to some spiritual lawyer skilled in soul-work <note>A term for the inner struggles and processes of the human soul in relation to God; someone experienced in spiritual counseling.</note>; do not keep the devil’s counsel, for he opposes nothing more than making your doubts known.

->**Direction 3**<-

<margin>
3. To consider as impartially <note>original: "indifferently"</note> what may work in their favor as much as what may work against them.
</margin>

The third direction I give to such people is that they keep and lend an ear as well to hear and consider what makes for their comfort, as to what may make against them.

This direction addresses a great weakness of those in distress, who through Satan’s temptations have their hearts so deeply filled with biased <note>original: "prejudicial"</note> ideas about the misery of their condition. Just as the people of God in Exodus 6:9—through the anguish of their hearts—were so mentally disturbed that they did not listen to the good message Moses brought them, nor believe that such good news could be true for them, so are the souls of many who are in distress. They are so filled with anguish and a sense of misery, and so strongly biased with desperate opinions, and so far beyond hope, that they reject everything spoken for their comfort. 

Consequently, they will not even be brought to cast an eye or a thought upon anything that might be an occasion of comfort to them. They are like some prisoners at the bar: because of extreme fear, they cannot read the evidence in their hearts or in the Word that might favor them. Tell them what God has done for them and in them as evidence of His love, and just as they often cannot, so they often will not read those signs. Or, if they do, they read them only as a man reads a book he intends to refute <note>original: "confute"</note>: they pick quarrels and make objections to everything that is said.

<summary>The author advises those in spiritual despair to seek experienced counsel and to try to view evidence of God's love as impartially as they view their own fears, rather than reflexively rejecting comfort.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual counsel, despair, comfort, Exodus 6:9, soul-work, spiritual lawyer, Satan's temptations</keywords>

...as if they were hired as lawyers to plead against themselves, and to find flaws in their own evidence of grace <note>original: "evidences"; the signs or proof that a person is saved or has God's favor</note>. They have even been glad if they could raise an objection that might puzzle those who have come to comfort them, as if they were holding an argument against themselves and disputing only for the sake of victory. 

Through much obsessing over <note>original: "poring upon"</note> and considering only what might work against them, the bolts of their hearts have been shot so far into despair and fixed in desperate sorrow. Furthermore, the true internal parts <note>original: "wards"; the circular ridges inside a lock</note> of sound evidence have been so twisted and forced by false keys that when the most skillful and strongest comforters have come with true keys to slide back the bolt, the lock would not turn; indeed, they could scarcely even get the key into the entrance.

This was David's weakness <note>original: "infirmity"</note>, as seen in the tenth verse of the 77th Psalm compared with the second verse: *My soul refused to be comforted.* He spilled all the healing drinks <note>original: "cordials"</note> and medicine <note>original: "physick"</note> that were brought to him; he was not only empty of comfort but actively refused it. "What? Bring me promises to comfort me?" such a person will say. "You might as well carry them to someone in hell, or give medicine to a man past recovery." And so, they will take nothing that is given to them. 

This was also the case with the Church in the third chapter of Lamentations, verses 17 and 18. Her heart was deeply possessed with a desperate apprehension: *My hope* (she says) *has perished from the Lord.* And what was it that drove her soul into such a fixed despair? Verse 17 says: *She forgot all good.* She forgot—that is, she would not even take into consideration—

By this he means: although God deals harshly with me, as you see—and even though the practice of grace is hidden, the sunshine of God’s favor is withdrawn, His face is hidden from me, and the joyful fruits of righteousness and the vibrant, "green" speeches and leaves you once saw on this now-withered trunk have fallen off—yet **the root of the matter** remains in me. 

<margin>+</margin> This is a root of faith that does not decay, a constant state of grace that still remains, which hates sin and loves God; and you will never be able to beat me away from that truth.
