# Walking in Darkness

<margin>Isaiah 66:11</margin>
Are the breasts of consolation milkless? Yet suck again; comfort may come in the end. If, after you have empanelled a jury and a grand inquest to search your soul, their first verdict condemns you, or they bring in a verdict of "we do not know" <note>original: "ignoramus"; a legal term meaning "we do not know," used by a jury when they find insufficient evidence to proceed with a charge</note>, yet do as wise judges often do: send them back to consider it again. They may find evidence the next time. It seems Jonah looked once and found no comfort (**Jonah 2:4**), for he said, "*I will look again toward your holy Temple.*" A person's heart is like those two-faced pictures <note>These refer to anamorphic or "perspective" paintings popular in the 17th century, which showed different images depending on the angle from which they were viewed</note>: if you look at them from one side, you shall see nothing but the horrid shape of a devil or something similar; but go to the other side and look again, and you shall see the image of an angel or a beautiful woman. Similarly, some people have examined their hearts for signs of grace at one time and, to their thinking, found nothing but hypocrisy, unbelief, hardness, and self-seeking. But not long after, when examining their hearts again by the same signs, they have seen the image of God drawn clearly upon the tablet of their hearts.

->Direction 5.<-

<margin>5. To renew one's faith and repentance.</margin>
But now, if the memory of former signs brings you no comfort—if the waves that wash over your soul prove so deep that you can find no bottom to cast anchor on, and the storm and stress are so great that no cable will hold, but all snap apart (as is often the case for many a poor soul):

Then, fifthly, take and put into practice this fifth direction: **renew your faith and repentance**. Set your heart to work, to believe and repent afresh, as if you had never yet begun. Do not spend all your time in casting...

<summary>The author encourages those in spiritual doubt to persist in self-examination, comparing the heart to a perspective painting that reveals God's image only when viewed correctly. If looking for past grace fails, the author advises "renewing" one's faith and repentance as if starting for the first time.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "fifth direction," advising the believer that when they cannot find evidence of past grace, they should stop arguing about their history and instead begin the process of believing and repenting anew.</meta>

<header>A Child of Light</header>

<margin>
It must be by the <unclear>spirit</unclear> of the Spirit of God
</margin>

...anchor, but instead start pumping. Stop and cease for a while to reason about the goodness of your former faith and repentance, and set to work on believing and repenting anew. Say, "Well, suppose I have not been in a state of grace until now; I am still capable of receiving it in the future; I may yet obtain grace." Suppose my faith and repentance have not been true up to this point; I will therefore now begin to strive for that which is true. To that end, use whatever flaws the devil finds in either to guide you in what to mend and rectify for the future. Begin to repair the breaches and the instability that has been discovered; strive to provide for all those deficiencies he claims exist in either. Patch all the holes he picks. 

Say, "Lord, I cast myself upon your mercies anew. I desire now to make my heart perfect before you for the time to come—to part with every sin, to submit to every duty, to condemn every secondary motive <note>original: "by-end"; a selfish or ulterior motive</note> to hell, and to set up God and Christ as my target, north star, and goal in everything." And once you have done this, let the devil say his worst. 

Of all the previous directions, I recommend this one to you as a special means to dissolve and end these temptations concerning assurance. I mark it as "it is proven" <note>original: "probatum est"; a Latin phrase used in early medicine and recipes to indicate a remedy has been tested and found effective</note>; take it and practice it, for it is a tried method. It is the same course that the Church eventually takes when in a state of spiritual desertion: *Lamentations 3:40, "Come, let us test our ways, and turn back to the Lord."* That is the final path she follows. 

Now, when the water is at its lowest and the tide of assurance has ebbed, repair your riverbanks as people do at low tide. Now, when nothing but hypocrisy, unbelief, and falseness of heart appear to you to be within you, you should groan...

<summary>The author advises those struggling with a lack of spiritual assurance to stop debating their past state of grace and instead focus on renewing their faith and repentance immediately. By treating spiritual low points as opportunities to "repair their banks," believers can move past the devil's accusations.</summary>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the "fifth direction," advising those in spiritual darkness to stop obsessing over past evidence of grace and instead focus on performing new acts of faith and repentance.</meta>

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>Walking in Darkness.</header>

So, endeavor after the opposite sincerity, and let Satan say his worst. I prescribe this direction to you next because, in times of temptation regarding assurance, it is the usual course for some troubled souls to spend all their thoughts on what they previously had, as if they must find comfort only from their past work or not at all. They spend all their time and expense on new lawsuits and new trials <note>original: "new suits & new trials"; legal terminology comparing the search for spiritual assurance to a courtroom battle over property titles.</note> concerning their former title to grace; and even when they have been defeated again and again, they still do nothing but reread old evidence <note>original: "evidences"; legal documents proving ownership, here referring to past experiences of grace.</note> over and over, and bring in and study new proofs.

But know that, although this is to be done and not neglected—and is often found to be comforting, which is why I encouraged it in the previous direction—you are not only to take that course. You should not look back so much at your former faith and repentance that you forget to practice new acts. But performing new acts of faith and repentance is the most direct way, the shortest route, and requires just as little effort. You can just as easily get a new lease renewed as prove that the old one is still valid. It will require many legal terms to examine all your evidence again and again, which perhaps is also blotted and blurred. You can cut the knot and resolve the temptations sooner through new faith than by untying it with reasonings and arguments. And the truth is, in the end you must come to this, for God's great purpose in delaying <note>original: "deferring"</note> comfort is to lead you to renew your faith and repentance: "Unless you are converted" (as Christ says to his Apostles), you must be converted, as it were, anew. He will not deliver you out of the dungeon until you enter into new bonds and bail <note>original: "bonds and baile"; legal terms for financial guarantees or security provided for a prisoner's release.</note> for your good behavior.

Therefore, begin to do it soon. And whereas you might think that by doing this you may prejudice <note>original: "prejudice"; here meaning to harm or invalidate the legal claim to his salvation</note> your former title <note>original: "title"; legal terminology referring to one's claim to grace and salvation</note>, that is not my meaning—as if you should utterly give up your old faith and repentance as counterfeit. "I will keep my integrity," says Job (Job 27:5). My advice is only to pause and cease pleading your case <note>referring to the ongoing "lawsuit" over whether one is truly saved</note> for a time, and instead begin to renew it. That is what I am encouraging you to do. And then the comfort of your old repentance will return. As the Apostle says of the Law, so I say of your former title: it is not *destroyed* but rather *established* by this (Romans 3:31). 

And as Christ says in John 7:17, *"If any man will do his will, he shall know of the doctrine, whether it be of God."* Just as the best way to know the truth is not to spend all your time disputing about it but to practice it (which puts an end to controversies in men's hearts), so I say: to know the truth of your former grace, and so to come to have the comfort of it, is to add to these reasonings the practice of believing and repenting anew. This baffles the devil exceedingly and gains an advantage over him. By this, the lawsuit is moved to a different court <note>original: "the suit is removed"; a legal term for transferring a case to another jurisdiction, thereby resetting the legal process</note>, all his old pleas are dashed, and he is forced to provide a new reply. It diverts the war and indeed nonplusses <note>original: "non-pluffeth" (OCR for non-plusseth); to be at a complete loss or stuck, unable to proceed with an argument</note> him. For what can he say to it? He must now prove that you are incapable of grace and that you shall never repent—which no one in all the world, nor the devils in hell, can prove. And yet, if in this case he tells you (as he usually does) that all will be in vain in the time to come, just as it has been in the time past:

<meta>This page continues the "fifth direction" for those experiencing spiritual desertion, urging the reader to persist in faith and religious duties regardless of their feelings or fears of damnation.</meta>

...ever nonplussed. Job vows he will trust Him though He kills him (Job 13:15). So you should do: whether He will damn or save you, whatever He does with you, do not stop casting yourself upon Him for mercy, nor stop serving and loving Him.

Go on using the means <note>original: "the meanes"; referring to the "means of grace" such as prayer, scripture reading, and church attendance</note> diligently and constantly. Be even more diligent: continue to fear and hate sin, and pray day and night, as Heman did when he thought himself cut off (Psalm 88:1): *"I have cried day and night,"* even though *"I am like one whom You remember no more"* (verses 4–5). Similarly, at the ninth verse: *"I have called daily,"* even though in verses 10, 11, and 12, he thought himself in hell and thought it would be a miracle to ever be raised up again. Yet, he says in verse 13, *"But I have cried to You."* That "But" seems to be an expression of his resolution up to that point—that although these were his perceptions of his condition, he nevertheless had sought the Lord and would continue to do so.

Suppose you find no relish <note>spiritual pleasure or satisfaction</note> in the ordinances <note>religious duties and rituals like preaching or the sacraments</note>; use them anyway. Even if you are desperately sick, you must still eat; take all that is brought to you, for some strength comes from it. Say to yourself: "Whether I am damned or saved, a hypocrite or not a hypocrite, I am resolved to go on."

And there is good reason for this: for if you should stop serving the Lord and resolve never to look for Him again, then you are certain to be damned. If there is an "evil heart" in you that departs from the living God, you are ruined. But by this other way—seeking Him—you may prevail in the end. Who knows? Perhaps God will be merciful. In Lamentations 3:29, this counsel is given to a soul that is burdened <note>original: "yoked"</note> and deserted: *"to put his mouth in the dust"* <note>an expression of absolute humility and submission to God's sovereignty</note>. He waits, the author says, and suffers, and does anything, not only if he *has* hope, but *if there might be hope*—if there is even the slightest thought that it might turn out well,

<meta>This page continues the "fifth direction" for those in spiritual darkness, encouraging a "holy venture" or risk-taking faith. It picks up from the previous discussion on the necessity of persisting in religious duties despite feeling hopeless.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

He will take a risk <note>original: "make a venture"</note>, and so should you. See what Esther did in a similarly desperate case: she thought, "If I hold my tongue (and so Mordecai told her), God will destroy me." It was better to take a risk and speak to the King, putting her life in her hands; and therefore she says, *"If I perish, I perish"* <note>Esther 4:16</note>. 

So too with the lepers; see how they reasoned in a desperate case (2 Kings 7:3–4): *"If we go into the city, we are sure to die, for the famine is there; if we sit still, we die also. Come, let us go over to the camp of the Syrians <note>original: "Assyrians"; the biblical account in 2 Kings 7 refers to the Syrians (Arameans), though 17th-century texts often used the terms interchangeably</note>. If they save us alive, then we live; if they kill us, we shall die anyway."* And there were many strong arguments to make them think the enemy would kill them: they were Jews, and so on the enemy’s side, and might be suspected as spies. If not, they were lepers who might infect the camp, and were fit for no service—useful for nothing but to be knocked on the head <note>a colloquialism for being killed or executed</note>. Yet in this desperate case, they took the surer and more probable path and ventured to go to the Syrian side, and the success of that choice is known to you all. 

So you should reason: "If I give up on my believing, on humbling myself, on praying, and on attending the means of grace <note>the standard practices of Christian life like prayer and scripture</note>, I shall certainly perish; there is no help for it. I will therefore instead go on to do all these things as sincerely as I can to the utmost, and if God saves me—a sinner, a leper, an enemy—then so be it; if not, I can only be damned."

But let me tell you, such a resolution can never go to hell with you. Indeed, if anyone has a place in heaven, such a soul shall. For this does more than just silence the devil (for in this case, what can the devil himself say to you?); for if you will venture your faith, your service, and your all upon God, let the outcome be what it will in this resolute, yet safe way; if you will...

<header>Walking in Darkness.</header>

Pure trust refers the matter to God with contentment and submission (for what is it to the soul?). But it also puts God to the test, for he cannot find it in his heart to condemn someone who continues to act this way. When you freely serve him, choose him, take a risk, and leave yourself and the outcome of all your ways to him, you shall win more of his favor and love by this one act in such a situation than perhaps <note>original: "haply"</note> by all your obedience throughout your entire life. Your salvation and the assurance of it—that is God’s work. Leave it to him and test his faithfulness. It is self-love that makes us worry too much about it. You must continue to believe, repent, mourn for sin, hate it, forsake it, and use the means <note>the "means of grace," such as prayer, scripture, and sacraments</note>, etc. That is your work; and by doing so, you cannot be condemned.

---
### Direction 7.
---

->*Let him trust in the Name of the Lord*<-

If you ask what ground a soul in this situation may have to take such a risk, I answer: *His Name*. This will form our seventh direction.

Being thus resolved to turn to God and to go on *fearing and obeying him*, you may safely and confidently trust in and rely <note>original: "stay upon"</note> the *Name of God* when you have nothing else to lean on. This, as you see, is the direction the text gives. I had intended to make a separate point of it, but I will instead explain it more fully here as a direction and a means of support and comfort in this distress. So, take it as it arises from the text: Thus,

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues Direction 7, explaining how a believer in spiritual darkness can find a firm foundation for faith by looking outward at God’s character when they cannot find any evidence of grace within themselves.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>
<header>A Child <note>original: "Childe"</note> of Light</header>

> *That for someone who resolves to fear God and obey him, the Name of God is an all-sufficient support and anchor <note>original: "prop and stay"</note> for his faith to rest on, even when he sees nothing in himself, or in any promise in the Word <note>the Bible</note> that seems to belong to him.*

The *Name of God* alone is here contrasted with <note>original: "opposed to"</note> all other means and supports which faith has to rest on. It is contrasted with any comforting feeling of God’s love, and with the sight of any grace within a person’s own self to which a promise is made. So, when the soul looks into itself with one eye, and glances over the whole Word of God with the other, and yet sees not a single grace in the one, nor any promise in the other made to any grace within itself upon which it may rest—even then, the soul looking upon God and considering *what kind of God he is*, and what he says of himself, *of his mercy and kindness, and free grace* toward sinful men, can be supported. The mere consideration of what it knows to be in God, as he is revealed in the covenant of grace, may sustain him. This is what it means to rely <note>original: "stay upon"</note> on his name.

Now, to explain this further to you: By the "Name of God," two things are meant.

First, those glorious attributes, especially of *grace and mercy*, by which God has expressed himself and made himself known to us.

Secondly, Jesus Christ, as he is made and set forth to be "righteousness" to the sons of men.

Regarding the first, in Exodus 34:4–7, the Lord proclaimed his name: *The Lord God, merciful, gracious, long-suffering, abundant in goodness and truth, keeping mercy for thousands, forgiving iniquity, transgression, and sin, and will by no means clear the impenitent.* <note>The original text uses "impenitent" here to clarify that God's mercy does not extend to those who refuse to turn from sin, though the standard King James Version uses the word "guilty."</note>

For the second point, I refer to that passage, *Jeremiah 23:6*, speaking of Christ: *This is the name by which he shall be called, or made known to us;* THE LORD OUR RIGHTEOUSNESS. This means that Jesus Christ, who is God, possesses righteousness within himself for our benefit, and this righteousness can be made our own.

So, when a poor soul in distress is unable to say, "I see this or that sign, or any evidence in myself by which I can say God is my God, or that Christ is mine"; yet, because I see enough free grace in God and righteousness in Christ—of which I, being a sinful human and not a devil <note>The author implies that humans are capable of redemption while fallen angels (devils) are not.</note>, may therefore be capable of and may come to have a share <note>original: "interest"</note> in—even though I know nothing in myself by which I can claim <note>original: "challenge"</note> any present share; and because grace and mercy is His *Name*, and *Our Righteousness* is His Son’s *Name*; therefore, I cast myself upon both for pardon and favor. Upon these my soul leans, stays, and remains, and from these it will not be driven. 

So these two realizations, when they meet truly in the heart, help to form this act of "resting upon his Name" spoken of here; namely, First, [that there is such free grace, goodwill, and mercy, etc., in God, and that Jesus Christ is appointed and made to be our Righteousness.] And secondly, [that I am capable of receiving, and may come to have a share in both of these,] and that although there is nothing in me which may claim a share in them, yet there is nothing that excludes me. Therefore, I cast myself upon God for both and rest there; yes, even though I cannot yet say for certain that I will obtain them. And where this is true, and accompanied by that firm resolution to turn to God in all things a—

And so I come to the proof of this: namely, that when the names of God and Christ are simply and alone understood <note>original: "apprehended"</note>, they may be a sufficient ground for faith to rest upon; and nothing can be more comforting to a poor, distressed believer. 

In Numbers 14, when God's wrath grew hot <note>original: "waxed"</note> against his people (as sometimes it does in a similar manner against a poor soul), God began to say, *"How long will they provoke me?"* (verse 11) and spoke of destroying them and of making Moses a great nation. What did Moses's faith have recourse to but that proclamation of His Name you heard of before? He urged that in verses 17 and 18: *"Let the power of my Lord be great, according as you have spoken. The Lord is long-suffering, and of great mercy, forgiving iniquity, transgression, and sin."* He desired God to show His power in pardoning, because as much power is seen in overcoming His wrath as in making the world. *"Let your power be great"*; it was His Name, you see, that was cited <note>original: "alleged"</note> by Moses and prevailed with God for mercy. 

So also for His Son's sake, "The Lord our Righteousness." Elihu says in Job 33, when a man's soul is in deepest distress—as he describes it in verses 19, 20, 21, and 22—yet he says in verse 23: *"If there be a messenger to show a man [his] uprightness,"* that is, the righteousness that is laid up for men in the Lord Jesus, *"then God is gracious to him,"* and says, *"deliver him."* By the man resting upon that, *"his flesh returns to him again,"* becoming a means to support <note>original: "stay"</note> and restore him. <page-num>189</page-num>
### Walking in Darkness.
Cc 3

God's name and His Son's name are all-sufficient to uphold and support a soul. This is what the writer of *Psalm 130*—whether *David* or someone else—did when he was in his depths (as described in *verses 1 and 2*), plunged over head and ears in sorrow and distress. What did his faith turn to <note>original: "have recourse unto"</note>? In *verse 4*, he turned *to God’s name*—not to anything within himself, but simply to what his faith understood <note>original: "apprehended"</note> to be in God.

*"Mercy is with You,"* he says—nothing more. It is to be found *in Him and with Him*. He confirms his faith in that with this argument: because otherwise *none would fear Him*. He reasons, *"If You, Lord, should mark iniquities, who could stand?"* <note>original: "If thou wert extreme to mark what were amiss, no man could stand"</note> or be saved? Therefore, he says, surely *mercy is with You*, and therefore *let Israel hope in the Lord* (*verse 7*).

And why? Is it because *Israel* sees he has grace within himself? No, but *because mercy is with Him and plenteous redemption* (*last verse* <note>original: "v. ult." — short for the Latin *versus ultimus*</note>). This word *redemption* relates to His Son's name <note>referring to Christ's role as the Redeemer</note>. There is enough in Him—otherwise He would have none—and *Israel* is mentioned in His will as being capable of receiving it. Therefore, he says, *I will wait and hope in the Lord* (*verse 5*). 

Even though he could not say for certain that God had forgiven him, he knew that *forgiveness was with Him*. There he settles <note>original: "pitcheth"</note> and rests his soul, like a beggar at a great man’s door when there is no one else in the country able or willing to help <note>original: "relieve"</note> him. There he stays, even though he does not know whether he shall have anything or not. 

*"In my father's house there is bread enough,"* says the *Prodigal*. It is to be had there and nowhere else, and there is enough. *"Crumbs will satisfy me,"* says the *woman of Canaan*. *"You are the Mediator,"* <term>Mediator: a title for Jesus Christ, referring to His role as the go-between for God and humanity</term> she thought, *"and it is Your business to save. Even though I am a dog, I am still capable of having crumbs."* 

*"Woman,"* says *Christ*, *"great is"*

<meta>This page continues the argument that God’s character (His "name") is a sufficient foundation for faith, even when a believer feels they have no merit of their own. <page-num>190</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

...your faith; I have not found such faith among all my disciples. <note>The author is likely concluding a discussion of the Syrophoenician woman or the Centurion from the previous page, whom Jesus praised for their faith.</note> These people trusted in His name and not at all in themselves.

Similarly, in *Psalm 62*, David says in *verse 5* that *he trusted in God for salvation and mercy*. He continues to exhort the reader throughout the psalm: *Trust in God fully and at all times, and in no created thing* (*verses 8 and 9*). And what did he rest his hope upon? Simply two of God's attributes—namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note>, *mercy* and *power*.

*"God has spoken once"* (meaning irrevocably, as in *Psalm 89:35*: *"Once I have sworn,"* etc.), *"and twice I have heard this"*—meaning he had often encountered it in God's Word and reflected upon it—*"that power belongs to God"* (*verse 11*). This means God is able to save even in the greatest distress. David also says, *"I have heard that mercy also belongs to Him"* (*verse 12*). Therefore, God may be willing to help; and because these qualities are in Him, even though I have nothing in myself, I rest upon these alone. Many more such instances could be cited.

<margin>
Reasons.

1.
</margin>
The reasons why the name of God—and all that is in God—is a sufficient support <note>original: "prop"</note> for faith to rest upon are as follows:

First, because the name of God (that is, God's attributes and Christ’s righteousness) sufficiently, adequately, and fully answers all our needs, doubts, objections, and distresses. Whatever our lack or temptation may be, He has a name that provides a solution <note>original: "make supply"</note>. For example, take that name of His mentioned in *Exodus 34:5–6* and look at it piece by piece. Consider every "letter" (or part) of that name; every part answers a specific temptation we might face.

First, are you in misery and great distress? He is merciful: *"The Lord, the merciful."* Because He is *"The Lord,"* He is able to help you; because He is *"merciful,"* He is willing. "Yes," you might say, "but secondly, I am unworthy..."

<summary>The author explains that faith should rest on God's attributes—specifically His power and mercy—rather than on one's own merit. He uses David's trust in God's "name" and the divine titles revealed in Exodus 34 as evidence that God's character provides a specific remedy for every human doubt.</summary>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>walking in Darkness.</header>

"I have nothing in me to influence Him to do it." Well, therefore, He is **gracious**; now, grace is to show mercy freely. "Yes, but I have sinned against Him for a long time, for many years; if I had turned to Him when I was young, mercy might have been shown to me." To this He says, "I am **long-suffering**." "Yes, but my sins abound in every way in number, and it is impossible to count them; and they abound in heinousness <note>original: "hainousnesse"</note>. I have committed the same sins again and again; I have been false to Him and broken my promises with Him again and again." His Name also answers this objection: He is **abundant in goodness**. He abounds more in grace than you do in sinning. And though you have been false again and again to Him and broken all covenants, yet He is **abundant in truth** also—He is even better than His word, for He cannot, according to our human capacity <note>original: "capacities"</note>, express all the mercy that is in Him for us. "Yes, but I have committed great sins, made worse by many great circumstances—acting against knowledge, willfully, etc." He forgives **iniquity, transgression, and sin**—sins of all sorts. "Yes, but there is such mercy in Him only for a few, and I may not be among that number." Yes, there is **mercy for thousands, and He keeps it**; treasures of it lie by Him and are kept, if people would only come to Him and take them.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Object whatever you can, His Name will answer you. Do you need **comfort** as well as **pardon**? He is both the **Father of mercies and the God of all comfort**; that is His Name (2 Corinthians 1:3). Do you need **peace** of conscience while being filled with terrors? He is the **God of peace** (1 Thessalonians 5:23). "Yes, but I have a heart empty of grace and holiness, and full of corruptions <note>original: "corruptions," referring to sinful inclinations</note>." He is the **God of all grace** to heal you, as well as the God of peace to pardon you. Do you need wisdom and direction—

...one <note>This likely completes a word from the previous page, such as "alone."</note>: He is the *Father of lights*, as the Apostle says. Is your heart inconstant and full of double-mindedness? He is *unchangeable* also, as He speaks there in *James* 1. Thus, all objections that can be made may be answered from His Name; therefore, it is all-sufficient for faith to rest upon.

The same may be just as fully shown in His *Son’s name*, in whom God has made Himself strong to show mercy and bestow all good things. His Name is equal to God’s Name; that is, it is as vast in worth and merit as God’s heart is in His purposes of showing and bestowing mercies, in order to purchase all that God intended to give. His Name likewise has an all-sufficiency in it to supply all our needs and desires and satisfy all doubts <note>original: "scruples"</note>.

He is the *Prince of Peace* and is made *righteousness* to us. Are we in the depths of distress, with terrors within and terrors without, from which we see no escape? He is the *Mighty God*, able to save to the uttermost, being made redemption for us. Do we lack grace and want His image to be renewed and increased within us? He is the *Everlasting Father*—a *Father* to give birth to His likeness in us, and *everlasting* to maintain it forever once it has begun. He is made *sanctification* for us. Do we lack wisdom to guide us? He is the *Counselor* and is made *wisdom* for us. Everything we lack, He possesses—just as we need everything He has. Furthermore, even if we lack all of these things to an extreme degree, His Name is also *Wonderful*. For He is "wonderful" in all these attributes; able to

...do beyond all our expectations, to our utter amazement.

Or if the soul desires more distinct and particular satisfaction regarding justification—which consists in the pardon of sins and acceptance into the favor of God—this being the very point questioned in this state of spiritual desertion <note>A state where a believer feels abandoned by God or lacks the sense of His presence and favor.</note>, and where the soul seeks reassurance: that other Name of His, *The Lord Our Righteousness* (*Jeremiah 23:6*), will answer all objections and doubts that our hearts can make, if we only had the skill to understand all that it contains <note>original: "to spell all the letters in it."</note>. For if His righteousness satisfied God—who, when He condemns us, is greater than our hearts <margin>1 John 3:20</margin>—then it may satisfy our hearts much more. The righteousness of His life and death is not only a <term>sufficient ransom</term> <note>original Greek: *antilytron*; a price paid in exchange for another.</note> (*1 Timothy 2:6*), but there is *plenteous redemption* in it (*Psalm 130:7*). Indeed, there is even a superfluity of it, as the Apostle’s phrase implies: it <term>overflowed</term> <note>original Greek: *hyperpleonase*; to be exceedingly abundant or to abound beyond measure.</note> (*1 Timothy 1:14*). That is, it was "overfull"—more than would serve the purpose—and sufficient to pardon the sins of one who was the *chief of sinners* (*verse 15*). 

The Apostle elsewhere challenges all the wit and powers of sin, hell, and darkness to appear in this dispute, and he undertakes to answer them all based on this one position, which he lays down as a fundamental truth: *Christ has died* (*Romans 8:34*). This is essentially the same as the Name *The Lord Our Righteousness*. "Who, therefore," he asks, "shall condemn?" What can be alleged—either regarding the heinousness of sin in general or any of your sins in particular—for which an answer cannot be found in the righteousness of His life and death? Is it that sin is an offense against the great God? "Against You, against You only," as David speaks; and is not this His righteousness the righteousness of Jehovah? *Jehovah*

...vah our righteousness, who is the *mighty God*. The glory of this great God and all his excellencies were debased <note>This refers to the "emptying" or *kenosis* of Christ's divine status to become human.</note> by us through our sinning. Will not the emptying of his glory—he whose name is the *brightness of his Father’s glory*—satisfy and make amends by performing this righteousness for us? Are our sins the transgression of the holy and righteous Law in every part of it? And did not Jehovah, who gave and made that Law, *make himself under the Law* (Galatians 4:4) to become our righteousness? And to create a complete righteousness, did he not fulfill every part of it (Romans 8:4)? 

Is it your continued sinning, the sheer number of your sins, or the repetition of them that terrifies you? *All fullness dwells in him* (Colossians 1:19) who is our righteousness, and that fullness has dwelt in him longer than sin has dwelt in you. Furthermore, the righteousness of our Messiah is *everlasting righteousness* (Daniel 9:24). The merit of this righteousness is so great that even an eternity of sinning could not exhaust it or make it void.

And is all this righteousness stored up for himself only, or for some other kind of creature, so that you might never have an interest in it? No! The greatest part of our comfort is that [*Our*] righteousness is one "letter" of his Name, and that our names are included within his <note>Referencing the title "The Lord Our Righteousness" from Jeremiah 23:6.</note>. It is *for us* and it is ordained to be *ours*. It is as much *ours* to save us when we trust in it as it is his own to glorify him. It is *ours*, not for himself; he has no need of it, being God, blessed forever. It is *ours*, not the angels'; not the good angels, for they are justified by their own righteousness, and not the bad angels, for they are excluded from God’s will forever. Instead, it is *ours*—we who are the sons of men. And among men, it belongs especially to those who are *broken*, *whose souls draw near to the grave*, *whose lives draw near to the destroyers*, and *who come and pray to God*...

Thus, his Son's name is also all-sufficient to answer all objections upon which faith might rest: *So those who know his name will trust in him* (Psalm 9:10).

<margin>Reason 2.</margin>
A second reason why his Name is sufficient—even though you have and see nothing in yourself, nor any promise made to any grace in you to rest upon—is because all those promises made to certain conditions in us, which we ordinarily look toward, are *Yea* and *Amen* only in His Name and His Son’s Name. *His Name is the origin of them all, the root and the seed of them all; his Name is the* first matter <note>original: *materia prima*—a philosophical term referring to the fundamental substance from which all things are created</note>, *the primary substance of all those secondary promises,* out of which they are made and into which they are resolved <note>original: *ex quo fiunt, & in quod resolvuntur*</note>. His Name gives being to them all. If it were not for the mercy, grace, truth, and kindness in him, and the righteousness which is in his Son, what would all the promises that are made be worth? Just as the value of legal bonds depends upon the sufficiency <note>In this context, "sufficiency" refers to the financial credit or reliability of the person issuing the bond.</note> of the man who makes them, so all these promises depend upon his Name. Therefore, when you rely upon his Name—even if you do not yet have a promise made to any quality within yourself to rely upon—you are relying upon the foundation of all those promises. You are having recourse to the original, which is more authentic than derived copies. You rely on that which all others are resolved into; therefore, it is sufficient, even if all the rest seem to fail you in your understanding.

Thirdly, his mere Name is support enough for faith, and may be so, because he does everything he does for his *Name’s sake* and for his Son’s Name’s sake. And...

...for nothing in us, but merely for what is in himself, so **Isaiah** 48:9–10: *For my Name's sake*, etc. So also **Ezekiel** 36:22, 32: *For my Name's sake, and not your sake*; and **Isaiah** 43:25: *I am he who blots out your transgressions for my own sake, and will not remember your sins.* 

For if he blots out transgressions and pardons them—and if it is for his Name's sake that he does all he does, fulfilling all promises made to us and to the qualities within us—then when you see nothing in yourself to which any promise is made, and nothing appears to be an argument or motive for him to pardon you, then trust in that Name of his. Because he is God and has mercy within him, he will therefore do it. For that which is the only or main motive for God himself to do anything for us must necessarily be (when understood and believed) the strongest and surest ground for our faith as well, to persuade the heart that he will do it. As it is in knowledge, where knowing the causes of things produces the most certain knowledge; so it is in faith: knowing the main motive of God (who is the cause of all) produces the greatest certainty of conviction.

This may then direct poor souls in distress toward what they should venture everything upon—on what ground they should stake their souls, labors, endeavors, faith, repentance, obedience, and all else. They should stake it upon his Name when they see nothing in themselves to which any promise belongs. As **David** says in **Psalm** 73:26: *My heart may fail, and my flesh may fail, but God will never fail.* So I may say: your comforts in prayer, your experiences in hearing the Word, your joys, and the "earnest pennies" <note>original: "earnest-penies"—a small down payment or deposit given to guarantee a larger future payment; here, it refers to early spiritual experiences that serve as a pledge of salvation</note> you have stored up, may all be spent during a spiritual famine; your own graces and all...

### *Direction 8.*

<margin>To wait in the use of all means.</margin>
The eighth direction is to *wait upon God*, thus *trusting in His Name* through the constant use of all ordinances <note>original: "ordinances"; refers to religious practices such as prayer, attending church, or reading the Bible, which were seen as the "means" or tools God uses to provide comfort and grace.</note> and means of comfort. *Waiting* is indeed simply an act of faith stretched out further. Just as an allegory is merely a continued metaphor, so waiting is merely continuing to believe in God and to look for help from Him with submission, even if He stays away for a long time before He comes. *Waiting* is an act of faith resting on God, an act of hope expecting help from Him, and an act of patience—the mind quietly contenting itself until God comes—and of submission if He should not come at all. <margin>Lamentations 3:26.</margin> Therefore the Church says, being in this very situation: *It is good to hope and quietly wait for the salvation of the Lord.* 

It is indeed good to do so. For God will afflict you less, ease you sooner, and comfort you more when He does come. In the meantime, waiting allows you to possess your souls and remain yourself, and it upholds them. To do otherwise—to be impatient and to give up *looking for the Lord* as King *Ahaz* did <note>King Ahaz of Judah was criticized in the Bible for refusing to trust in God during a military crisis, seeking help from Assyria instead.</note>—is the greatest folly possible. As *Job* says in *Chapter* 12, *verse* 14: *If He shuts up, there is no opening.* The whole world cannot let you out; He keeps the keys of the dungeon, and you must wait for His leisure. He waits for the proper time to let you out: *Isaiah* 30:18 says, *He will wait to be gracious to you, for He is a God of judgment.* He is a wise and judicious God who knows the fittest times and seasons. That He stays away so long is not out of a lack of mercy, for He waits and longs to be gracious; rather, He does it out of *judgment* <note>original: "judgement"; here meaning wisdom or discernment in choosing the most effective moment.</note>, and His wisdom sees

<summary>This page explains the spiritual discipline of "waiting on God" as an extension of faith and hope during times of distress. It encourages patience, arguing that God’s delays are timed by His perfect wisdom rather than a lack of compassion.</summary>

That trouble of mind only hurts you if it drives you away from these means. Therefore, the devil attempts nothing more than to keep such souls from the Word, from good company, from the Sacraments, and from prayer, by arguing that these things are useless to you, that it is all in vain, and that you are only increasing your condemnation.

And secondly, just as when people are sick and eat, then throw it up again, you would say, "Still, swallow something, for some strength is gained; something remains in the stomach which keeps life and soul together." So I say here: even if you should forget almost everything you hear and seem to reap no benefit from it, keep listening, for some secret strength is gained by it. And as for the fear of increasing your condemnation, know that to utterly neglect and despise the means of grace leads to greater condemnation; using these means will actually lessen your condemnation. 

Therefore read, pray, meditate, hear sermons, confer <note>original: "conferre"; meaning to discuss spiritual matters with others</note> with others, and receive the Sacraments; do not skip these, your appointed spiritual meals. Indeed, when the body is sick, you often avoid your regular food, but when the soul is sick, there is more need for these spiritual meals than ever. All of these are both meat and medicine—food, physician’s remedies <note>original: "physick"</note>, heart-strengthening drinks <note>original: "cordials"</note>, and everything else you need. 

Make use of reading the Word; *the Scriptures were written for our consolation* <note>original: "consolation"; meaning comfort or solace</note>, therefore read them often. Attend preaching, for *God creates the fruit of the lips, peace* (Isaiah 57:19). Likewise, receive the Sacrament often, for those days are "sealing days" <note>In the 17th century, the Sacraments (like Communion) were viewed as "seals" on a legal document, confirming God's promise of forgiveness to the believer.</note>. Go and confess your sins; write out your request for pardon, including everything you know about your own failings, and bring it to Christ so he may set his seal to it.

Only take this caution: do not trust in the use of the means themselves, but in God who works through the means. To think, "Oh, I shall find comfort from such a man," or "at such a time," or "in such an ordinance," often ruins <note>original: "dasheth"</note> everything.

### Direction 9.
->*To Pray: Pleas to be used to God in Prayer for recovery from this condition.*<-

And ninthly, above all things *pray*, and ask others also to pray for you; for God often *restores comfort to such people* at the request of *mourners* <note>original: "Mourners"; here referring to those who intercede or grieve on behalf of the afflicted person.</note> for them (Isaiah 57:18). But yet, you must especially be earnest and fervent in pouring out your own complaint yourself. For though the speaking of friends may somewhat further your suit, yet (just as between two suitors) it must be worked out between God and you alone in private; and His goodwill must be obtained by seeking <note>original: "wooing"</note> Him in secret. This is the advice the Apostle gives you in James 5:13: *Is anyone among you suffering? Let him pray.* And because, of all other afflictions, this darkness in a person's spirit needs prayer the most, *David* wrote a Psalm specifically for this purpose—not only for his own private use, but for the benefit and use of all others in similar distress, as is clear by its title: *Psalm 102: A prayer for the afflicted, when he is overwhelmed and pours out his complaint before the Lord.* And this, says David, is my constant practice: *When my soul is overwhelmed, I pour out my prayer to You* (Psalm 61:3 <note>The author likely refers to the sentiment in Psalm 61:2 or Psalm 62:8.</note>). This was *Christ’s* practice also; for in His agony, He prayed even more earnestly (Luke 22:44).

When at any time, therefore, your sins and God’s

...wrath meeting in your conscience makes you deathly sick, as Isaiah <note>original: "Esay"</note> says, then pour out your soul; lay it open and confess your sin. Just as confession will ease you (as vomiting usually does), so also it will move God to pity you and give you cordials <note>original: "cordials"; medicinal drinks intended to invigorate the heart and spirits.</note> and comforts to restore you again.

This is what David did in Psalm 38:18 when he was in great distress (verses 2, 3, 4, 5): *I will declare my iniquity and be sorry for my sin.* He also used this as an argument for God to pardon him when his "bones were broken" in Psalm 51: *Cleanse me from my sin* (verse 2), *for I acknowledge my transgressions* (verse 3). And after he had confessed (verses 4, 5, 6), then he cried out, *Make me hear of joy and gladness* (verse 8), and *restore to me the joy of your salvation* (verse 12). 

What was the chief ingredient—the main and principal motive—that worked most effectively within him to make him confess and mourn, bringing everything to the surface? It was the thought: *Against you, you only.* He emphasizes this consideration twice as much as any other factor to make his heart mourn; this, primarily if not solely, melted and dissolved him. 

In your own confessions, let this same thought be your main focus: *Against you, you, have I sinned—so often, so grievously, and so presumptuously. Against you, a God so great, and yet also so good, so kind, and so willing to receive and pardon me—if only (I say) my heart were as willing to turn to you.* 

And when your situation is like Job's in Job 10:15–17—when you are full of confusion (as he says there), so full that you think your heart could hold no more, and yet it increases and He fills you even fuller—then pour out your complaints to Him just as He pours confusion into you. When He hunts you like a "fierce lion," as Job complained, then fall down...

<summary>This page advises those experiencing spiritual "sickness" to find relief through the honest confession of sin, using the biblical examples of David and Job to show how vulnerability before God leads to restoration.</summary>
<keywords>confession, repentance, Isaiah, David, Job, spiritual healing, conscience, Psalm 51, Psalm 38</keywords>

Ask Him, therefore, *What profit will there be in my blood?* And if the satisfaction of His justice is His goal, He might better accept the payment which His Son made for Him; in that way, He will be certain to lose nothing through you. By doing this, He not only receives the glory of His justice but shows the riches of His grace and mercy also, thereby doubling the revenue of His glory through you.

Or is it, Lord, that You aim to have more obedience from me than I have given in the past? You are *distracted with terrors* (as Heman <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the traditional author of Psalm 88, known for its tone of deep despair.</note> pleads in Psalm 88:15), so that the powers and forces of your soul are scattered and dissolved and cannot focus on or attend to their duty. # A Child of Light

Look into your heart, and be bold to inquire of Him if He can see nothing there which He Himself wrote, never to be erased. Ask if there is not some spark of love for Him and a fear of Him <note>original: "his feare," meaning a holy reverence for God</note> which He Himself put there, and ask Him if He recognizes His own hand <note>original: "hand," meaning handwriting or signature</note>. And for your comfort, know that even when you cannot read it (your graces being much blurred), yet He can read His own handwriting at any time and will not deny it.

You may be even bolder. Yes, desire Him to look into His own heart and therein to view the *idea* He had of you, and those secret, ancient thoughts He bore toward you from all eternity. And if at first He still seems silent, then desire Him to look upon you again and ask Him if He does not know you—and if He has not known and taken you as His own forever, and engraved you on the palms of His hands and the tablet of His heart with such deep and lasting letters of loving-kindness as are not yet, and indeed will never be, erased.

Tell Him you dare refer yourself entirely to what passed between Him and His Son concerning you, and let His own heart decide it. Appeal to *Christ* as your guarantor <note>original: "surety," a legal term for one who takes responsibility for another's debt or obligation</note> and a witness for you, who was privy to all His counsel, and ask whether you are not one of those He gave to Him with a charge to redeem and save. And desire Him to look into *Christ’s heart* also, to see if your name is not written there with His own hand; and if *Christ* did not bear your name written upon His heart (as the High Priest did the names of all the *Tribes of Israel* <note>A reference to Exodus 28:29, where the High Priest wore the names of the twelve tribes over his heart on a breastpiece whenever he entered the Holy Place</note>) when He hung upon the *Cross* and when He ascended into the *Holy of Holies* <note>The innermost chamber of the Sanctuary, representing the immediate presence of God</note>. <meta>This page continues the pastoral advice for a believer enduring spiritual "darkness," focusing on how to persist in prayer even when God seems silent or when the believer feels unable to speak.</meta>

<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>->Walking in Darkness.<-</header>

The Church has taught us to plead, as in *Habakkuk* 1:12, *“O Lord, are you not from everlasting my God, and my Holy One?”* It was a bold question, yet God does not dislike it; instead, He approves of it and immediately assents to it with a gracious answer to their hearts before they could go any further. For their next words—spoken abruptly because of His sudden answer—are an assurance of this: *“We shall not die.”* God, being challenged in this way and hearing His own thoughts voiced by them, could not deny it; He acknowledged it was true. And thus, while you may be speaking blindfolded, as it were, casting anchor in the dark, yet speaking His very heart, He perhaps <note>original: "haply"</note> may claim you as His own, and fall upon your neck and kiss you.

And if yet, after continually praying in this way, you still find no comfort and no answer from Him—but He seems rather to even shut your very prayers out (as in *Psalm* 22:2–3)—then reason with Him <note>original: "expostulate," meaning to argue or reason earnestly with someone</note> (as *David* does in *Psalm* 80:4 <note>The original text cites Psalm 70:4, but the quote "Why shuttest thou out our prayers" corresponds more closely to Psalm 80:4: "How long will you be angry against the prayer of your people?"</note>): *“Why do you shut out our prayers and will not hear us pray?”* For alas, he thought, we have nothing else to help us in our time of need but prayer. "And if prayer will do no good," you should say, "I am ruined."

<margin>1 John 2:1</margin>
And if, through all these discouragements, your condition proves worse and worse, so that you cannot pray but are struck dumb when you come into His presence (as *David* says in *Psalm* 77:4, *“I am so troubled that I cannot speak”*), then begin to make signs when you cannot speak. Groan, sigh, sob, and chatter as *Hezekiah* did <note>In Isaiah 38:14, King Hezekiah describes his prayer of suffering: "Like a crane or a swallow, so did I chatter; I did mourn as a dove."</note>; bemoan yourself for your own unworthiness and ask Christ to speak your requests for you, and ask God to hear Him on your behalf. Christ is *an advocate with the Father, and he pleads no bad case, nor was he ever defeated in any legal suit <note>original: "cast in any suit," a legal term for losing a court case</note> he pleaded.*

<meta>This page continues the pastoral guidance for a believer in deep spiritual despair, moving from the act of persistence in prayer to a state of ultimate submission and selfless adoration of God, even when the soul feels rejected.</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>

And if perhaps <note>original: "haply"</note> still, after many years, He does not acknowledge you as His own, but it grows darker and darker—suppose even until your death approaches, or to such extremes that He seems to you to cast you off forever, so that your distress boils up to such thoughts as these: "There is no other remedy; He and I must part"—then, in the midst and depths of such sad fears and perceptions <note>original: "apprehensions"</note>, get down upon your knees once more. Despite everything, fall to blessing Him for all those glorious excellencies of holiness, kindness, grace, wisdom, and so on, which are in Him. It was the beauty of these that first took your heart and made you fall in love with Him, even if it seems you will never benefit from them yourself. 

Bless Him for all the mercy He shows to others, through which they have occasion to magnify Him, even if you should be found unworthy. Bless Him, and those who shall live with Him forever, who stand around Him, see His face, and enjoy Him forever. For whatever sins you think He will condemn you for, condemn yourself for them first, and continue to ask forgiveness for them. Whatever service you have done for Him in any way—by which He received any glory—get your heart to say that you do not repent of doing it, but are glad for all that was done for Him and wish it had been better. 

Whatever mercies you have tasted from Him, confess yourself unworthy of them and thank Him, even if you should never partake of any more. (Dispositions like these in such extreme circumstances often appear in the hearts of God's children.) And ask Him that He would simply preserve good thoughts of Him within you, so that you might not blaspheme Him. And when you are going—sinking into hell in your own perception <note>original: "apprehensions"</note>,

See what He Himself says in Jeremiah 31:18, 19, 20: *Ephraim is my son, his dear son, my pleasant son* (as He says there). And yet He began to speak against him with words as bitter and sharp as any He has ever spoken against you; He rebuked him severely and looked sternly at him, as if He intended never to have mercy on him. Upon being thus rebuked <note>original: "snib'd"</note>, Ephraim begins to cry and *bemoan himself*, just as I have taught you to do. Being under the yoke (as you are) to tame him, he acknowledges that it was justly done, for he had been like an *untrained calf* <note>original: "a bullock unaccustomed to the yoke"</note>. Ephraim began to be ashamed and confounded, unable to look up because he had sinned against God, and so he seeks after repentance—asking it from God, without whose help he was unable to turn to Him: *Turn me, and I shall be turned*. He even dares to claim <note>original: "challenge"</note> God and His eternal love, saying, *You are the Lord my God*. 

Well, says God, though it has been a long time since I spoke against him, and I have allowed him to lie thus plunged in misery for a long time, yet *I remember him still*. His tears and his sighs will never leave my mind. And though he thinks that I had forgotten him, yet I remember him, and *my heart yearns for him* <note>original: "my bowels are troubled for him," a biblical expression for deep compassion</note> as much as or more than he yearns for himself. I can hold back no longer; *I will surely have mercy on him*. Indeed, had God condemned him, His heart would have been troubled for him for all eternity.

->**Direction 10.**<-

<margin>Do not rest in ease, but in healing.</margin>
The tenth and last direction is that, having done all this, you should not rest merely in being at ease, but in being *healed*: not just in the ease of conscience, but in the healing of conscience. I base this on Isaiah 57:17, 18. What

[mercy on him. What] was the true result <note>original: "issue"</note> of his trouble there, with whom God contended? It was *healing* and *guiding*.
> *I will guide him, and I will heal him.*

You who are troubled in mind, do not think your spiritual condition is good simply because you have begun to stop being troubled. Think it good only when the result of your trouble is the healing of your spirit through some solid ground of comfort, and when the result is guidance in God's ways and a closer walk with Him. For God may slacken the cords and take you off the rack even though He has not yet pardoned you. A traitor who was cast into the dungeon and had many chains <note>original: "irons"</note> on him may be let out of the dungeon, have his chains removed, have the liberty of the Tower <note>A reference to the Tower of London, used as a prison for high-status individuals who were sometimes allowed to walk the grounds while still under guard</note>, and walk about again with his keeper alongside him, and yet still not have his pardon. In fact, before an execution, they usually take the chains off and allow the prisoner more freedom. 

It is this way with many. "I thank God," one says, "I have had much trouble of mind and distress of conscience; such and such sins terrified me, and I could not sleep because of them. But now I am well again, and now they do not trouble me." Yes, but is this all? You have reason to fear that your chains have only been removed in preparation for your execution. 

It is the same for people regarding trouble of mind over the guilt of sin as it is regarding the power of sin; it is the same in justification <term>being declared righteous before God</term> as it is in sanctification <term>the process of being made holy</term>. A man who has had a strong lust stirring within him may think he is utterly free of it if a year or two passes without it stirring. Yet, this may only be a *restraint* of it, not the *killing* of it; a *temporary cessation*, not a *mortification* <term>the spiritual "putting to death" of sinful desires</term>. So it often is with this trouble of mind which arises from the guilt of sin: because a man no longer finds those doubts,

[...and because he no longer feels the] fears and terrors in his heart which he used to feel, he therefore immediately thinks all is well. However, it may be merely a *truce*, not a *peace*; a laying down of arms only for a while, to make greater preparations against the soul afterward; a reprieve and a little more freedom <note>original: "enlargement"</note> in prison, not a pardon, if this is the only result of it.

To help you further understand the meaning of this: between one who is God’s child and a wicked man (though both may be, and often are, troubled in mind and conscience), there is a major difference, both in the primary cause of their trouble and in the outcome and removal of their trouble. A wicked man’s trouble is for the anguish and immediate pain <note>original: "smart"</note> he feels from sin, and from God’s wrath lashing his conscience. It arises out of fears that his sin will not be pardoned, but that he shall endure these tortures forever in hell. So it was with *Judas*, *Cain*, and many others. 

But a godly man’s trouble (though it often includes all of this) has a deeper cause. It is not only the pain or the sting of sin, but also the filth, the foulness, and the offense of it done to God that wounds him. For he has a *heart after God’s heart*, and therefore looks on sin with the same kind of eye that God does; and just as God accounts the offense done to Him the greatest evil in sin, so does a godly heart also. It is not only the sting of this serpent, but the poison of it that disquiets him. Neither is it only the lack of pardon for sin and the fear of God’s everlasting wrath which mainly troubles him, but the lack of God’s favor—the separation from Him whom he sees as so excellent and glorious; the lack of seeing His face. His desire is to live in His sight, and

Therefore, because the one [the wicked person] is only troubled with the sting and the sharp pain <note>original: "smart"</note> of sin, if you simply pull that sting out and take that burden off, he is perfectly fine—as cheerful <note>original: "jocund"</note> and pleasant as ever. It is immediate relief that he seeks; to that end, he confesses his sin and does anything for the moment to escape it. He is like *Pharaoh* in *Exodus 10:17*: "Take away this death only." Or, at most, his goal is merely the pardon of sin and peace with God, so that he may be freed from the fears of undergoing that punishment forever, the down payment <note>original: "earnest"</note> of which he feels in his conscience now.

Consequently, the remedies they often turn to are appropriate to their shallow needs; they are like rattles used to quiet crying children. They run to cheerful company and to music, and so on—just as *Cain* took to building cities—and in this way, they push away the terrors of their consciences. They seek relief and nothing more. Or, they rush into a formal performance of religious duties. It is just like the poor souls under Roman Catholicism <note>original: "Popery"</note>; when they were stung by the friars' sermons, they were assigned penances and good deeds to perform. These activities quieted them for a while, and they believed that because of those deeds, they should be pardoned. Similarly, men today rush to perform holy duties with the same mindset they had then—treating them as bribes for a pardon. "What shall I give," as the man asks in *Micah*, "for the sin of my soul?"

<margin>Micah 6:7</margin>

"O wretched man that I am, who shall deliver me from this death only?" <note>original: "man that I am..." I may allude to what *Absalom* said in address <note>original: "complement"</note> to his father when he was banished from him, to express the true desire and greatest trouble of a soul in this case, as you have it in *2 Samuel 14:32*. *Absalom* was pardoned for his offense, but it did not satisfy him: *"Let me see his face, or let him kill me."* So it is with a poor soul: ease, pardon, and the knocking off of his <term>bolts</term> <note>original: "bolts" — iron shackles or chains.</note> do not satisfy him until he enjoys communion with God, until he sees His face in His <term>ordinances</term> <note>original: "ordinances" — religious rites such as prayer, preaching, and the sacraments, viewed as "means of grace."</note>: *Psalm 24:6. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Isaiah 50:10, specifically addressing the rarity of the deep spiritual "darkness" mentioned. It argues that while many fear God, only a few undergo the extreme spiritual terrors experienced by figures like Heman and Job.</meta>

<page-num>221</page-num>
<header>walking in Darkness.</header>

...of conscience means. But when he speaks of those who fear **God** and walk in darkness, and suffer terrors from their youth as Heman <note>Heman the Ezrahite, the traditional author of Psalm 88, which describes a life of deep spiritual affliction: "I have been afflicted and ready to die from my youth up" (Psalm 88:15).</note> did, such a person is **one in a thousand**. Of such a person, he asks, "*Who is among you?*" because few have experience with such a condition. **Job** had friends who were certainly godly—for **Job** was commanded to pray for them, and **God** said He would hear **Job** on their behalf—and they likely knew many other godly men besides **Job**. Yet when this condition of darkness befell **Job**, it was such a strange trial to them that they thought him a hypocrite because of it, as they had never felt or heard of the like in others. When **Christ** was about to enter His **agony** <note>original: "agony" — referring to Christ's intense spiritual suffering in the Garden of Gethsemane before His crucifixion.</note>, He would not have many of His **Apostles** even near Him to be witnesses of it, much less to feel the same; He took only two or three. The reasons are:

---

<margin>Reason 1.</margin> 
First, because although all of God’s people are fighting men and men of valor, He has only a few champions. Just as **David** had many soldiers but only a few **Worthies** <note>The "Worthies" were King David's elite warriors, known for their extraordinary feats of strength and courage (see 2 Samuel 23).</note>, God calls only a few to engage in single combat with **Satan** and His **wrath**. Although He exercises all believers in lighter skirmishes, He does not call everyone to fight such bloody battles. God said, "*Have you considered my servant Job? There is none like him.*" <note>Job 1:8</note> **God** was willing to venture Job into the field of battle, but He would not do the same with Job's friends.

<margin>Reason 2.</margin> 
Secondly, just as He has few champions able and fit for such an encounter, He also has a variety of **other temptations** with which to exercise His people. He uses poverty, a bad reputation, imprisonment, cruel mockings, loss of goods, and trials with friends; some believers have quite enough to do just struggling under one of these. There is no temptation that does not befall someone, but they seldom all befall...

<summary>This page argues that extreme spiritual darkness is a rare trial reserved for God's "champions," such as Job or Heman, rather than a universal experience for all believers. The author explains that God uses a variety of different trials—like poverty or social rejection—to test different people according to their strength.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of why intense spiritual terrors (the "darkness" of the soul) are relatively rare among believers. It concludes the second reason (God’s consideration of human weakness) and provides a third reason (God’s preference for gentler methods of correction) before offering comfort to those who have not experienced such extremes.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>A Child of Light</header>

---

...befall one: some spirits are so weak that they would faint and not be able to sustain themselves. God never allows anyone to be tempted beyond what they are able to handle (*1 Corinthians 10:13*). Some people’s bodies are weak, and if God should rebuke them for sin for a long time, they would be brought to nothing; He remembers they are but flesh and does not stir up all His wrath, as David says, "*Oh, spare me, that I may recover strength*" <note>original: "Oh suffer me to recover strength" — a reference to Psalm 39:13.</note>. Additionally, God has present use for some men in their callings <term>vocations or professional duties</term> and employments; if they were continually distracted with terrors (as Heman <note>Heman the Ezrahite, a biblical figure known for his deep spiritual suffering in Psalm 88.</note> was), they would be unfit for those duties.

<margin>Reason 3.</margin>
Thirdly, God usually afflicts in this way only in cases of extremity, when He encounters a very froward <term>stubborn or perverse</term> heart and a stout, proud spirit—a "knotty piece" <note>original: "knotty peece" — a metaphor for a gnarled piece of wood that requires more force to work with than smooth timber.</note> (*Isaiah 57:17*). If lesser crosses <note>original: "crosses" — trials, burdens, or troubles.</note> would do the job, He would not bring out the "great rod." If a small rod will thresh out the cumin, He does not use a heavy wheel or a flail to do it (*Isaiah 28:27*). For most of His children, lesser afflictions work effectively through His blessing; mercies work, disgrace works, and poverty works. He does not willingly afflict (*Lamentations 3:33*), and therefore He does not do so unnecessarily. He does not put men into the dungeon for every fault; and therefore, there are few who are long exercised in this way.

<margin>Application 1.</margin> <note>original: "Use. 1." — In Puritan sermons, a "Use" is the practical application or lesson derived from the doctrine.</note>
Therefore, do not think that you lack true grace because you were never terrified as some others have been. Just as some have true faith and have found peace, yet have never tasted of "joy unspeakable and full of glory," so some have experienced true humiliation <term>the spiritual state of being brought low by a sense of sin</term> who never knew terrors of conscience. If you see sin as the greatest misery and Christ as the greatest good, then you are truly humbled.

<summary>This page explains that God rarely uses extreme spiritual terrors because He considers human frailty and prefers gentler corrections unless dealing with a particularly stubborn heart. It concludes by reassuring readers that intense spiritual trauma is not a required proof of true faith or humility.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual depression, Heman, David, trials, cumin and flail, God's mercy, humility, grace, conscience</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on spiritual darkness and God's specialized care for those suffering from it. <header>Walking in Darkness</header>

<margin>Application 2</margin>
You see that we may often preach on subjects that concern only a few people in a congregation, yet we are still required to preach them. There are only a few who walk for a long time in darkness, yet Christ preaches specifically to them. *Yes, and for such people, God gives gifts*, specifically the *tongue of the learned* <note>original: "tongue of the learned" — a reference to Isaiah 50:4, describing the ability to speak words of comfort that sustain the weary.</note>. God often provides a *pastor after His own heart for the sake of just a few. Jeremiah 3:15 says, "I will take one or two from a city, etc., and I will give them pastors after my heart."* If God provides pastors for so few, then it is even more likely that many sermons are preached for the sake of only a few individuals. Christ Himself demonstrated this, as He says in *Luke 4:26: "There were many widows in Israel, but Elijah was sent to none of them except to a widow in Zarephath <note>original: "Sarepta" — the Greek name for the Phoenician city of Zarephath.</note>; and there were many lepers, but Elisha was sent to none of them except Naaman."* 

So Christ says, *“I am sent but to a few.”* Therefore, just as we must not deprive one poor soul of its portion of spiritual food simply because no one else is sharing in it, the rest of the congregation should not be resentful. Instead, just as people waiting for a charitable distribution <term>original: "Dole" — a formal distribution of food, money, or clothing to the poor</term>, they should wait until their own portion comes. If even one poor soul has had their spiritual condition revealed and comforted, everyone else should be thankful.

<margin>Application 3</margin>
Here we see the reason why some people in distress of mind complain that no one else has ever been in their exact condition; they are often inclined to feel this way. The *Church does the same in Lamentations 1:12* <note>"Is it nothing to you, all you who pass by? Look and see if there is any sorrow like my sorrow..."</note>. The reason they feel this way is that few people are so deeply troubled, and perhaps they have never met anyone else who was. However, some are, and others have been in that same state before. *For 1 Corinthians 10:13 says: "No temptation has overtaken you except what is common to man."*

<margin>Doctrine 3</margin>
**That those few in congregations who walk in darkness, yet still fear and obey Him, *God and Christ have a special eye toward* and care for.** You see that He singles them out, as it were, from all the rest, asking, *"Who is among you?" Isaiah 66:2 says: "All these things my hand has made, but to this man I will look—to him who is poor and of a broken spirit, and who trembles at my word."* That is to say, even though all

<meta>catchword: things</meta>

<header>A Child of Light</header>

everything and everyone else in the world are my creatures, and so I care for them all; yet He seems to overlook everything else, and says, *“to him will I look,” etc.* <note>original: "to him will I looke, &c." — a reference to Isaiah 66:2, "but to this man will I look, even to him that is poor and of a contrite spirit."</note> as if there were no one else in the Church.

<margin>Reason 1.</margin>
The first reason is because it is the office <term>original: "office" — a specific duty, position, or function</term> of Christ to do so. *The Spirit is upon Him for this purpose, as in Isaiah 61:1–3: "to open the prison to those who are bound"* and shut up in this dungeon; *"to appoint for those who mourn beauty for ashes, the oil of joy for mourning, and garments of praise for the spirit of heaviness."* He is the *Physician*, and has undertaken the cure (*Matthew 9:12*). To whom should the *Physician* give His attention but to the sick—and especially those who are most sick, such as those who cannot find their sins forgiven (*Isaiah 33:24*)? He is the *Shepherd* (*Isaiah 40:11*) and will take care of all His sheep; He knows them by name. But of whom especially? *The lambs who are weak—young Christians. He will gather them with His arms and gently lead the ewes with young*; those who are laboring and bringing forth <note>original: "travelling" — here used in the sense of "travailing," meaning the pains of childbirth, a metaphor for the spiritual struggle of new birth or deep conviction</note>, as those under spiritual terrors are. He will not overdrive them, for God has given Him the charge that *He should lose none of them.* He is that *good Shepherd* who will, as He has promised in *Ezekiel 34:16, "seek out that which was lost, bring back that which was driven away, bind up that which was broken, and strengthen that which was sick."* He names all the misfortunes that befall them because He helps in all miseries. Yes, and as verse 12 says, after they have been bewildered <term>original: "wildred" — lost in a wilderness or confused</term> in a cloudy and dark day and have walked in darkness for a long time, He will *find them out and deliver them*, just as His promise says there.

<margin>Reason 2.</margin>
Secondly, if His office did not move Him to it, His love would; for He is a merciful and a compassionate <note>original: "pittifull" — in this context, full of pity or empathy rather than "pathetic"</note> high priest,

<summary>This page explains that Christ prioritizes those in spiritual darkness because His divine roles as Physician and Shepherd specifically require Him to care for the sick, the lost, and the weak. It highlights how Christ's primary mission is to provide comfort and restoration to those suffering from spiritual heaviness.</summary>

...high priest (Hebrews 4:15), and was *in all points tempted just as we are*. This was especially true regarding his agony of spirit; in that, he drank more deeply than anyone else. Therefore, he is suited to pity us the most in those moments, and the more someone is troubled, the more he is moved with compassion. *Isaiah 63:9: "In all their affliction he was afflicted." And Jeremiah 31:20: "Since I spoke against him, I remember him still; therefore my heart yearns <note>original: "bowels are troubled" — in the 17th century, the "bowels" were considered the seat of deep compassion and mercy, similar to how we use the "heart" today</note> for him."* When a child is sick, the mother is more troubled and concerned about them; her eye and mind are more focused on that child than on everyone else in the house.

<margin>Application 1</margin>
This application <term>original: "use" — a standard term in Puritan sermons for the practical "so what?" of a doctrine</term> is intended to address that misconception <note>original: "conceit"</note> that strikes everyone who is in spiritual darkness: they think that of all people, God disregards them. *Zion said, "The Lord has forgotten me" (Isaiah 49:14).* Similarly, *David* asked if *God had forgotten to be merciful (Psalm 77:9).* Because they find their own hearts hardened toward God, they think his heart is hardened toward them. Because they can find no love in their hearts for God, they think he bears no love for them. But you see that *Christ* especially looks for such people, even overlooking everyone else. *God has engraved you on the palms of his hands (Isaiah 49:16)*; every sigh of yours touches his heart. *Isaiah 57:15 <note>The original text cites Isaiah 59:15, but the quote "I dwell with him that is broken" is from Isaiah 57:15</note>: "I dwell with him who is of a broken and humble spirit, to revive the spirit of the humble." God is near to him.*

<margin>Application 2</margin>
The second application is this: if God's eyes are on us more when we are in spiritual trouble than on anyone else, then let our eyes be fixed on him. *“We do not know what to do, but our eyes are upon you” (2 Chronicles 20:12).* Let our eyes look toward him for help, just like those who looked at the bronze serpent <note>In the Book of Numbers, those bitten by venomous snakes were healed by looking at a bronze serpent raised by Moses; this was a common metaphor for looking to Christ for salvation</note> for healing. Let our eyes look toward him for service, *as the eyes of female servants look to the hand of their mistress (Psalm 123:2)*. We should look not to men or to our own reputation, but keep our eyes on God in everything we do, as if there were none else

<summary>This page argues that Christ’s own experience with spiritual agony ensures His compassion for those in distress, using the metaphor of a mother caring for a sick child. It concludes by urging those in spiritual darkness to fix their eyes on God rather than their own feelings of abandonment.</summary>
