<header>A Child of Light</header>

...else in the world to approve ourselves unto.

<margin>Doctrine 4</margin>
In this passage, when he speaks of those children of his who are in darkness, he chooses to describe them by their *fear and obedience* rather than by any other grace <note>original: "grace" — here meaning a spiritual virtue or quality given by God</note>. Observe: **when the children of God are under terrors, the most prominent grace that appears in them is a fearfulness of offending God and a willingness to obey him.** Other graces may be active, but these are the most prominent, and therefore he mentions them for their comfort.

<margin>Explication</margin>
First, for an explanation <term>original: "Explication" — the part of a sermon where the preacher clarifies the meaning of the doctrine</term>: know that different occasions draw out different graces. When the sunshine of God’s favor melts the heart, then *love and obedience* proceeding from that love are most prominent, as is *godly sorrow*. For example, Mary Magdalene wept much and loved much because *much was forgiven her* (Luke 7:47); her heart was full of assurance. 

On the contrary, when the sense of God’s love is withdrawn, and fears and terrors are spread throughout the heart, then fear and obedience show and reveal themselves. Therefore, in Isaiah 66:2, the phrases "he who is poor and contrite" and "trembles at my word" are joined together. Such a person trembles at every command and threat, and is fearful of transgressing. Those in that spiritual state find this to be true. The reason is:

<margin>Reason</margin>
Because graces, and the affections <note>original: "affections" — the deep-seated emotions or dispositions of the soul</note> in which those graces are located, stir more or less within us according to what they are focused on and how we perceive them. Therefore, when the soul is occupied mostly with God's displeasure for sin and the perception of his wrath, it fears most. Then, that fear works accordingly against anything that might displease God. This is why the Apostle says that since *our God is a consuming fire* (Hebrews 12:29), *let us serve God acceptably with reverence and...*

<meta>The catchword "godly" refers to the completion of the verse: "godly fear."</meta>

<summary>The author explains that when believers experience spiritual "darkness" or "terror," their most visible virtues are a deep fear of offending God and a commitment to obey Him. He argues that different spiritual circumstances draw out different character traits, with fear becoming dominant when God's felt presence is withdrawn.</summary>

...godly fear. Therefore, when we feel his presence in this way <note>original: "feele him so" — referring to the feeling of God’s displeasure or the withdrawal of his comfort discussed on the previous page</note>, we should be most suspicious of our own hearts if we do not find ourselves more fearful of offending him and more ready to obey him.

<margin>Application 1</margin>
The first application <term>original: "Use" — a standard section in a Puritan sermon where the doctrine is applied to the listener's life</term> is one of self-examination <note>original: "trial"</note>: to see whether you are a child of God experiencing his wrath. Ask yourself: Do you fear him more, and does that fear result in obedience? Just as *Christ learned obedience by what he suffered* (Hebrews 5:8), so will you also, if you possess his Spirit.

<margin>Application 2</margin>
The second application is to always exercise your spiritual virtues <note>original: "graces"</note> according to their proper seasons. When you are afflicted, pray; when you are joyful, sing *Psalms* (James 5:13 <note>original: "Iam. 1. 13." — likely a misprint in the original text for James 5:13, which contains this specific advice</note>). When you are filled with assurance, then mourn and be humbled <note>original: "confounded" — meaning to be deeply moved or silenced by a sense of one’s own unworthiness in the face of God's mercy</note> (Ezekiel 16:63 <note>original: "Ezek. 16. ult." — "ult." stands for "ultimo," referring to the last verse of the chapter</note>).

-> ISAIAH 50:11 <-

> *Behold, all you who kindle a fire, who surround yourselves*
> *with sparks: walk in the light of your fire,*
> *and in the sparks which you have kindled; this you*
> *shall have from my hand: you shall lie down in sorrow.*

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'L' adorned with interwoven vines and leaves.</image-desc>

Let us now consider the opposite condition of wicked men, who are described here as *walking in the light of their own fire*, and so on.

The main difficulty lies in explaining <note>original: "opening"</note> what is meant by *their own fire*, and what is meant by *walking in the light of it*. This is contrasted here with *walking in darkness* and with *trusting in the name of the Lord*—namely, *Christ's* righteousness, as I said before. To understand this, you must remember that the author's purpose is to show the different supports and foundations <note>original: "props and stayes"</note> for justification <term>justification: the act of being declared righteous before God</term> and comfort that a godly person has compared to a natural man <term>natural man: a person living according to human nature without the influence of the Holy Spirit</term>.

The support and comfort of a godly person's soul lies 

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>walking in Light.</header>

in the light of God's countenance. When he lacks this, he is in *darkness*, even though he has many outward comforts. The support, comfort, and prop of his faith for justification in that state—when he sees no righteousness in himself—is the name of the Lord *Jesus Christ our righteousness*.

<margin>Two things meant by *fire* and the *light of it*.</margin>
Conversely, therefore, by the *fire* and the *light of their fire* which wicked men are said to *walk in*, two things must be meant.

<margin>1.</margin>
*First*, their *own natural righteousness* which they have by nature and in the state of nature, along with its sparks and actions. They trust in this and neglect the *Name of the Lord*, as well as the *righteousness of justification and of the new creature* <note>the "new creature" refers to the spiritually reborn person</note>.

<margin>2.</margin>
*Secondly*, the *light of outward comforts* from created things <note>original: "the creatures"</note> which they enjoy in this world, and the sparkling pleasures they walk in and content themselves with. They neglect *communion with God*, are estranged *from the life of God*, and live *without him in the world*. Since the contrast strongly suggests it, both of these should be understood as their *fire* here. While all interpreters give the first explanation, I have added this second one to make the meaning complete.

<margin>1. Their own righteousness.</margin>
*First*, to understand how the *fire of their own kindling* means their own righteousness without *Christ*—such as the *Pharisees* had and *Paul* had before his conversion—we must consider that the prophet, speaking here to the Jews, alludes to the types <term>types: symbolic patterns or prefigurations in the Old Testament</term> of the *Old Law* with which they were familiar. In that law:

*First*, as you know, *fire* was what they used to offer their sacrifices. This represents—as in Mark 9:49 <note>original: "Mar.9.ult." referring to the last verse of the chapter: "For everyone will be salted with fire..."</note>—our inward, habitual grace and righteousness, by which we of—

*Secondly*, when they offered incense or a sacrifice acceptable to God, they were not to offer it with *common fire*, which is ordinary in the world in their chimneys and kitchens—fire kindled by themselves from sparks out of stones or from other things here below. Instead, it was to be *fire from heaven*, taken *from the Altar. So it was in Leviticus 9:24*, where the fire was kept continually burning; therefore, when a new altar was made, *fire came from heaven, 2 Chronicles 7:1*, and the *high priest was to take fire off the Altar* whenever he offered incense, *Leviticus 16:12.* 

Consequently, when *Nadab* and *Abihu* offered *common fire of their own kindling, Leviticus 10:1*, they were consumed for thinking they could please God with it. Now, all these things occurred as types <term>types: symbolic patterns or prophetic foreshadowings in the Old Testament</term> for them. Correspondingly, by the *fire of their own kindling* is meant the *common righteousness of civility* <note>original: "Civility"; here meaning social morality or outward decency without spiritual renewal</note> and natural devotion. This was found *by nature in some of the heathens, Romans 2:14*, existing even in their own "chimneys." 

The *Jews* also performed these things through the common help of nature, custom, and education—driven by the strength of natural principles, a conscience enlightened by the law, and improved self-love. All of this, because it comes only from nature and is not renewed by grace, is said to be of *their own kindling*. Yet, they thought to please God by it and relied on it as a sacrifice well-pleasing to him, just as *Nadab* and *Abihu* did. In reality, the righteousness they should have offered up to God should have been *that* which is from heaven: the *righteousness of Christ apprehended by faith*, and a *new work of Grace* acting *as fire from off that altar, Christ*, changing their...

Therefore, it differs as much from that *goodness of nature* you bring into the world—or which has been developed in you through education—as the fire and light of the *sun* in heaven differs from common fire, or the light of a glow-worm differs from that of the sun.

Now, because the *Jews* relied on such an *outward* conformity to the law—one of their own making—they are said in *Romans 10:3* to *establish their own righteousness*. They were ignorant of both forms of true righteousness: *Christ's righteousness*, which is the fire from heaven, and the *righteousness of regeneration* <note>original: "regeneratiō"; the spiritual process of being "born again" or internally renewed by God</note> to change their natures, which is the fire *from the Altar*. *Nicodemus* <note>A Pharisee and ruler of the Jews who visited Jesus by night; see John 3</note> was ignorant of both, and so he would have lain down in sorrow as his forefathers did if *Christ* had not changed him and caused him to be born again.

<margin>What the sparks represent.</margin>To this end, you may further consider that in human hearts—though they are *stony toward God*—there are (to use the language of the metaphor here) *some sparks of fire*. These sparks may be struck out of them by the Word <note>The Bible or preaching</note>, by education, by the enlightening of the conscience, and by appealing to a person's self-love. The sparks of this fire are those outward acts of righteousness that arise and spring from self-love and a natural conscience. These die out like sparks and *do not remain*, unlike the true righteousness of regeneration, which is said to endure (2 *Corinthians 9* and *John 2:16* <note>The author likely refers to the "abiding" nature of God's Word or righteousness; 1 John 2:17 is a more common reference for this concept</note>). ->*Application*<-
<note>original: "Uſe"; in Puritan preaching, this was the section of the sermon focused on the practical application of the doctrine to the listener's life.</note>

<margin>
*Application:* Examine what kind of fire we offer up to God.
</margin>
The first application is this: seeing as so many offer up nothing but common fire to God, it is vital that you examine whether the righteousness you think will please God is anything more than a fire of your own kindling.

<margin>
1. The origin of it.
</margin>
*First*, consider that righteousness which is kindled in your heart and shines in your life: *Where was it first enkindled?* Examine its origin. Was it kindled in you by fire from heaven? That is, by the *Holy Spirit* coming down upon you through God’s ordinances <note>The "means of grace," such as preaching, prayer, and the sacraments</note> like *fire*—burning up your lusts, melting your heart, dissolving the works of the devil, and kindling sparks of true love for God and zeal for His glory? These things are beyond the reach of human natural ability. 

Or is it nothing more than the sparks every person has? Every man has some sparks of integrity <note>original: "ingenuity"; in this context, it refers to natural nobility of character or fairness</note> and honesty toward others, and of sobriety and devotion to a Deity, buried in the ashes of our corrupt nature (for even the heathens had the *Law written in their hearts*, Romans 2:14). Because you live in the Church, where civility and religion are practiced, these sparks have been fanned into a bit of a blaze by a moral education, natural wisdom, and the promptings of an enlightened natural conscience. This leads to some fair dealing and a general care for serving God. Yet know that if there is no other principle, nor any more,

<meta>The text continues on the next page, likely explaining that these natural virtues are insufficient for salvation without the "more" of divine grace.</meta>

<summary>This page warns readers to examine whether their religious devotion stems from true divine transformation or merely from natural conscience and social upbringing. The author argues that true righteousness must be kindled by the Holy Spirit rather than human effort.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the self-examination regarding the "common fire" of natural morality versus the "heavenly fire" of true grace. It focuses on the "fuel" of one's religious life—the specific duties and motivations that drive a person's actions.</meta>

<header>Walking in Light.</header>

moreover, it is but *fire of your own kindling, and you will lie down in sorrow.* <note>A reference to Isaiah 50:11, warnining those who rely on their own light rather than God's.</note>

<margin>2. The fuel.</margin>
*Secondly,* examine *what duties specifically serve as the fuel for that fire within you:* in which duties is that righteousness—with which you think you please God—chiefly spent and exercised? Are they principally the duties of the Second Table <note>The Ten Commandments were traditionally divided into two "tables": the First Table (Commandments 1–4) concerns duties toward God, while the Second Table (Commandments 5–10) concerns duties toward fellow human beings, such as honesty and sobriety.</note>, consisting of honest dealings with men and sobriety? Perhaps you also bring along a stick or two from the First Table to this fire; that is, some duties of worship that you feel you must not omit for the sake of your reputation, such as attending God’s ordinances of public worship. 

If this is the only fuel, it suggests it is nothing but common fire. For if you look into the "chimneys" of the heathens <note>original: "heathen"; refers to non-Christians or those living by natural reason alone without divine revelation.</note>, you shall find most of this practiced. Because you place the core of your religion in these things, it suggests your fire is kindled only from those sparks found in nature. For those common sparks present in every person’s heart are primarily those concerning the Second Table. 

But if it were a fire from heaven, then while those moral duties would not be left undone, the greatest heat of your heart would be toward the duties of heaven—the worship of God, both public and private. Many people practice only as much righteousness as is necessary to live in the world with any comfort or reputation; being honest and sober is necessary for that, as is attending God's ordinances because the laws of the State require it. 

But when a person's zeal and fervor also struggle for and live upon duties that the world ignores—such as mourning for sin, working diligently on one's heart in private between God and one's own soul, and feeding upon heavenly things and thoughts—it is a fire such as the

<meta>This page continues the comparison between "common fire" (natural morality) and "heavenly fire" (true divine grace). <header>A Child of Darkness</header>

the world quenches, it is a sign it is more than common fire. <note>The author is concluding a point from the previous page: if your spiritual fire is not put out by worldly distractions or opposition, it is a sign of true grace.</note>

<margin>3. By warming only the outward person.</margin>
*Thirdly*, in these duties, common fire warms only the outward person, just like the physical fire that you feel daily; it does not heat you within. Similarly, common righteousness contents itself with *bodily exercise* <note>External religious rituals or physical behaviors performed without inner spiritual devotion.</note>, a formal performance of duties, both public and private. But fire from heaven heats the inside first; it heats the heart within. As it was said regarding the hearing of the **Word** <note>The preaching of the Bible.</note>, "*Did not our hearts burn within us?*" <note>Luke 24:32.</note> So it heats the heart in prayer and makes a person *fervent in spirit, serving the Lord.* <note>Romans 12:11.</note>

<margin>4. What incentives inflame it.</margin>
*Fourthly*, examine what bellows cherish and keep alive that fire of righteousness that is in you and makes it flame—that is, what motives set you to work to do what you do. If worldly ends make you abstain from sin and be just in your dealings—such as your reputation with the world, fear of disgrace, the accusations of conscience alone, the fear of hell, or the hope of heaven—this is only common fire. But if love for God, the consideration of His mercies, His eternal love, the love of *Christ*, and zeal for His glory are the bellows, then the fire is heavenly. But if your heart is not stirred when you are moved by such things as these, it is only *common fire*.

<margin>Application 2.</margin>
The second **Application** <note>original: "Use"; a standard term in Puritan sermons for the practical application of a theological doctrine.</note> is to take heed of walking in the light of such fire—that is, relying on it for salvation and contenting yourselves with it, as most in the world do, and as the *Jews* here did; for you will lie down in sorrow if you do.

But you will say, "We do not trust in this, our own righteousness, for we profess *Christ* and believe in Him, which, added to this, is enough."

<summary>The author explains that true spiritual life is internal and motivated by love for God, whereas "common" morality is merely external and driven by fear or reputation. He warns readers not to rely on their own moral efforts for salvation, as this leads to spiritual sorrow.</summary>
<keywords>common fire, heavenly fire, hypocrisy, motives, fervent in spirit, bodily exercise, outward vs inward, righteousness, Isaiah 50:11</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to distinguish between "common fire" (natural, human-driven morality) and "heavenly fire" (true spiritual grace). It focuses on the necessity of abandoning one's own self-righteousness to fully rely on Christ's righteousness and sanctification.</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<header>Walking in Light.</header>
<sig>Ii2</sig>

I **answer**. Although you profess to follow **Christ**, unless you have had a light revealed to you that all the righteousness you possess by nature (or have improved through your own effort) is a false righteousness, you are still relying on your own merits and not relying wholly on **Christ**. So, in *Philippians 3*, **Paul** first saw all his previous accomplishments as dross and dung <note>original: "drosse and dung"; Paul uses these terms in the Bible to describe his former religious achievements as worthless trash compared to knowing Christ.</note>; he counted it as a total loss so that he might win **Christ**. This implies he could not have **Christ** otherwise. Though people seem to adopt **Christ’s** Title—just as many will obtain the King's Title for a living <note>In this context, a "living" refers to a "benefice," a fixed income-earning position or property within the church granted by legal authority.</note> to make everything secure—they still keep, hold onto, and plead their own case based on their own merits. But you must give that up first and rely wholly on **Christ**, or He will not save you.

2. He who does not daily—above all things—directly and immediately aim at and seek out **Christ’s** righteousness, making it the chief focus of his thoughts, prayers, and business, and is restless without it, is still resting in his own. For once **Paul** had given up his claim to his own merit, he primarily strove for this: *to be found in Christ (Philippians 3).*

**Thirdly**, you must still seek from **Christ** a new righteousness of sanctification <note>The process of being made holy and being set apart for God's service through the Holy Spirit.</note> as well; for you will see that the common righteousness of nature and education will not please Him. **Christ** must be made *sanctification* to you (1 Corinthians 1:30) as well as *righteousness*. Thus, although **Nicodemus** was a civil <note>meaning "upright" or "socially respectable" according to human standards.</note> man before, when he came to **Christ**, his old civility would not suffice without being born again and becoming a new creature. Therefore, you must not think to provide a substitute or an addition to **Christ** with fire of your own kindling; you must receive everything from the **Altar**. Your moral virtues must be turned into graces by having a new purpose put into them, carrying your 

<meta>catchword: hearts</meta>

<summary>The author argues that true faith requires completely abandoning reliance on natural morality or "civil" behavior, instead seeking both justification and holiness (sanctification) through Christ alone. He uses the biblical examples of Paul and Nicodemus to show that human effort and education are insufficient for salvation.</summary>
<keywords>righteousness, sanctification, Paul, Philippians 3, Nicodemus, 1 Corinthians 1:30, legalism vs grace, justification, spiritual rebirth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how some people rely on their own "fire" (self-created righteousness or worldly comforts) instead of the light of Christ. It begins by concluding a point about the lack of true devotion to God.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>A Child of Darkness</header>

---

hearts in them toward God.

<margin>2. By fire is meant external comforts.</margin>
The other interpretation I add is that the *fire of external comforts* is also meant. While people enjoy these, they continue along happily, neglecting God, Christ, and communion with Him. But the soul of a believer who lacks this communion with God is in darkness; until they enjoy God again, they can take comfort in nothing. Thus, *Ecclesiastes 7:6: The laughter of the wicked is compared to the crackling of thorns.* <note>Burning thorns make a loud noise and bright flame but are consumed quickly, providing no lasting warmth.</note>

<margin>Why fire represents comfort.</margin>
*Fire is a comforting creation, possessing both heat and light, which help against both cold and darkness—two of the greatest evils to our senses.*

Heat is comforting. Therefore, *Isaiah 44:16* says, *'He warms himself,' (says the Prophet) 'and cries, Aha!'*

*Light* is also comforting. For *Solomon* says, *'It is a pleasant thing to behold the sun.'* <note>Ecclesiastes 11:7</note> Therefore, *fire* here represents external *comforts*.

<margin>Why external comfort is compared to a fire of their own kindling—to an earthly fire.</margin>
But what kind of fire is this? It is only kitchen fire—*hearth fire* <note>original: "Ignis focalis"; a Latin term for a domestic or fireplace fire, as opposed to celestial or elemental fire.</note>—as the *philosopher* <note>Likely referring to Aristotle or a scholastic philosopher discussing the nature of elements.</note> calls it. For the text says, *'It is a fire of their own kindling.'* It is not that *purest element of fire from above*. God is said to be *light* and *fire*, whom the saints enjoy; they are refreshed with His *light, and in His light, they see light*.

And the comparison holds strong in this way as well:

For a kindled fire has two common things that go into making it; both of these together are called *fire*.

*First, fuel:* such as wood or coals, etc.

*Second, that element that consumes these.*

Correspondingly, those worldly <note>original: "carnall"; referring here to physical or sensory pleasures rather than spiritual ones.</note> pleasures and delights which wicked people enjoy and rest in, 

<meta>catchword: two</meta>

<summary>This page explores the metaphor of "fire" as a symbol for worldly comforts that provide temporary heat and light but distract from a true relationship with God. The author distinguishes between man-made "kitchen fire" and the divine light of God.</summary>
<keywords>comforts, light, fire, Isaiah, Solomon, Ecclesiastes, worldly pleasure, spiritual darkness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between worldly comforts and a "fire of one's own kindling," focusing on why earthly desires are like a domestic fire.</meta>

# Walking in Light.

*Two things also happen at the same time:*

*First,* the *object*, which acts as the *fuel*. These are *earthly things, belonging to this world.*

<margin>The comparison holds in six ways.</margin>
*Secondly,* their fiery hot and burning *desires* <note>original: "lusts"; in this context, it refers to any intense worldly cravings or passions</note>, which prey and live upon this fuel. Both of these together create the fire spoken of here. In both cases, this resemblance holds true in many ways.

<margin>
1. 1794
March 14
20 & 21
March 25—p. 157
L. 27—161 & 164
169 & 170 : 171
March 26—194
L. 18 : 200
March 25
220 & 221
222
</margin>
*First,* the fuel for these fires of their desires and comforts is *lowly* <note>original: "base"</note>; they are only things found here below. What is the fuel of your kitchen fires? Things dug out of the earth—dung, wood, and coal. Similarly, *earthly things are fuel to their desires.* Their desires are therefore called *earthly members* (Colossians 3:5), because all their comforts consist of, and all their desires are for, *earthly things*—such as beautiful wives, children, houses, food, and drink. *Their god is their stomach, and they think only of earthly things* (Philippians 3:19).

*Secondly,* when this fuel is taken away, the fire goes out. In the same way, people's hearts die when outward things are taken from them. When *Nabal* thought *David* might still come and take his goods, his heart *died within him* <note>1 Samuel 25:37. Nabal's heart "became as a stone" when he realized the danger he was in.</note>. For such people live through created things, and without them, they die.

<margin>3.</margin>
*Thirdly,* just as fire is a *consuming thing* (Hebrews 12:29 <note>original: "ult."; refers to the final verse of the chapter</note>), leaving nothing but *ashes*, so are *human desires* (James 4:4). People ask for things only to *waste them on their own desires.* No lasting value comes from the pleasures they have, and no spiritual strength is gained from them. They do everything for themselves, and when they die, everything dies with them.

<margin>4.</margin>
*Fourthly,* fire is a *devouring* thing; even a whole world would not satisfy it if it were left to burn on. One day, this whole world you see will be burned up by fire, like a witch punished for enticing men. Even such...

<summary>This page compares worldly comforts and desires to an earthly fire, noting that both rely on "base" fuel, die out when that fuel is removed, and ultimately consume everything without producing anything lasting.</summary>
<keywords>worldly comfort, fuel, earthly things, Nabal, Philippians 3:19, Colossians 3:5, consuming fire, desires</keywords>

men's desires for pleasures are never satisfied. The more fuel is added, the more you must add. They expand their desires like the fire of hell (Habakkuk 2:5).

Fifthly, the pleasures that arise when this fuel meets their desires <note>original: "lusts"; in this context, it refers to any intense worldly cravings or passions</note> are like sparks. Job calls sparks the "sons of fire" (Job 5:7), as they are produced by fire acting on fuel. Similarly, pleasures are the "offspring" of your desires when they meet their object. They do not live long; they are like sparks that die as soon as they are born. As Colossians 2:22 says, they "perish in the using," and like the crackling of thorns in a fire (Ecclesiastes 7:6), they quickly burn out.

Sixthly, smoke accompanies such fires because the fuel consists of "muddy" <note>original: "muddy"; meaning impure, worldly, or earthly rather than spiritual</note> things. Likewise, much sorrow accompanies their comforts (Proverbs 14:13), and they eventually go out and end in smoke—as the text says, they "lie down in sorrow."

When you put these together—both that "strange fire" <note>Leviticus 10:1; here used to describe a self-made religious righteousness that does not come from God</note> of their own righteousness (which comes from an unchanged human nature) and the "kitchen fire" <note>original: "kitching fire"; a metaphor for domestic, worldly comforts used for physical sustenance rather than spiritual warmth</note> of outward comforts—these are the two main obstacles that keep wicked people from Christ and from being justified through him.

The Covenant of Grace <note>The theological concept that salvation is a gift from God through faith, rather than earned by works</note> contains two main promises:

First, that God himself (who is the "God of comfort") will be an abundant reward (Genesis 17:1-2). Through faith, we accept him as such and are separated from all other comforts when compared to him. And

Secondly, that Jesus Christ, His Son, is made "The LORD our Righteousness" (Jeremiah 23:6). Therefore, before we can accept him as such, we must be emptied of all our own natural righteousness, so that God and Christ might be everything to us. And therefore, as the

<summary>The author concludes a six-part comparison, arguing that worldly pleasures are as fleeting as sparks and as unsatisfying as smoke. He identifies self-righteousness and worldly comfort as the two primary barriers preventing people from accepting the Covenant of Grace through Christ.</summary>
<keywords>desire, worldly pleasure, sparks, smoke, self-righteousness, Covenant of Grace, justification, Habakkuk 2:5, Jeremiah 23:6</keywords>

<margin>Joseph</margin> A poor, simple-minded <note>original: "half witted"</note> man named Joseph, whose employment was to go on errands and carry parcels, was passing through the streets of London one day when he heard Psalm-singing in a place of worship. He went into it, having a large parcel of yarn hanging over his shoulders. It was Dr. Calamy’s <note>Edmund Calamy the Elder (1600–1666), a prominent leader of the Presbyterian movement in London</note> meeting house, St. Mary Aldermanbury. A very well-dressed audience surrounded the Doctor as he read the text from 1 Timothy 1:15: "This is a faithful saying, and worthy of all

<insert>
I have read through this book, titled **A Child of Light**, etc., in which I find nothing contrary to sound faith or good morals. <note>original: "PErlegi hunc librum, cui titulus A Childe of Light, &c. in quo nihil reperio sanæ fidei aut bonis moribus contrarium." This is a formal Imprimatur, or license to print, required by law at the time.</note>

->**THOMAS WEEKES**<-
->**Domestic Chaplain to the Reverend Father, the Bishop of London**<- <note>original: "R. P. Ep°. Lond. Cap. Domest." Thomas Weekes was the official who reviewed books for the Bishop of London to ensure they were not heretical.</note>
</insert>

acceptance, that Christ Jesus came into the world to save sinners, of whom I am chief." From this he preached, in the clearest and simplest manner, the ancient Apostolic Gospel and the contents of this faithful saying: namely, that there is eternal salvation for the vilest sinners, solely through the worthiness of Jesus Christ, the God who made all things. "Not many rich, nor many noble" <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 1:26</note> are now called by this doctrine (says the Apostle); but "God has chosen the weak things of this world to confound the things that are mighty." While the elegant assembly listlessly heard this doctrine—or, if they were struck by anything at all, it was only by some brilliant expression or well-turned period <term>period: a long, complex, and grammatically polished sentence</term> that dropped from the Doctor;—
